set with gems, etc.; but this account is certainly false. The fleet left the river of

Tabasco on Holy Monday, and reached the port of Ulúa on Thursday. The mountains

of Tochtlan and of Mictlan, from which the fleet could most readily be observed, are

no less than 300 miles distant from the capital, nor is the latter distant from the port

of Ulúa by less than 220: so that even if the fleet had been seen on the very day on

which it sailed from Tabasco, it was impossible for the ambassadors to arrive at Ulúa

on Thursday. Besides, no record of such an event is to be found in any other author;

indeed from the account of Bernal Díaz it is seen that all this is false, and that the

Mexicans had already perceived the error into which the first fleet that appeared

there had led them.

(g) Bernal Díaz wrote Tendile instead of Teuhtlile, and Pitalpitoque instead of

Cuitlalpitoc. Herrera calls him Pitalpitoe, and Solís and Robertson, who wished to

emend them, Pilpatoe.

(h) Solís reproaches Bernal Díaz and Herrera with having affirmed, as he believed,

that Mass was celebrated at Chalchiuhcuecan on Good Friday. The author of the

preface which is in the 1730 edition of Herrera employs a great abundance of inop‐

portune and tedious erudition to justify the alleged celebration of the Mass on that

holy day; but with the good leave of this author and of Solís, they did not understand

Bernal Díaz's text well. He says only, in ch. 38, that on Good Friday they landed the

horses and the artillery, and we made, he adds, an altar, on which Mass was soon said. He

does not say that on that same day they made the altar; indeed in the same chapter

of his history he affirms in quite clear words that it was made on Sunday, after the

governor Teuhtlile had come to pay reverence to Cortés. [p. 17] seeking to win their goodwill by his attentions. As soon as they rose from the table, he led them aside to set forth his claims. He told Bk. VIII. them that he was a subject of Don Carlos of Austria, the greatest king of the East, whose goodness, greatness, and power he extolled in the most magnificent terms, and he added that this great monarch, having knowledge of that land and of the lord who reigned in it, had sent him to pay him a visit in his name, and to communicate to him by word of mouth some matters of great moment, and that he would therefore be glad to know where it would please him to hear the embassy. “Scarcely,” replied Teuhtlile, “have you arrived in this country, and you wish at once to see our king? I have listened with pleasure to what you have told me concerning the greatness and the goodness of your sovereign, but know that ours is no less good, nor a lesser king; indeed I marvel that there can be in the world another more powerful than he; but since you affirm it, I shall make it known to my sovereign, of whose goodness I trust that he will not only take pleasure in hearing such news of that great prince, but will also honor his ambassador. Accept in the meantime this gift, which in his name I present to you,” and, taking from a petlacalli, or chest woven of cane, several excellent pieces of gold work, he presented them to him, together with various curious pieces of featherwork, ten loads of fine cotton garments, and a great quantity of provisions. (i)

Cortés accepted the gift with a singular show of gratitude, and returned it with things of little value, but prized by them either because they were entirely new in that country or for their apparent brilliance. Teuhtlile had brought with him various painters, so that, dividing among themselves the different

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                       C                      objects

(i) Solís and Robertson make Teuhtlile commander of the armed forces, and deprive him of the political government of that coast, whereas from Bernal Díaz, Gómara, and other early historians we know that he was governor of Cuetlachtlan. Those authors also say that Teuhtlile at first opposed Cortés's plan of going to the court; but it is clear to us from the testimony of the best early historians that that governor did not oppose it until he had a positive order from his king.

[p. 18]

Bk. VIII.

objects of which the army was composed, they might copy it all in a short time, and so give the king the pleasure of seeing with his own eyes what they had to tell him by word of mouth. Cortés, perceiving their intention, ordered, to furnish the painters with a new subject capable of making a stronger impression on the king's mind, that his cavalry should gallop along the beach and perform some military evolutions, and that all the artillery should be fired at once: which was observed with such amazement as may be imagined by the two governors and by their numerous retinue, which, if we believe Gómara, was not less than four thousand men. Among the arms of the Spaniards Teuhtlile noticed a gilded helmet, which, because it was very like another that one of the principal idols of Mexico had, he asked of Cortés in order to show it to the king; and he granted it on the condition of having it back full of gold dust, under the pretext that he wished to see whether the gold that was taken from the mines of Mexico was like that of his own country. (k)

As soon as the paintings were finished, Teuhtlile took affectionate leave of Cortés, offering to return to him within a few days with the answer of his sovereign; and, leaving Cuitlalpitoc in his place, so that he might provide the Spaniards with everything they needed, he went to Cuetlachtlan, the place of his ordinary residence; whence he carried in person to the court the report, the paintings, and the gift of the Spanish general, as Bernal Díaz and Torquemada affirm, or else sent it all, as Solís conjectures, by the posts, which were always ready on the main roads to make a journey.

Everyone can easily imagine the great disquiet and perplexity in which Moctezuma found himself with the news of

that

(k) Some historians say that Cortés, to ask for the helmet full of gold, availed himself of the pretext of a certain heart ailment from which he and his companions suffered, which he said could not be cured with any other remedy than that precious metal; but this matters little to the substance of the fact. [p. 19] that fleet, and with a more exact report of the character of those

Bk. VIII. strangers, of the fire of their horses, and of the destructive violence of their arms. As one who was so superstitious, he had his gods con‐ §Moctezuma’s

7.

disquisulted on their claim, and was answered, as they say, that he should et. First embassy, and never receive that new people at his court. Whether he really had this gift  sent by him to

Cortés. oracle, as several authors persuaded themselves, from the Devil, who was at work to keep all the passes closed to the Gospel, or, as we believe, from the priests, for their own interest or for that of the whole nation, Moctezuma resolved from then on not to admit the Spaniards; but, to do so in a good manner and in keeping with his own character, he sent them an embassy with a gift truly worthy of his royal magnificence. The ambassador was a great personage of the court, very like the Spanish general both in stature and in features, as an eyewitness testifies. (*) Hardly had seven days passed since Teuhtlile took leave when he returned, accompanying the ambassador and bringing with him more than a hundred porters, who carried the gift. (l) As soon as the ambassador was in the presence of Cortés, he touched the ground with his hand and then brought it to his mouth, according to the custom of those nations, and incensed (m) the general and the other officers,

C2                          who

(*) Bernal Díaz del Castillo.

(l) Bernal Díaz calls this ambassador Quintalbor; but such a name is not, and cannot be, Mexican. Robertson says that the same officers who had until then treated with Cortés were charged with carrying him the king's reply, without making any mention of the ambassador; but Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, as well as other Spanish and Indian historians, affirm what we say. Solís, considering the short interval of seven days, and the distance of seventy leagues between that port and the capital, would not be persuaded that the ambassador came then; but, since he had said a little earlier that the Mexican posts were more diligent than the European, it is no wonder that in one day, or little more, they carried the news of the fleet to the court, and that in four or five days the ambassador went in a litter on the shoulders of the same posts, as was their custom. Since the fact is not improbable, one must believe Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness and truthful.

(m) The incensing done to the Spaniards, although it was nothing more than a [p. 20] who were near him, and saluted them respectfully; and, seating himBk. VIII.

self on a chair which Cortés offered him, he delivered his harangue,

which came down to congratulating that general, in the name of his

king, on his happy arrival in that country, to signify the pleasure

which His Majesty had had in learning that men so valiant had come

into his kingdom, and in hearing the news they brought him of so

great a monarch, and to show him how agreeable his gift had been;

wherefore, as a mark of his royal satisfaction, he sent him that

present. This said, he had fine palm mats and cotton cloths spread

upon the ground, upon which the whole gift was laid with good order

and symmetry. It consisted of many works of gold and silver, still

more valuable for their marvelous workmanship than for their pre‐

cious material, among which some had gems set in them and others

were figures of lions, tigers, monkeys, and other animals; of thirty

loads, or bales, of very fine cotton cloths of various colors, and partly

woven with most beautiful feathers; of several excellent works of

featherwork adorned with many little figures of gold; and of the hel‐

met full of gold dust, as Cortés had wished it, which was worth fif‐

teen hundred sequins; but the most valuable of all were two great

plates made in the form of wheels, one of gold and the other of silver.

That of gold, representing, according to what we have said elsewhere,

the Mexican century, had the image of the sun carved in the middle,

and around it various figures in bas-relief. Its circumference was thir‐

ty Toledan palms, and its value ten thousand sequins. (n) That of sil‐

ver, on which the Mexican year was figured, was still larger, with a

moon in the middle, likewise surrounded by figures in bas-relief. The

Spaniards were

no

purely civil act of respect, and the name of Teteuctin (Lords or Knights) by which they

were called, somewhat like that of Teteo (Gods,) made them believe that they were es‐

teemed gods by the Mexicans.

(n) There is some variance among the authors as to the value of the plates; but I

give more credence to Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, who knew it well, as one who

must have had his share in Moctezuma's gift. [p. 21] no less amazed than pleased at so great a wealth. “This gift,” added the ambassador, speaking to Cortés, “my sovereign sends for you and Bk. VIII. for your companions, since for your king he will send you shortly certain gems of inestimable value. In the meantime you may stay as long as you please on this beach to rest from the fatigues of so long a voyage, and to provide yourselves with whatever you need to return to your country. If you wish anything else of this land for your sovereign, it will be given you at once; but as regards your claim to go to the court, I am charged to dissuade you from so difficult and dangerous a journey, since you would have to travel through uninhabitable deserts and through enemy countries.” Cortés received the gift with the greatest expressions of his gratitude toward the royal beneficence, and responded as he could; but, without abandoning his request, he begged the ambassador to represent to the king the dangers and hardships they had suffered in so long a voyage, and the displeasure his sovereign would feel at seeing himself disappointed in his hope: that for the rest the Spaniards were of such a condition that neither hardships nor perils were capable of diverting them from their undertakings. The ambassador offered to make this known to the king, and courteously took leave together with Teuhtlile, Cuitlalpitoc remaining with a great number of people in a hamlet which they had formed of huts, not far from the camp of the Spaniards.

Cortés saw well, amid all the prosperity he had so far had, that he could not remain in that place; for besides the discomfort of the heat, and the nuisance of the mosquitoes, which abound too much along all that beach, he feared some damage to his ships from the violence of the north wind, to which that poor harbor is very much exposed: so he dispatched two ships under the command of Captain Montejo, that, proceeding along the coast toward the Pánuco, they might look for another, safer port. They returned within a few

days [p. 22] days with the news that they had found, thirty-six miles from Ulúa,

Bk. VIII.

an adequate port near a city built on a strong site.

§ 8.   Meanwhile Teuhtlile returned to the camp of the Spaniards, and, Moctezuma’s gift for the Catholic King. drawing Cortés aside with the interpreters, told him that his lord

Moctezuma had been pleased with the new gift sent him by Cortés:

that what he was then sending him was destined for the great King of

Spain: that he wished him the utmost happiness; but, for the rest,

that he should send him no more messages, nor speak any more of

going to the court. The gift for the Catholic King consisted of several

works of gold, worth fifteen hundred sequins, of ten bales of most cu‐

rious featherwork goods, and of four gems so prized by the Mexicans

that, according to what Teuhtlile himself affirmed, each was worth

four loads of gold. That ill-advised king thought by his liberality to

oblige the Spaniards to abandon that country, not perceiving that the

love of gold is a fire which is the more inflamed the more copious the

fuel that is offered to it. Cortés was greatly vexed at the king's refusal;

but he was not on that account turned from his purpose, since the

natural constancy of his mind was spurred on still further by the al‐

lurement of wealth.

Before taking his leave, Teuhtlile observed that the Spaniards, on

hearing the strokes of the bell at the Ave Maria, knelt down before a

holy cross, and, full of wonder, asked why they adored that piece of

wood. Father Olmedo thereupon took occasion to expound to him

the principal articles of the Christian religion, and to reproach him

for the abominable worship of idols and the inhumanity of their sac‐

rifices; but such a discourse was altogether fruitless, since for those

peoples the time of sanctification had not yet come.

The next day the Spaniards found themselves so abandoned by the

Mexicans that not even one was to be seen along all that beach: an ef‐

fect, beyond doubt, of the order already given by the king to with‐

draw from the camp of those strangers both the people assigned to

their service and the provisions, if they persisted

in [p. 23] in their rash resolution. Such a piece of news caused great dismay among the Spaniards, because at every moment they feared that the Bk. VIII. whole power of that vast empire would fall upon their wretched camp: so Cortés had their provisions secured on the ships, and had his troops put under arms for defense. There is no doubt that both on this occasion and on many others that we shall recall in the History, Moctezuma could easily have destroyed utterly those few strangers who were to do him so much harm; but God preserved them, that they might be instruments of his justice, making use of their arms to punish the superstition, the cruelty, and the other crimes with which those nations had provoked his wrath. We do not however wish to justify the intention and the conduct of the Conquerors; but neither can we fail to recognize in the course of the conquest, in spite of incredulity, the hand of God, who was preparing that empire for its ruin, and was availing himself of the very misdeeds of men for the lofty designs of his Providence.

§ 9.

On this very day of such dismay for the Spaniards, they also had a Embassy of the lord token of Divine Providence. Two soldiers who were standing guard of Cempoala, and its

consequences. outside the camp saw coming toward them five men somewhat different from the Mexicans in their dress and in their ornaments, who, being led to the Spanish general, said in Mexican (for there was no one there who understood their own language) that they were of the Totonac nation, and sent by the lord of Cempoala, a city twenty-four miles distant from that place, to salute them, to learn who they were and whence they came, and to beg them to betake themselves to that city, where they would be well received, adding that they had not come sooner to the camp for fear of the Mexicans. The lord of Cempoala was one of those feudatories who lived restive under the yoke of the Mexicans; aware of the victory the Spaniards had gained in Tabasco and of their arrival at that port, this seemed to him the finest occasion to shake off the yoke with the help of such valiant men. Cortés

as [p. 24] as one who desired nothing more than such alliances to increase his Bk. VIII.

forces, after being sufficiently informed of the state and condition of

the Totonacs, and of the wrongs they suffered from the overbearing

power of the Mexicans, replied, thanking the Cempoalan for his

courtesy, and promising to go without delay to find him.

Indeed he at once announced his departure for Cempoala; but first

it was necessary for him to overcome the obstacles put in his way by

his own soldiers. Some partisans of the governor of Cuba, already

weary of the hardships they had endured, frightened by the perils

they foresaw, and longing for rest and for the comfort of their houses,

strongly urged the general to return to Cuba, exaggerating the scarci‐

ty of provisions and the rashness of so great an undertaking as that of

opposing so small a number of soldiers to the great power of the king

of Mexico, especially after having lost in those sands thirty-five men,

partly by the wounds received in the battle of Tabasco and partly on

account of the unwholesome air of that beach. Cortés, now with

gifts, now with promises, now with a little rigor opportunely applied,

now with other means devised by his rare intelligence, managed their

minds so well that he not only quieted the malcontents and induced

them to remain willingly in that rich country; but, advancing also in

his negotiations, he obtained that the army, in the name of the king,

and without any dependence on the governor of Cuba, should con‐

firm him in the supreme command, both political and military, and

that for the expenses already incurred by him on the expedition, and

to be incurred in the future, there should be assigned to him from

then on the fifth part of the gold that should be acquired, after de‐

ducting first what belonged to their king. He also had created the

magistrates and all the offices necessary for a colony, which he wished

to found on that coast.

Having therefore overcome these difficulties, and taken the mea‐

sures suited to the execution of his vast projects, he set out on the

march with his troops. His intention was not merely to refresh his

men, much distressed on that sandy

beach [p. 25] beach, and to seek allies, but also to choose a good site for the found‐

Bk. VIII. ing of the colony, since Cempoala lay on the road to Chiahuitztla, (*) in whose district was the new port already found by Captain Montejo. The army, with a part of the artillery, marched toward Cempoala in good order and prepared to defend itself should it be attacked, whether by the Totonacs, of whose good faith they were not yet sure, or by the Mexicans, whom they supposed offended by their resolution: precautions deemed superfluous by no good general, which were never neglected by Cortés even in the time of his greatest prosperity, being always useful for maintaining military discipline, and for the most part necessary to one's own safety. The ships proceeded along the coast to the port of Chiahuitztla.

Three miles before arriving at Cempoala twenty distinguished Cempoalans came to meet the army, and presented to Cortés a refreshment of pineapples and other fruits of the country, saluted him in the name of their lord, and begged his pardon that he had not come himself in person because he was prevented. They entered the city drawn up in battle order, fearing some treachery on the part of the inhabitants. A light horseman, having advanced as far as the principal square, and having seen a bastion of the palace of that lord, which, because it was freshly whitewashed and well polished, shone in the sun, thought it silver, and returned at full gallop to give so fine a piece of news to the general. Such deceptions are too frequent in those whose minds are filled by some strong passion. The Spaniards walked through the streets no less delighted than amazed at seeing that city, the largest they had seen until then in the New World, such a crowd of people, and such beautiful orchards and gardens. Some, because of its greatness

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                       D                        called

(*) Solís and Robertson give Chiahuitztla the name of Quiabislan, which is not, and cannot be, Mexican. [p. 26] called it Seville, and others Villaviciosa for its pleasantness. (o) Bk. VIII.

When they reached the great temple, the lord of that state came

to receive them at the door of the court; and although he was unable

to move on account of his immoderate fatness, he was a shrewd man

and of good understanding. After saluting them according to the cus‐

tom of the country, and incensing the general, he took leave, promis‐

ing to return as soon as they should have rested from the fatigues of

the journey. He lodged the whole Spanish troop in certain large and

handsome buildings that were within the enclosure of the temple,

whether built expressly for the lodging of strangers, like the one in

the great temple of Mexico, or intended as dwellings for the ministers

of the idols. There they were well treated, and supplied with whatever

they needed at the expense of that lord, who returned to them after

dinner in a portable chair, or litter, accompanied by a great number of

nobles. In the secret conference which he had with Cortés, this gen‐

eral extolled, by means of his interpreters, the greatness and power of

his sovereign, by whom he had been sent to that country and charged

with several commissions of the highest moment, and among others

with that of giving aid to oppressed innocence. “If, however,” he

added, “I can serve you in any way with my person and with my

troops, tell me so, for I will do it gladly.” On hearing these offers, very

skillfully introduced into the conversation, the lord of Cempoala

heaved a great sigh, which was followed by bitter complaints of the

misfortune of his nation. He told him that the Totonacs, having been

from time immemorial free, and ruled by lords of their own nation,

had found themselves for

a

(o) There can be no doubt of the ancient greatness of Cempoala, given the testi‐

mony of the authors who saw it, and the extent of its ruins; but no account can be

taken of the reckoning made by Torquemada, since in bk. 3, ch. 5, he numbers from

twenty-five to thirty thousand inhabitants, in another place fifty thousand, and in the

index of the first volume a hundred and fifty thousand. There happened to Cempoala

the same as to other cities of the New World, namely, that with the sicknesses and

the hardships of the sixteenth century it went on dwindling until it was entirely

depopulated. [p. 27] a few years past overwhelmed by the heavy yoke of the Mexicans; that the Mexicans, on the contrary, from humble beginnings had Bk. VIII. risen to so great a grandeur through their close and constant alliance with the kings of Acolhuacan and of Tlacopan, who had made themselves masters of all that land: that their power was unmeasured, and their tyranny proportioned to their might: that the king of Mexico seized the gold of his subjects, and the tax collectors of the tributes demanded of the tributaries their daughters to violate them, and their sons to sacrifice them, besides other unheard-of vexations. Cortés showed himself greatly moved to compassion at his misfortunes, and offered to help him in everything, leaving for another occasion the discussion of the manner of doing so, because just then he was eager to go to Chiahuitztla to see the state of his ships. During this visit the lord of Cempoala made him a gift of gold work, which amounted, according to what some historians say, to about a thousand sequins.

The next day four hundred porters presented themselves to Cortés, sent him by that lord for the transport of the baggage, and then he learned from Doña Marina the custom that prevailed among those nations of supplying spontaneously, and without any payment, porters to every person of distinction who passed through their cities.                                                                    § 10.

From Cempoala the Spaniards proceeded to Chiahuitztla, a small Imprisonment in city situated on a steep and craggy mountain, little more than twelve Chiahuitztla      of five

royal ministers. miles from Cempoala toward the north, and three from the new port. Here Cortés had another conference with the lord of that city and with the lord of Cempoala, who for this reason had had himself carried there. At the very time when they were discussing the matter of liberty, there arrived with a great retinue five Mexican nobles, collectors of the royal tributes, showing themselves exceedingly indignant against the Totonacs because they had dared to receive those strangers without awaiting the royal consent, and demanding of them twenty human victims to be sacrificed to their gods for the expiation of so great a crime. The whole city was troubled at such news, and especially the two lords, who felt themselves more

D2                     guilty [p. 28] guilty. Cortés, having learned from Doña Marina the cause of their Bk. VIII.

consternation, found an extraordinary expedient to extricate them

from that intrigue. He suggested to those two lords the bold plan of

seizing the tax collectors and putting them in prison; and although at

first they refused to do so, thinking it an attempt too rash and dan‐

gerous, they finally yielded to his entreaties. Those five nobles were

therefore shut up in their cages, they who had entered that city with

such pride, and with such contempt for the Spaniards that they had

not even deigned to look at them as they passed before them.

No sooner had the Totonacs taken this first step than, gaining

courage from it, they went so far as to want to sacrifice them that

same night; but they were dissuaded by Cortés, who, having by that

capture won the love and respect of the Totonacs, wished also to gain

the goodwill of the Mexicans by freeing the prisoners. His artful and

double conduct certainly shows his talent; but it can be praised only

by those courtiers who know no other politics than the art of deceiv‐

ing men, and who, setting honesty at naught, seek only advantage in

their actions. Cortés therefore gave orders to his guards to take two

of the Mexicans out of the cages during the night, and to bring them

secretly into his presence, without the Totonacs being able to notice

it. So it was done, and the Mexicans were so obliged to the Spanish

general that they thanked him a thousand times, and advised him not

to trust the barbarous and perfidious Totonacs. Cortés charged them

to set before their sovereign his great displeasure at the attempt of

those mountaineers against his ministers; but that as he had set those

two at liberty, so he would do with the other three. They departed

immediately for the court, conducted by some Spaniards in a boat as

far as the borders of that province, and the next day Cortés pretend‐

ed to be angry with the guards, through whose carelessness those

prisoners had escaped; and so that the same might not happen with

the others, he said that he wished to secure them in

another [p. 29] another, closer prison, and to make this believed, he had them taken in chains onto the ships; but a little later he set them secretly at liber‐ Bk. VIII. ty, like the first.

He at once had word spread through all the mountains of the § 11. Totonacs that they were now free of the tribute which they paid to Confederation of the

Totonacs with the the king of Mexico, and that if by chance there were any other collec‐ Spaniards. tors of tribute, they should let him know at once so that he might seize them. At the sound of this news the sweet hope of liberty awoke throughout the whole nation, and several other lords began to come to Chiahuitztla, no less to thank their supposed liberator than to deliberate on the means of confirming their liberty. Some, who had not yet driven the fear of the Mexicans from their minds, held that pardon should be asked of the king for the attempt committed against his ministers; but through the suggestions of Cortés and of the lords of Cempoala and of Chiahuitztla the opposite view prevailed, to withdraw from the tyrannical domination of the Mexicans with the help of those brave strangers, offering to put a formidable army under the command of the Spanish general.

After Cortés had assured himself sufficiently of the sincerity of the Totonacs, and had informed himself of their forces, he took advantage of this favorable moment to induce that numerous nation to render obedience to the Catholic King. This act was celebrated with the intervention of the notary of the expedition, and with all the other legal solemnities.

This great affair having been happily concluded, Cortés took leave § 12. of those lords in order to carry out another project of the greatest im‐ Founding of portance, which he had formed a little earlier: namely, that of planti‐ Veracruz. ng on that coast a strong colony, which should serve them as a refuge in misfortune, a fortress to keep the Totonacs in the fealty they had already sworn to the Spaniards, a landing place for the new troops that might come to their aid from Spain or from the Antilles, and a warehouse in which to store the goods that might be sent to them by their countrymen, or that they themselves might wish to send to Europe. Thus was founded

this [p. 30] this colony, then, in the very country of the Totonacs, on a plain at

Bk. VIII.

the foot of the mountain of Chiahuitztla, twelve miles from

Cempoala toward the north, and near the new port. (p) They named

it Villa Rica (that is, Rich City) de la Vera Cruz because of the signs of

wealth they had seen there, and because they had landed there on

Good Friday; and this was the first colony of the Spaniards on the

continent of North America. Cortés was the first to set his hand to

the building, in order to encourage his men by his example, who with

the help of the Totonacs constructed in a short time a sufficient num‐

ber of houses and a small fortress capable of offering some resistance

to the arms of the Mexicans.

Meanwhile the two first tax collectors whom Cortés had already

§ 13. New embassy and set at liberty had arrived in Mexico, and had informed the king of all

gift from that had happened, with singular praise of the Spanish general.

Moctezuma.

Moctezuma, who was already on the point of sending an army to pun‐

ish the insolent rashness of those strangers and to drive them from

his dominions, was calmed by this news; and, obliged by the services

which that general had done to his ministers, he sent him two

princes, his nephews (sons perhaps of his brother Cuitláhuac), accom‐

panied by numerous nobility and servants, with a gift of gold work

worth more than a thousand sequins. They thanked Cortés in the

king’s name, and at the same time complained of him because he had

gone so far in friendship with the rebel Totonacs, whence that nation

had had the insolence to

refuse

(p) Almost all the historians have made a mistake concerning the founding of

Veracruz, for they say that the first colony of the Spaniards was the Antigua, or Old,

founded on the river of the same name, and they believe that there were no more

than two places called Veracruz, namely the old Veracruz and the new one planted on

the same sandy ground where Cortés landed; but there is no doubt that there have

been three cities of the same name of Veracruz. The first, founded in 1519 near the

port of Chiahuitztla, to which afterward only the name of Villa Rica remained; the

second, the Old Veracruz, founded in 1523 or 1524; and the third, the New Veracruz,

which today preserves the name of Veracruz, and was founded by order of the Count

of Monterrey, viceroy of Mexico, at the end of the sixteenth century or the beginning

of the seventeenth, and received from Philip III the title of city in 1615. [p. 31] refuse the tribute which it ought to pay to its sovereign. They added that it was only out of regard for such guests that an army had not Bk. VIII. come to punish the rebellion of those peoples; but that in the end they would not remain unpunished. Cortés, after expressing his gratitude in the most fitting terms, sought to clear himself of the charge of friendship with the Totonacs by the necessity in which he had found himself of seeking provisions for his troops, because he had been abandoned by the Mexicans. He said then that, as to the tribute, it was not possible for that nation to serve two lords at once; that he hoped to betake himself shortly to the court, to satisfy the king more fully and to let him see the sincerity of his conduct.

The two princes, after watching with great pleasure and wonder the military exercise of the Spanish cavalry, returned to the court. The lord of Cempoala, who had been much displeased by that embassy, in order to bind the alliance with the Spaniards still closer, presented to Cortés eight maidens, well dressed, that they might marry the captains; and among them was a niece of his, whom he destined for the general himself. Cortés, who had spoken with him several times on the point of religion, told him that he could not accept them unless they first renounced idolatry and embraced Christianity; and he took occasion thereupon to set before him once more the pure and holy truths of our religion, and argued with all the force he could against the vain worship of their lying gods, and especially against the horrible cruelty of their sacrifices. To so fervent an exhortation the Cempoalan replied that, although he prized his friendship exceedingly, he could not oblige him by abandoning the worship of Smashing         of the idols

of Cempoala. those gods, from whose hand they received health, abundance, and all the goods they had, and from whose anger, provoked by ingratitude, they must fear the severest punishments. Cortés’s martial zeal was inflamed the more by this reply; and turning to his soldiers he said to them, “Come, Spaniards, what are we waiting for? How can we endure that these men, who boast

of [p. 32] of being our friends, give to the statues and abominable images of the Bk. VIII.

Devil the worship that is due to our one and true God? How can we

permit them daily, before our very eyes, to sacrifice human victims to

those images so cruelly? Courage, soldiers, courage: now is the time

to show that we are Spaniards, and that we have received as an inheri‐

tance from our ancestors an ardent zeal for our holy religion. Let us

smash the idols, and remove from the sight of these infidels this

wicked fuel of their superstition. If we succeed, we shall do our God

the greatest service we can. If we die in the undertaking, He will re‐

pay us with eternal glory for the sacrifice we shall make to Him of our

life.”

The Cempoalan, who from the look of Cortés and the movements

of the soldiers saw clearly their intention, made a sign to his people

to prepare for the defense of their gods. The Spaniards were already

beginning to climb the steps of the temple when the Cempoalans,

stunned and indignant, cried out that they should beware of commit‐

ting that outrage, if they did not wish all the wrath of the gods to fall

at once upon them. Cortés, being incapable of being frightened by

their threats, answered them that he had already warned them many

times to give up that infamous superstition; that since they had never

been willing to take his counsel, so advantageous to them, neither did

he wish any longer to have them as friends; that if the Totonacs

themselves did not resolve to remove those abominable images, he

with his men would smash them; and that they should take good care

not to commit any hostility against the Spaniards, for they would at

once hurl themselves against them with such force that they would

not leave alive even one Totonac. To these threats Doña Marina

added another, more effective: that if they wished to oppose the pur‐

pose of those strangers, then instead of allying themselves with the

Totonacs against the Mexicans, they would ally themselves with the

Mexicans against the Totonacs, and then their ruin would be in‐

evitable. This argument turned the Cempoalan

from [p. 33] from the first impulse of his zeal, and the fear of the Mexican arms prevailing in his mind over that of his gods, he said to Cortés that he Bk. VIII. might do whatever he pleased; since they had not the heart to lay sacrilegious hands on their gods. No sooner had the Spaniards received this permission than fifty of their soldiers, climbing hastily to the top of the temple, took the idols from the altars and threw them down the steps. The Totonacs meanwhile, weeping bitterly and covering their eyes so as not to see so great a sacrilege, prayed with mournful voice to their gods not to punish in their nation the rashness of those strangers, since they could not prevent it without sacrificing themselves to the fury of the Mexicans. Nevertheless some, either less cowardly or more jealous of the honor of their gods, were preparing to take vengeance on the Spaniards, and would infallibly have come to blows if the Spaniards had not seized the lord of Cempoala and five principal priests, and, threatening to put them to death at once, had not compelled them to restrain the impetus of their fellow citizens.

After an action so bold, in which prudence had no part, Cortés commanded the priests to remove the fragments of the idols from his sight and to cast them into the fire. He was promptly obeyed; and, full of joy then, as if by smashing the idols he had wholly done away with idolatry and driven superstition from those peoples, he told the lord of Cempoala that he would now gladly accept the eight maidens whom he offered him; that from then on he would count the Totonacs among his friends and brothers, and would help them against their enemies in all their needs; that since they would no longer have to worship those detestable images of the Devil, their enemy, he wished to place in that same temple an image of the Mother of the true God, so that they might revere her and implore her protection in all their needs. Then he expatiated at length on the holiness of the Christian religion, and when he had finished he ordered the Cempoalan masons to remove from the walls of the temple those loathsome stains

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                E                       of [p. 34] of human blood, which they kept there as trophies of their inhuman

Bk. VIII. religion, and to clean and whitewash the walls carefully. He had an al‐

tar made in the manner of the Christians, and placed on it the image

of Most Holy Mary. He committed this new sanctuary to the care of

four Cempoalan priests, on condition that they should always go

clean, and dressed in white in place of that black and melancholy

dress which they wore because of their ministry. And so that there

should never be a lack of lights before that sacred image, he taught

them the use of the wax which the bees made in their mountains; and

in order that in the time of his absence they should not put the idols

back there, or in some other way profane that sanctuary, he left there

one of his soldiers named Juan Torres, who on account of his age was

of little use for war, and who made to God the sacrifice of staying

among those infidels to promote His worship. The eight maidens, af‐

ter they had been sufficiently instructed, received holy baptism, tak‐

ing the name of Doña Catalina, the niece of the lord of Cempoala,

and that of Doña Francisca, the daughter of Cuexco, one of the princi‐

pal lords of that nation.

From Cempoala Cortés returned to the new colony of Veracruz,

where he had the consolation of reinforcing his small army with two

more captains and ten soldiers, who landed there from Cuba; to

whom were added shortly afterward six other men, who were taken

by a ruse from a ship of Jamaica.

§ 15.    Before undertaking the journey to Mexico, Cortés wished to give Letters of Cortés and his sovereign an account of all that had happened up to then; and so

of the army to the

Catholic King. that his news might be more welcome, he sent together with it all the

gold they had amassed, each of the officers and soldiers of the army

giving up his share at the suggestion of the same general. In this letter

Cortés forewarned the king against the attempts of the governor of

Cuba. Two other letters were also written to the king, one signed by

the magistrates of the new colony, and the other by the principal offi‐

cers of the army, in which they begged him

to [p. 35] to be pleased to approve what they had done, and to confirm the of‐

Bk. VIII. fices of captain general and chief justice, already conferred on Cortés by their votes, whose person they recommended with the most magnificent praises. These letters, together with the gift of gold, were sent to Spain by the two captains Alonso Hernández Portocarrero and Francisco de Montejo, who set sail on July 16 of the year 1519.

Scarcely had the aforesaid procurators departed when Cortés, who § 16. was always turning great designs over in his mind, carried out an en‐ A famous deed of

Cortés. terprise which by itself would suffice to make known his magnanimity and to immortalize his name. To take from his soldiers every recourse, and with it every hope of returning to Cuba, and to reinforce his small army with all the sailors, after having punished with the extreme penalty two soldiers who were plotting treachery and flight in one of the ships, and with lesser corporal penalties three other accomplices, he induced by arguments and entreaties some of his confidants, and some of the pilots in whom he trusted most, to bore secretly one or two of the ships, to persuade everyone that they had sunk because they were worm-eaten by the broma, and to represent to him that the others were no longer fit for service for the same reason, having lain three months idle in the port. Cortés made use of such a trick lest his men conspire against him, finding themselves reduced to the hard necessity of conquering or dying. Everything was done as he had ordered, and with the consent of the whole army, after the sails, the rigging, the ironwork, and everything that could be of any use had been removed from the ships. “Thus by an effort of magnanimity,” says Robertson, “to which nothing is comparable in history, five hundred men agreed of their own will to shut themselves up in an enemy country, full of powerful and unknown nations, with every road to escape closed, remaining with the sole refuge of their valor and perseverance.” I do not doubt that if Cortés had not taken that counsel, the bold enterprise he was meditating would have been wholly impossible; since the soldiers

E2                       at [p. 36] at the sight of the great obstacles which they met at every step,

Bk. VIII. would have avoided the danger by flight, and the general himself

would have been forced to follow them.

§ 17.    Free, then, of this anxiety, with the alliance with the Totonacs rati‐

Journey of the Spaniards to the

fied, and the proper orders given for the advancement and security of

country of the the new colony, he thought of making his journey to Mexico. He left

Tlaxcalans.

in Veracruz fifty men under the command of Captain Juan de

Escalante, one of the best officers of the army, and charged the

Cempoalans to give aid to the Spaniards in completing the building of

the fortress and to supply them with the provisions they might need;

and he set out on August 16 with four hundred and fifteen Spanish

foot, sixteen horse, two hundred Tlamama, that is, burden-bearers for

the transport of the baggage and the artillery, and some Totonac

troops, among whom were forty nobles, taken by Cortés both as aux‐

iliaries for the war and as hostages of that nation; the most distin‐

guished of whom were three called, according to what the historians

say, Teuch, Mamexi, and Tamalli.

He set out by way of Xalapa and Texotla, and after crossing with

great labor some deserted mountains, where the air was somewhat

raw, arrived at Xocotla (*), a considerable city with fine buildings,

among which rose thirteen temples and the palace of the lord, which

was built of stone and lime and furnished with a great number of

good halls and chambers, the most complete edifice that they had

seen until then in the New World. The king of Mexico had in that

place, and in the hamlets dependent on it, twenty thousand vassals

and five thousand Mexicans as a garrison. Olintetl (this was the name

of the lord of Xocotla) came to meet the Spaniards and lodged them

comfortably in his city; but in providing them with provisions he

showed himself at first somewhat sparing, until through

the

(*) Bernal Díaz and Solís call this city Zocotlan, which could cause some error, since

it would be easy to confuse this city with that of Zacatlán, situated at a distance of

thirty miles from Tlaxcala toward the north. [p. 37] the information supplied by the Totonacs, he formed a more favorable idea of their bravery, of the strength of their arms, and of their Bk. VIII. horses. In the conference which he had with the Spanish general, each boasted in rivalry of the greatness and power of their respective sovereigns. Cortés rashly demanded of him that he at once give obedience to the Catholic King and with some quantity of gold pay homage to his sovereignty. I have gold indeed, replied Olintetl, but I will not give it without the express order of my king. I shall soon see to it, said Cortés, that he orders you to give the gold, and all you have. If he commands me, rejoined Olintetl, not only the gold, and all my goods, but I shall give even my own person. But what Cortés could not obtain by his threats from that lord, he had by sheer liberality from two other persons of distinction of that valley, who, going to visit him at Xocotla, presented him with some gold necklaces and seven or eight slaves. Cortés found himself perplexed here about the road he ought to take to go to Mexico. The lord of Xocotla and the commanders of the Mexican garrison suggested to him that he should go by way of Cholula; but he judged more safe the advice which the Totonacs gave him, to set out by way of Tlaxcala; and in fact he would have perished in Cholula with his whole army had he gone there directly, as will be seen from what we shall say below. In order then to obtain from the Tlaxcalans permission to pass through their country, he sent to the senate four messengers from among those same Cempoalans whom he brought with him; but these, as we shall soon see, did not deliver the embassy in the name of the Spaniards, but in the name of the Totonacs, whether because the Spanish general so ordered, or because it seemed to themselves more fitting.

From Xocotla the Spanish army proceeded to Iztacmaxtitlan, whose population extended for ten or twelve miles in two unbroken rows of houses built on one and the other bank of a small river which runs through the middle of that long and narrow valley; but the city of Iztacmaxtitlan proper, furnished with good buildings and inhabited by six thousand souls

or [p. 38] or thereabouts, occupied the summit of a high and steep mountain,

Bk. VIII. whose lord was one of those two persons of distinction who visited

and made gifts to Cortés at Xocotla. To the natural ruggedness of the

place there had been added by art good walls with their barbicans,

and ditches (*); since, because that stronghold was a frontier against

the Tlaxcalans, it was more exposed to their invasions. There the

Spaniards were very well received and given gifts.

§ 18.    Meanwhile their claim was being debated in the senate of Tlaxcala.

Agitation of the Tlaxcalans, and delib‐ That whole great city had been stirred up by the news of such eration of that senate strangers, and especially by the account which the Cempoalan mes‐

on the affair of the

Spaniards. sengers gave there of their appearance and bravery, of the size of their

ships, of the agility and strength of their horses, and of the frightful

thunder and destructive violence of their artillery. The republic was

then governed by Xicotencatl, lord of the quarter of Tizatlan;

Maxixcatzin, lord of Ocotelolco, general of the forces of the republic;

Tlehuexolotzin, lord of Tepeticpac; and Citlalpopocatzin, lord of

Quiahuiztlan. The Cempoalan messengers (r) were courteously re‐

ceived and lodged in the house destined as a lodging for ambassadors,

and after they had rested and dined they were introduced into the

hall of the senate to set forth their embassy. There, after making a

most profound bow and all the other ceremonies customary on such

occasions, they spoke thus: “Most great and valiant lords, may the

gods prosper you and grant you victories over your enemies. The lord

of Cempoala, and

with

(*) Cortés, in his second letter to Charles V, compares the fortress of

Iztacmaxtitlan with the best in Spain.

(r) Bernal Díaz says that the messengers were only two, and that as soon as they

arrived in Tlaxcala they were put in prison; but Cortés himself, who sent them, af‐

firms that they were four, and from the context of his account it is seen that Bernal

Díaz was ill informed of what happened in Tlaxcala. The narrative of this writer, con‐

trary to that of other early historians, both Spanish and Indian, has led many authors

into error, and among them Robertson.

[p. 39]

Bk. VIII. with him the whole Totonac nation greet you, and let you know that from the east there have arrived in our country, on large boats, certain heroes, strong and exceedingly courageous, with whose help we now see ourselves free of the tyrannical domination of the king of Mexico. They declare themselves subjects of a powerful monarch, in whose name they wish to visit you, offering to give you knowledge of the true God, and to help you against your ancient and mortal enemy. Our nation, by reason of that close friendship with your republic which it has constantly cultivated, advises you to receive these heroes as friends, who, although few, are worth many.” Maxixcatzin answered them in the name of the senate that they thanked the Totonac lords for the news and for the advice, and those brave strangers for the help which they offered to give them, but that to deliberate on a point of such great weight, time was needed; that in the meantime they should return to their lodging, where they would be treated with the distinction which their birth and their character demanded. The messengers withdrew, and the senate went into consultation.

Maxixcatzin, who was held in high esteem by all, both for his prudence and for his kindness, said that the advice given by such faithful friends, so hostile to the greatest enemy of the republic, was not to be rejected; that those strangers, judging by the tokens which the Cempoalans gave of them, seemed to be those heroes who, according to their tradition, were to arrive in that country; that the earthquakes which had been felt a short time before, the comet which was then letting itself be seen in the sky, and several other events of those last years were indications that the time for the fulfillment of that tradition had already arrived; that if they were immortal, the republic would try in vain to oppose their entry. “Our refusal,” he added, “might cause us the gravest harm, and for the king of Mexico it would be a subject of malicious pleasure to see introduced by force into Tlaxcala those whom the republic did not willingly

accept [p. 40] accept:” he was nevertheless of the opinion that they should be reBk. VIII.

ceived in a friendly manner. This view was heard with applause, but

Xicotencatl at once opposed it (s), an old man of great authority be‐

cause of his long experience in civil and military affairs. “Our law,” he

said, “orders us to welcome foreigners; not, however, enemies who

might do harm to the state. These men, who claim to enter our city,

seem to me more like monsters cast up by the sea, because it could

not bear them in its waters, than gods come from heaven, as some

foolishly imagine. Is it possible that they are gods, those who seek

with such avidity gold and pleasures? And what should we not fear

from them in a country so poor as ours, where we are deprived even

of salt? It does an injury to the bravery of our nation to think it so

easily conquered by a few strangers. If they are mortal, the arms of

the Tlaxcalans will make it plain to the world; and if they are immor‐

tal, there will be time to appease their anger with homage and to im‐

plore their clemency with repentance. Let their request, then, be re‐

jected, and if they should ever wish to enter by force, let their rash‐

ness be repulsed with arms.” This opposition of views between two

persons of such authority divided the minds of the other senators.

Those who were inclined toward trade and accustomed to a peaceful

life adhered to the opinion of Maxixcatzin, and the military men em‐

braced that of Xicotencatl. Temiloltecatl, one of the senators (t), sug‐

gested a compromise to reconcile both opinions. He proposed that

there should be

sent

(s) Solís ascribes to the younger Xicotencatl the speech of his old father; but we

believe that we should give greater credence to the early historians, who informed

themselves from the Tlaxcalans themselves.

(t) Herrera and Torquemada say that Temiloltecatl was one of the four lords of

Tlaxcala; but from the memoirs of Camargo and of other Tlaxcalans, and even from

Torquemada himself in bk. 3 and in bk. 16, it appears that the four lords were then

those mentioned by us above. It may be believed, in order to save the truth of what

Herrera and Torquemada say, that Tlehuexolotzin also had the name of Temiloltecatl,

just as he had that of Tezcacaltecuhtli; since we know that many were called by two

and even by three names. [p. 41] sent to the leader of those strangers a courteous and friendly reply,

Bk. VIII. granting him permission to enter it; but that at the same time orders be given to Xicotencatl the younger, son of old Xicotencatl, to go out with the Otomi troops of the republic to oppose their passage and to try their strength. “If we remain victors,” he said, “the glory of our arms will be immortal; if we are defeated, we shall blame the Otomis, and give it to be understood that they undertook the war without our order.” (u) Political expedients too frequent in the world, especially among civilized nations; but not for that reason less contrary to the good faith which men owe one another. The senate accepted the counsel of Temiloltecatl; but before dispatching the messengers with the reply, it gave the proper orders to Xicotencatl. He was a fearless young man, an enemy of peace, and too much inclined to military glory; whence he welcomed the commission, as one that furnished him so fine an occasion to show his bravery.

Cortés, after waiting eight days for the reply of the senate, believing that such delay was the effect of that slowness which the majesty of potentates is wont to affect, and not doubting, because of what the Cempoalans told him, that he would be well received by the Tlaxcalans, left Iztacmaxtitlan with his whole army, which besides the Totonacs and the Spaniards was composed of a fair number of Mexican troops of the garrison of Xocotla, and marched in good order, as was his custom, as far as the great wall which on that side separated the states of Tlaxcala from those of Mexico; the description and measurements of which we gave already in the preceding book, where we discussed the fortifications of the Mexicans. It had been built by the Tlaxcalans to defend

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                    F                themselves

(u) We have already said elsewhere that very many Otomis had taken refuge in Tlaxcala to escape from the domination of the Mexicans, and that they served that republic very well. [p. 42] themselves against the Mexicans on the eastern side (x), just as they Bk. VIII.

had made great ditches and entrenchments for the same reason on

the western side. The gate of the wall, which was always guarded by

the Otomi troops, now, when it was most needed, was found, we do

not know why, without any garrison; whence the Spanish army en‐

tered the lands of the republic without any hindrance, which other‐

wise it could not have done without shedding much blood.

On that day, which was August 31, some armed Indians let them‐

selves be seen, and as the cavalry, which was scouting the road,

wished to overtake them to get from them some information about

the resolution of the senate, two horses were killed and three others

wounded, and two men: a truly great loss for so small a cavalry. There

then appeared a body of troops estimated at about four thousand

men, against which the Spaniards and the allies hurled themselves,

and in a short time defeated it with the death of fifty Otomis. Soon

after there arrived two of the Cempoalan messengers with some

Tlaxcalans (y), who paid their compliments to Cortés in the name of

the senate and informed him of the permission which was granted

him to go with his army to Tlaxcala, blaming the Otomis for the hos‐

tilities which he had suffered and offering to pay him for the horses

killed by them. Cortés pretended to believe it, and protested his grat‐

itude toward the senate. The Tlaxcalans took their leave, and with‐

drew their dead from the field to burn them. Cortés for his part had

the two horses that were killed buried, so that the sight of them

should not encourage the enemy to make new hostilities.

The

(x) From what the Mexicans said to Cortés about the aforesaid wall, someone

might be persuaded that they were the ones who built it; but there is no doubt that

it was built by the Tlaxcalans.

(y) Bernal Díaz says that the first Cempoalan messengers returned to Cortés be‐

fore he had entered the country of Tlaxcala; but Cortés frankly affirms the contrary.

As for the account of the Cempoalan messengers who had remained in Tlaxcala, al‐

though it has been believed by almost all the Spanish historians, it is wholly incredi‐

ble for the reasons adduced by us. Dr. Robertson reports several conjectures to make

it plausible; but in vain.

[p. 43]

The next day the Spanish army marched as far as the neighborhood of two mountains, between which there were some ravines. There the Bk. VIII. other two Cempoalan messengers, who had remained in Tlaxcala, arrived, bathed in sweat and tears, and blaming the perfidy and cruelty of the Tlaxcalans; because, disregarding the law of nations, they had maltreated and imprisoned them, and had destined them for sacrifice, from which they said they had freed themselves by untying one another. This account of the Cempoalans was certainly false: for it was altogether impossible, not to say difficult, for the victims to free themselves by themselves, both because of the narrowness of the cage in which they were kept and because of the vigilance of the guards who watched them; besides which there is no record that the Tlaxcalans ever failed in the respect due to the character of ambassadors, and much less in the case of these, who belonged to a nation so closely joined to them in friendship as the Totonac. What seems more probable is that the senate, after sending back the first two messengers, kept the other two in order to dispatch them when they should have tested the strength of the Spaniards, and that they, impatient of waiting, absented themselves secretly, and sought to excuse their flight with such pretexts.

Scarcely had the Cempoalans finished their account when a

§ 19. Tlaxcalan squadron of about a thousand men appeared, who, as soon The war of Tlaxcala. as they came near the Spaniards, began to hurl stones, darts, and arrows at them. Cortés, after having declared to them before the royal notary of the expedition, by means of three prisoners, that they were not coming to do them any harm, and after having begged them not to treat them as enemies, seeing that this was of no avail, gave the order to repulse them. The Tlaxcalans went on retreating until they had led the Spaniards to the ravines, where the horses could not hold their own, and where a large army awaited them; as to the number of which there is a great variety among the authors. (z) There was a terrible fight there, in which the Spaniards believed

F2                  themselves

(z) Bernal Díaz says that the army of the Tlaxcalans was of forty thousand [p. 44] themselves lost; but, rallying in the best order they could, and encouraged by Bk. VIII.

the exhortations and the example of their general, the Spaniards withdrew

from that very dangerous position, and, coming into the plain, with the ar‐

tillery and the horses made such great slaughter among the enemy that they

forced them to turn back. Of the Tlaxcalans very many were wounded, and

not a few remained dead. Of the Spaniards, although fifteen were dangerously

wounded, only one died the next day. On this occasion there took place a fa‐

mous duel between a Tlaxcalan captain and a Cempoalan noble, one of those

who had gone as messengers to Tlaxcala. They fought bravely for a good while

in view of both armies; but in the end the Cempoalan prevailed, who, having

thrown his opponent to the ground, cut off his head and carried it in triumph

to the camp. The victory was celebrated with acclamations and with military

music. The place where this battle was fought was called Teoatzinco, that is,

the place of the divine water.

That night the Spanish army encamped on a hill, where there was a tower,

about eighteen miles from the capital city of Tlaxcala. Huts were built there

for the convenience of the troops, and entrenchments were made for their

defense. There the camp of the Spaniards remained until the peace with the

Tlaxcalans.

Cortés, in order to oblige the Tlaxcalans by his hostilities to accept the

peace and friendship which he offered them, sallied forth on September 3

with his cavalry, a hundred Spanish foot, four hundred Cempoalans, and three

hundred Mexicans of the garrison of Iztacmaxtitlan, burned five or six neigh‐

boring hamlets, and took four hundred prisoners, whom, after caressing them

and giving them gifts, he set at liberty, charging the leading men

among

men or so. To Cortés it seemed that it exceeded a hundred thousand. Other historians wrote

that it was thirty thousand. It is difficult to judge by the eye the number of a great army, espe‐

cially when the order of the European militia is not observed in it. In order not to expose my‐

self to error, I am content to say that the army was large. [p. 45] among them to go and offer on his part peace to the heads of the na‐

Bk. VIII. tion. These went straight to Xicotencatl the younger, who was encamped with a large army six miles from that hill. This proud Tlaxcalan replied that if the Spaniards wished to treat of peace they should go to the capital, where they would be victims consecrated to their gods, and their flesh destined to be food for the Tlaxcalans; that as for him, the next day he would come in person to give them a decisive reply. This resolution, made known to the Spaniards by the same messengers, threw them into such great consternation that that night they prepared for death by sacramental confession, without however neglecting the proper arrangements for their defense.

The next day, September 5, the Tlaxcalan army appeared, no less terrible to the sight by its innumerable multitude (A) than beautiful for the lovely variety of their plumes and other military ornaments. It was divided into five squadrons of ten thousand men each; each carried its own standard, and in the rear guard, according to the custom of that nation, came the common standard of the republic, which was, as we have said elsewhere, a golden eagle with outspread wings. The arrogant Xicotencatl, to show the little account he made of the Spaniards, and that he did not wish to take them by hunger but to conquer them by arms, sent them a refreshment of three hundred turkeys and two hundred baskets of tamalli, exhorting them to restore their strength for the battle. Shortly afterward he detached two thousand courageous men, so that they should

assault

(A) Cortés says that the army of the Tlaxcalans was of more than 149,000 men. Bernal Díaz affirms, as a thing certain and well known to him, that it consisted of only 50,000 men, namely 10,000 of Maxixcatzin, 10,000 of Xicotencatl, 10,000 of Tlehuexolotzin, 10,000 of Chichimecatecuhtli, one of the most distinguished lords of that republic, and 10,000 of Tecpanecatl, lord of Topoyanco, a considerable city of the same republic, although these names have been much altered by this author. This number seems more probable; that which is read in the letter of Cortés may be believed to be a mistake of the first printer of it. [p. 46] assault the camp of the Spaniards. This assault was so violent that, Bk. VIII. forcing the entrenchments, they entered the camp and fought hand to hand with the Spaniards. The Tlaxcalans might have been victors this time, not so much because of the far superior number of their troops as because of their bravery and the quality of their arms, which were pikes, lances, swords, and darts with two and three points, had not the discord that arose among them made victory easier for their enemies. The son of Chichimecatecuhtli, who was commander of the body of troops of his father (*), having been insulted with words by the arrogant Xicotencatl, conceived such great indignation that he challenged him to a duel, which should decide the question of their courage and of their fortune; and not being able to obtain this satisfaction from him, in order to take some vengeance he withdrew from the field the troops who were under his orders, and induced the same course in those of Tlehuexolotzin. Despite so great a diminution of the army, the battle was very obstinate and bloody. The Spaniards, after having courageously repulsed the troops that had assaulted their camp, marched in battle order against the main body of the Tlaxcalan army. The slaughter which the artillery made in the crowded multitude was not enough to make the Tlaxcalans turn their backs, nor to prevent them from promptly filling those gaps which the dead left; indeed with their firmness and intrepidity they had thrown the Spaniards into confusion, in spite of the clamors and reproaches of Cortés and his captains. At last, after four hours of fighting, the Spaniards returned victorious to their camp, although the Tlaxcalans did not cease to assault them often on that same day. Of the Spaniards only one man was lost, and sixty were wounded, and all the horses. Of the Tlaxcalans very many were killed; but not even one corpse

## was

(*) Solís says that Chichimecatecuhtli was an ally of the Tlaxcalans, but this is a manifest error, since we know from all the historians that he was a Tlaxcalan, and one of the most distinguished lords of that republic. [p. 47] was seen by the Spaniards, because of the great diligence and prompt‐

Bk. VIII. ness with which they took them away from the field of battle.

Xicotencatl, disgusted at the unfortunate outcome of this expedition, had the diviners of Tlaxcala consulted, and they replied that those strangers, as children of the Sun, were invincible during the day; but as soon as night arrived, together with the vital heat of that planet their strength to defend themselves also failed. In consequence of such an oracle that general resolved to make an assault upon the Spanish camp by night. Meanwhile Cortés sallied forth once more to commit hostilities in the neighboring villages, of which he burned ten, and among them one of three thousand hearths, and returned with some prisoners.

Xicotencatl, so that the blow he was planning against the Spaniards should not fail, wished first to inform himself of the disposition and strength of their camp. He therefore sent fifty men to Cortés with a gift accompanied by courteous expressions of goodwill, charging them to observe everything; but they could not make their observations with so much dissimulation that Teuch, one of the three principal Cempoalans, did not notice it and at once warn Cortés. That general, having called some of the spies aside, forced them with threats to declare that Xicotencatl was preparing to make the assault the following night, and that they had been sent expressly to observe by what part of the camp entry would be easiest for him. Cortés, having heard their confession, had the hands of all fifty cut off (B), and sent them back to Xicotencatl, charging them to let him know that whether he came to his camp by day or by night, he would always make him know who the Spaniards were; and, these circumstances appearing to him very opportune for battle, before the enemy had completed all their preparations for the assault, he sallied forth at nightfall with a good number

of

(B) Some Spanish historians say that only the fingers of the Tlaxcalan spies were cut off; but Cortés himself says that he had their hands cut off. [p. 48] of troops, and with his horses, on which he had bells put on the

Bk. VIII.

breast-straps, and went to meet the enemy, who were just then

marching toward the Spanish camp. The sight of the punishment in‐

flicted on the spies, and the noise of the bells in the silence and the

darkness of the night, so frightened the Tlaxcalans that they at once

fell into disorder and fled by various roads; and Xicotencatl himself

returned in shame to Tlaxcala. Maxixcatzin therefore took the occa‐

sion to press his first opinion, adding now to the reasons already ad‐

duced by him the disastrous experience of so many expeditions that

had turned out unhappily: which moved all the minds of the senate to

peace.

§ 20.     While this affair was being debated in Tlaxcala, in Mexico they New embassies and

gifts from were consulting about what should be done with those strangers.

Moctezuma to Moctezuma, aware of the victories of the Spaniards, and fearing by

Cortés.

now their confederation with the Tlaxcalans, summoned the king of

Texcoco, his nephew, Prince Cuitláhuac, and other councilors, set be‐

fore them the state of affairs, revealed his fears, and asked their coun‐

sel. The king of Texcoco held to his first opinion, namely that those

strangers should be magnificently treated in all the places through

which they passed; that they should be graciously admitted into the

court, and their proposals heard, as those of any vassal would be

heard, the king always displaying his superiority and observing that

decorum which befits the majesty of the throne; that if they should

ever plot anything against the person of the king or against the state,

force and severity should be employed against them. Prince

Cuitláhuac repeated what he had said in the other conference, name‐

ly that it was not expedient to receive those strangers in the court;

that a good gift should be sent to their leader, he should be asked

what he liked in that country for the great lord in whose name he

came, and the friendship and good understanding of the Mexicans

should be offered him; but that at the same time new entreaties

should be made to him to return to his own country. Among the

other

councilors [p. 49] councilors some embraced the opinion of the king of Texcoco, and

Bk. VIII. others that of the lord of Iztapalapa, to which Moctezuma also adhered. This unhappy king found everywhere only objects and reasons to fear. The imminent confederation of the Tlaxcalans with the Spaniards kept him in extreme disquiet. On another side, Cortés’s alliance with Prince Ixtlilxochitl, his nephew and sworn enemy, disquieted him, who since he had conspired against the king of Texcoco, his brother, had never laid down his arms, and just then was in Otompan at the head of a formidable army. These fears were increased by the rebellion of several provinces which had followed the example of the Totonacs.

He therefore sent six ambassadors to Cortés with a thousand curious cotton garments, and a good quantity of gold and of lovely feathers, and charged them to congratulate him on his victories, to offer him other, greater gifts, and to dissuade him from the journey to Mexico by representing to him the difficulty of the road and other obstacles hard to overcome. These ambassadors set out promptly with a retinue of more than two hundred men, and, arriving at the camp of the Spaniards, carried out exactly what had been ordered them. Cortés received them with all the honor due to their character, and declared himself extremely obliged to the goodness of so great a monarch; but he purposely detained the ambassadors, hoping that in the meantime some skirmish with the Tlaxcalans might occur which would establish before the Mexicans the bravery of his troops and the superiority of European arms; or else that, peace having been made with the republic, they might be witnesses of the severity with which he wished to reproach the Tlaxcalans for their obstinacy. It was not long before the occasion he so greatly desired presented itself. Three enemy squadrons fell upon the camp of the Spaniards with frightful yells and a tempest of arrows and darts. Cortés, although he had taken a purge that very day, mounted his horse and sallied out intrepidly against the Tlaxcalans, who without too much effort

History of Mexico, Vol. III.               G                    were [p. 50] were defeated in sight of the Mexican ambassadors.

Bk. VIII.

The partisans of old Xicotencatl being at last persuaded that war

§ 21. with the Spaniards was not expedient for the republic, and fearing bePeace and confederation of the Tlaxcalans

sides that these might ally themselves with the Mexicans, resolved by

with the Spaniards. common consent to make peace, and took as mediator for it that very

man who had been general in that war. Xicotencatl, although at first

he refused to do so for the shame he felt at the unfortunate outcome

of the war, was in the end obliged to take charge of the commission.

He betook himself to the camp of the Spaniards with a noble and nu‐

merous retinue, saluted Cortés in the name of the whole republic, ex‐

cused the hostilities already committed with the pretext that he had

believed him an ally of Moctezuma, both on account of the superb

gifts sent him from Mexico and because of the great Mexican troop

that he brought with him, promised a firm peace and an eternal al‐

liance between the Spaniards and the Tlaxcalans, and presented him

with a little gold and some bales of cotton garments, excusing the

scantiness of the offering by the poverty of their country, caused by

the perpetual war with the Mexicans, who prevented their trade with

other provinces. Cortés omitted no demonstration of respect toward

Xicotencatl: he made a show of being satisfied with his excuses; but

he required that the peace be sincere and lasting; since if they ever

broke it he would take so terrible a vengeance on them that it would

serve as an example to the other peoples.

The peace made, and Xicotencatl dismissed, Cortés had the Holy

Sacrifice of the Mass celebrated in thanksgiving to the Most High.

Everyone can imagine the displeasure which the Mexican ambas‐

sadors felt on seeing such an accommodation concluded. They com‐

plained to Cortés, and reproached him for his too great readiness in

giving credence to the promises of men so perfidious as the

Tlaxcalans. They told him that those appearances of peace were di‐

rected to no other end than that of inspiring him with confidence in

order to draw him to their capital, and to do there without danger to

themselves what they had not

been [p. 51] been able to obtain by arms in the open field; that he should compare the conduct of that senate with that of the court of Mexico. The Bk. VIII. Tlaxcalans, after granting them with show of peace permission to enter their country, had not ceased to make war on them until they recognized all their efforts as wholly useless. From the Mexicans, on the contrary, they had suffered no hostility; indeed they had had the most distinguished reception, and the greatest attentions and services in all the places of their dominions where they had arrived, and from their sovereign the most notable proofs of goodwill and friendship. Cortés replied that he had never believed that he did any wrong by that accommodation to the court of Mexico, to which he declared himself extremely obliged; since he wished to have peace with everyone; that for the rest he did not fear the Tlaxcalans in case they should choose to be his enemies; that for him and for the other Spaniards it was all one to be assailed in the city as in the open country, by day as by night, since they knew how to conquer in any place and time; that indeed for the very reason that those men said about the Tlaxcalans, he wished to go to their city, in order to have occasion to take there a resounding vengeance for their perfidy.

The Tlaxcalans were far from that disloyalty which the Mexicans imputed to them; for from the very moment in which the senate decreed peace they were always the most faithful allies of the Spaniards, as will be seen in the course of this history. The senate was eager to have Cortés in Tlaxcala with all his troops in order to bind still closer the mutual friendship and to treat seriously of the confederation against the Mexicans, and they had already, by means of their messengers, invited that general to take lodging in their houses, declaring the regret they felt at seeing such illustrious friends of the republic in so great discomfort.

The alliance of the Tlaxcalans was not the only fruit which the Spaniards gathered from their victories. In the same camp in which he had heard the ambassador of Tlaxcala,

once been vassals of the Crown of Mexico and enemies of the

Embassy of Prince Tlaxcalans, had withdrawn from the dominion of the Mexicans and Ixtlilxochitl, and con‐

federation of the confederated with the Tlaxcalans, their neighbors, and now followed
