Huexotzincas. their example in the confederation with the Spaniards. Prince

Ixtlilxochitl sent ambassadors to Cortés to congratulate him on the

victories won over the Tlaxcalans, and to invite him to make the jour‐

ney by way of Teotlalpan, where he wished to unite his forces with

those of the Spaniards in order to make war on the King of Mexico.

Cortés, when he had become sufficiently informed of the quality, the

pretensions, and the forces of that prince, gladly accepted his al‐

liance, and offered to place him on the throne of Acolhuacan.

At the same time the Mexican ambassador, who was expected, re‐

turned from the court with a gift of gold jewels worth fifteen hundred

sequins, and of two hundred precious garments of feathers, and with

fresh entreaties from Moctezuma to turn that general aside from the

journey to Mexico and from friendship with the Tlaxcalans: vain ef‐

forts of the faint-heartedness of that monarch, since to spend so

much gold on gifts to those strangers was in truth nothing other than

to buy at a higher price the chains which were soon to bind his

liberty.

§ 23.

Six days had passed since the peace made with the Tlaxcalans,

Submission of the when the four lords of that republic, in order to oblige the Spaniards Republic of Tlaxcala to the Catholic King. to go to Tlaxcala, had themselves carried in litters to the Spanish

camp with a great retinue. The demonstrations of joy and respect

were extraordinary on both sides. That illustrious senate, not content

with ratifying the alliance, spontaneously submitted to the Catholic

King, which was all the more welcome to the Spaniards in that the

liberty the Tlaxcalans had enjoyed from time immemorial had been

the dearer to them. They complained affectionately of Cortés’s dis‐

trust, and by their entreaties induced him to fix his departure for

Tlaxcala for the next day.

Fifty-five

[p. 53]

Fifty-five Spaniards were by now missing from the number that had

Bk. VIII. enlisted in Cuba, and the survivors were for the most part wounded or ailing; and such was the dismay this had caused among the soldiers that they not only spoke ill of their general in private, but even begged him to go back to Veracruz. But Cortés rallied them, and with effective reasons of honor, and with his own example of courage and constancy in perils and in hardships, rekindled them for that bold undertaking. At length they took fresh heart, with the hope of a happy outcome, founded on the confederations then made.

The Mexican ambassadors, who still kept with Cortés, declined to § 24. accompany him to Tlaxcala; but he persuaded them to go with him, Entry         of the

Spaniards into promising them that at his side they would be perfectly safe. This dif‐ Tlaxcala. ficulty overcome, the army marched, well ordered and prepared for any eventuality. In the cities of Tecompantzinco and Atlihuetzian it was received with all possible magnificence, though not comparable with that of the capital, from which the four lords of the republic came out to meet the Spaniards with a fine and numerous dance of the nobility, and with so great a throng of people that some estimated it at more than a hundred thousand persons: a number not improbable, considering the population of Tlaxcala and the surprising novelty of those extraordinary men, who aroused the curiosity of all those peoples. In all the streets of the city there had been set up, according to the custom of those nations, arches of branches and flowers, and everywhere there resounded a confused music of instruments and acclamations, with such great demonstrations of joy that it seemed rather the triumph of the republic that was being celebrated than that of its enemies. This day, so memorable in the annals of Tlaxcala, was September 23, 1519.

At that time this city was one of the most remarkable of the country of Anahuac. Cortés in his letters to Charles V affirms that in size, in population, in the quality of its buildings, and in the abundance of the things necessary to

life [p. 54] life, it surpassed Granada at the time it was taken from the Moors;

Bk. VIII. and that in its market, of which he gives a description, as many as thirty thousand merchants gathered daily. The same Conqueror testifies that, having obtained from the senate that the houses and the inhabitants in the cities, villages, and hamlets of the republic be counted, there were found more than a hundred and fifty thousand houses, and more than five hundred thousand inhabitants.

The Tlaxcalans had prepared for the Spaniards, and for all their allies, a fine and comfortable lodging. Cortés wished the Mexican ambassadors to lodge in an apartment near his own, as much to do them honor as to remove from their minds the distrust of the Tlaxcalans. The chiefs of the republic, to give the Spaniards a new proof of their sincere friendship, presented to Cortés, according to the custom of those peoples, three hundred beautiful young women. Cortés at first refused them, alleging that the Christian law forbade polygamy; but then, in order not to displease them, he accepted some of them as maids to Doña Marina. In spite of such a refusal they soon presented him with five virgins of the first nobility, whom Cortés did accept, in order to strengthen still more the friendship with that republic. These virgins, together with the others, were promptly instructed, and, renouncing the superstition of their parents, were solemnly baptized in a temple, which was cleaned and made ready for the celebration there of the most holy mysteries of our religion. One of the five ladies was a daughter of Prince Maxixcatzin, who took at baptism the name of Doña Elvira, and she fell to Captain Juan Velázquez de León. Another, a daughter of the elder Xicotencatl, was called Doña Luisa Techquihuatzin, and was given to Captain Pedro de Alvarado, (C) and the other three

were

(C) Alvarado had by Doña Luisa two children, Don Pedro and Doña Leonor Alvarado. The latter married Don Francisco de la Cueva, a knight of the Order of Santiago, governor of Guatemala, and a cousin of the Duke of Alburquerque, and from this marriage several children were born. [p. 55] were given to the captains Cristóbal de Olid, Gonzalo de Sandoval, and Alonso de Ávila.                                                      Bk. VIII.

Encouraged by so happy a beginning, Cortés wished to persuade the chiefs of the republic and the nobility to detest their superstition and to acknowledge the one true Deity; but they, though convinced by his reasons, and confessing the goodness and the power of the God whom the Spaniards worshiped, were nevertheless not induced to renounce their so-called gods, because they considered them necessary for human happiness. “Our god Camaxtle,” they said, “grants us victory over our enemies; our goddess Matlalcueye sends the rain necessary to our fields, and defends us from the inundations of the Zahuapan. (*) To each of our gods we are indebted for a part of the happiness of our life, and their wrath, provoked by our ingratitude, could bring down the most terrible punishments upon the state.” Cortés, animated by a zeal too ardent and violent, wished to do to the idols of Tlaxcala what he had done with good success to those of Cempoala; but Father Olmedo and other persons of standing dissuaded him from so rash an attempt, representing to him that such violence, besides being unsuited to the peaceful promulgation of the Gospel, could cause the total ruin of the Spaniards in a city so populous and so attached to superstition. Nevertheless, during the twenty days that he stayed there he never ceased to reproach them with the abominable cruelty of their sacrifices, and to inculcate the purity and the holiness of the Christian religion, the falsity of the gods they worshiped, and the existence of a Supreme Being, who rules all natural causes and watches with admirable providence over the preservation of his creatures. These exhortations, made by a man of such authority, of whom the Tlaxcalans had formed too exalted an idea, though they did not produce all the fruit that was desired, were nevertheless quite useful, since the senate

## was

(*) A river of Tlaxcala. [p. 56] was moved by them at last to consent that the cages be broken open, Bk. VIII.

and that all the prisoners and slaves who were kept there to be sacri‐

ficed to their gods at the solemn festivals, or in the public needs of

the state, be set at liberty.

Thus every day the alliance of the Tlaxcalans was established more

firmly by new demonstrations, in spite of the repeated suggestions of

the Mexican ambassadors to break it. Cortés, although well persuad‐

ed of the sincerity of the Tlaxcalans, had given orders to his troops to

remain constantly under arms against whatever might happen. The

senate took offense at this, and complained bitterly of Cortés’s dis‐

trust after so many and such clear proofs of their good faith; but

Cortés excused himself, protesting that this was not done out of dis‐

trust of the Tlaxcalans, but rather because it was the custom of the

Spaniards. The senate was appeased by the reply, and such discipline

in soldiers so pleased them that Maxixcatzin wished to introduce it

among the troops of the republic.

At last Cortés, having procured during the time he stayed in

Tlaxcala a more exact knowledge of the situation of the city of

Mexico, of the forces of that kingdom, and of everything that could

serve his purpose, determined to continue his journey; but before de‐

parting he gave the Tlaxcalans, as a gift, a great number of the finest

garments that the King of Mexico had sent him. He was in doubt

about the road he should take to go to that court. The Mexican am‐

bassadors wished him to go by way of Cholula, where good lodging

had been prepared for all his men. The Tlaxcalans dissuaded him

from such a course, representing the perfidy of the Cholulans, and

counseled him to take the road by Huexotzinco, a state likewise con‐

federated with the Tlaxcalans and with the Spaniards; but Cortés re‐

solved to go by way of Cholula, as much to please the ambassadors as

to show the Tlaxcalans how little account he made of the efforts of

his enemies.

The Cholulans had formerly been allies of the Tlaxcalans; but on

the arrival of the Spaniards they were confederated with the

Mexicans, and sworn enemies

of [p. 57] of that republic. The cause of so great an enmity had been the perfidy

Bk. VIII. of the Cholulans. In a battle that, as allies of the Tlaxcalans, they gave to the Mexicans, being in the vanguard of the army, they went over, § 25. by a sudden maneuver, into the rear guard, and, attacking the Enmity               between the

Tlaxcalans and the Tlaxcalans from behind while the Mexicans beat them in front, made Cholulans. a great slaughter among them. The hatred kindled in the hearts of the Tlaxcalans by so detestable a treachery was seeking some occasion to avenge it, and none seemed to them more opportune than this, now that they found themselves confederated with the Spaniards. Now, in order to inspire the same hatred in Cortés, and to move him to war against that state, they made him aware of the conduct of the Cholulans toward him; for they had not sent their messengers to pay him compliments, whereas the Huexotzincas had sent theirs, although they were somewhat farther away than the Cholulans. They told him, moreover, of the message which they said they had received from the Cholulans, reproaching them for the alliance made with the Spaniards, calling them cowards and base, and threatening them that if they attempted anything against that Holy City they would all die drowned; for among their other errors they were persuaded that whenever they pleased they could, merely by scraping the walls of the sanctuary of Quetzalcoatl, make great rivers spring from it, which in a moment would flood the city; and though the Tlaxcalans feared such a misfortune, the desire for vengeance surpassed their fear.

Moved by such suggestions, Cortés sent four Tlaxcalan nobles to Cholula to learn from those lords the reason why they had not shown toward him the regard that the Huexotzincas had shown. The Cholulans excused themselves on the ground of their enmity with the Tlaxcalans, whom they could never trust. (D) This reply was brought by four commoners, which

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.             H                        was

(D) Torquemada adds that the Cholulans detained the chief of the Tlaxcalan messengers, called Patlahuatzin, and with unheard-of cruelty flayed [p. 58] was a manifest demonstration of contempt. Cortés, warned of this by the

Bk. VIII. Tlaxcalans, had those lords told, through four Cempoalans sent there by him,

that the embassy of a monarch so great as the King of Spain was not to be en‐

trusted to such base messengers, since not even they themselves were worthy

to hear it; that they should know that the Catholic King was the true lord of

all that country, and that in his name he was coming to exact the homage of

those peoples; that those who submitted to him would be honored, and the

rebels would be punished according to their deserts; that they should there‐

fore appear within three days in Tlaxcala, to render obedience to their sov‐

ereign; otherwise they would be treated as enemies. The Cholulans, although

among themselves they mocked, as may be believed, so arrogant an embassy,

nevertheless, in order to dissemble their malign intent, presented themselves

the next day to Cortés, begging him to excuse their failure, caused by their

enmity with the Tlaxcalans, and protesting that they were not only friends of

the Spaniards but vassals as well of the King of Spain.

§ 26.

Entry of the   Having therefore resolved on the journey by way of Cholula, Cortés left Spaniards into

Cholula. Tlaxcala with all his men and with a great number of Tlaxcalan troops (E),

which he promptly dismissed, except six thousand men whom he took with

him. A little before arriving at Cholula, the principal lords came out to meet

him, and the priests with censers in their hands and with music of instru‐

ments, and after performing the customary ceremonies of respect they told

the general to enter, by all means, with all his Spaniards

and

his face and arms, and cut off his hands; but this account is certainly false, for such an atrocity

could not have been unknown to the Spaniards; yet neither Bernal Díaz, nor Cortés, nor any

other of the first historians makes mention of it. Cortés would not have omitted it in his letter

to Charles V to justify the severity of the punishment inflicted on the Cholulans; nor is it likely

that after such an outrage committed against one of his messengers he would have waited for

other signs of the perfidy of the Cholulans in order to punish it.

(E) Cortés says that the Tlaxcalans who accompanied him to within six miles of Cholula

were about a hundred thousand warriors. Bernal Díaz counts only two thousand of the ten

thousand that the senate offered him; but this author seems to have forgotten. [p. 59] and with the Totonacs; but that he should not permit the Tlaxcalans,

Bk. VIII. their enemies, to enter. Cortés consented in order to please them, and the Tlaxcalans remained encamped outside the city, imitating in the arrangement of the camp, in the order of the sentinels, and in other things the military discipline of the Spaniards. On the entry of the Spanish army into Cholula there was a similar throng of people, and the same ceremonies, acclamations, and courtesies as there had been in Tlaxcala, but not so sincere.

At that time Cholula was a populous city, eighteen miles distant from Tlaxcala to the south, and about sixty from Mexico to the east, and no less celebrated for the commerce of its inhabitants than for its religion. It was situated, as it is at present, in a beautiful plain, and not far from that cluster of high mountains which surround the valley of Mexico on the east. Its population at that time was, according to Cortés, about forty thousand houses, and almost as many more were in the surrounding villages, which served it in place of suburbs. Its commerce consisted in cotton manufactures, in gems, and in earthenware, and the jewelers and potters of Cholula were very famous. As for religion, it may be said that Cholula was the Rome of Anahuac. The celebrated Quetzalcoatl’s having stayed so many years in that city, and his having so greatly favored its citizens, brought it about that after the apotheosis of this god a special worship was dedicated to him there. The astonishing multitude of temples that it had, and above all the great temple, erected upon a mountain made by hand, which still survives, drew to that place, held to be holy, endless pilgrims, not only from the neighboring cities but even from the more distant provinces.

Cortés was lodged with all his troops in certain large houses, where for the first two days they were abundantly supplied with provisions; but soon these began to run short, until nothing was furnished them but water and wood. This was not the only proof of their malign disposition,

H2                       for [p. 60] for at every moment new signs were observed of the treachery they were Bk. VIII.

plotting. The Cempoalan allies observed that in the streets of the city there

were some pits, in which they had planted sharp stakes and covered them

with earth, which seemed made for no other purpose than to disable the

horses. Eight men, moreover, who came from the camp of the Tlaxcalans,

warned him that they had seen the city emptied of women and children: a

sure sign among those nations of some imminent war. Besides, it was known

that in some streets they had built entrenchments, and had made great heaps

of stones on the flat roofs of the houses. Finally, a Cholulan lady, who had fall‐

en in love with the beauty, the wit, and the discretion of Doña Marina,

begged her to save herself in her house from the danger that threatened the

Spaniards; whereupon Doña Marina had occasion to learn the whole con‐

trivance of the conspiracy, and at once informed Cortés. He learned by word

of mouth from the same Cholulan lady that her fellow citizens had agreed to

make a general slaughter of the Spaniards with the help of twenty thousand

Mexicans (*) who were encamped near the city. Not content with these re‐

ports, he charged Doña Marina to use all her arts to bring to his lodging two

priests, who confirmed everything that the lady had declared.

Seeing himself in so grave a risk of perishing, Cortés resolved to employ

the most suitable means to save himself. He had the principal persons of the

city come into his presence, and told them that if they had any complaint

against the Spaniards they should state it frankly, as befitted honorable men,

and he would give them fitting satisfaction. They replied that they were well

satisfied with his conduct and ready to serve him; that whenever he deter‐

mined on his departure, he would be abundantly

provided

(*) Bernal Díaz says that the Mexican army, so far as was known, was twenty thousand men.

Cortés affirms that the very lords of Cholula confessed to him that this army was no fewer

than fifty thousand men. [p. 61] provided with everything he might need for the journey, and with men of war besides for his security. Cortés accepted their offer, and Bk. VIII. fixed his departure for the following day. The Cholulans went away content, because it seemed to them that everything was going well for the happy success of their treachery; and to make it more sure, they sacrificed to their gods, so they say, ten children, five of each sex. Cortés assembled his captains, disclosed to them the perverse designs of the Cholulans, and bade them state their opinion. Some wished that the danger be avoided by withdrawing to the city of Huexotzinco, barely nine miles from Cholula, or else to Tlaxcala; but the greater part left the matter to the determination of their general. Cortés gave the orders that seemed to him most suited to his purpose, protesting that he would never think himself safe in Mexico without leaving that perfidious city well punished. He ordered the auxiliary troops of Tlaxcala to fall upon the city the next day at sunrise, putting to the sword all the citizens, sparing none, save only the women and children.

At last came that day so ill-fated for Cholula. The Spaniards made § 27. ready their horses, their artillery, and their arms, and drew up in a The       massacre at

Cholula. great courtyard of their lodging, which was to be the principal theater of that tragedy. The Cholulans arrived there at daybreak. The lords, with as many as forty nobles, and the porters entered the halls and the rooms to take out the baggage, and guards were at once posted there, that they might not escape. The Cholulan troops entered (at least in large part) the courtyard together with the principal lord of that city, at the request, as is to be believed, of Cortés himself, who, mounting his horse, spoke to them in this manner. “I, gentlemen, have striven to make you my friends; I entered your city peacefully, and here you have suffered no wrong from me or from any of mine; indeed, that you might have nothing to complain of, I did not permit the auxiliary troops of the Tlaxcalans to enter. Besides, I have

begged [p. 62] begged you to tell me frankly whether we have done you any injury, in Bk. VIII.

order to give you satisfaction; but you, with detestable perfidy, have

contrived under the semblance of friendship the most cruel treachery,

to make me perish with all my men: I am ignorant of nothing of your

malign designs.” And calling aside four or five Cholulans, he asked

them what reason they had had for resolving upon so execrable an at‐

tempt. They replied that the Mexican ambassadors, to please their

sovereign, had induced them to plot his death. Cortés then, with a

countenance full of fire, spoke thus to the ambassadors who were

present: “These wicked men, to excuse their crime, blame for the

treachery both you and your king; but I neither believe you capable

of such villainy, nor can I persuade myself that the great monarch

Moctezuma would wish to play the cruel enemy toward me at the

very time when he gives me the most weighty proofs of his friend‐

ship, or that, being able to oppose my claims by open force, he would

avail himself of traitors to thwart them. Rest assured that I shall have

your persons respected in the massacre that we are about to carry

out. Today these traitors will perish, and their city will be thrown into

ruin. I call Heaven and Earth to witness that it is their perfidy that

arms our hands for a vengeance so contrary to our inclination.”

That said, and the signal to attack having been given by the firing

of a musket, the Spaniards hurled themselves with such fury upon

those wretched victims that of all those who were in the courtyard,

although they were many, not one was left alive. The streams of blood

that ran through that courtyard, and the piteous laments of the dying,

would suffice to cause horror and pity in any heart that was not dri‐

ven on by the fury of vengeance. Having no more to do within the

house, they went out into the streets, bloodying their swords with the

same severity on as many Cholulans as presented themselves to them.

The Tlaxcalans for their part entered the city like hungry lions, reviv‐

ing their ferocity with the hatred

of [p. 63] of their enemies, and with the desire to please their new allies. A blow so fierce and so unexpected at once threw the citizens into dis‐ Bk. VIII. order; but having gathered in several squadrons they made for some time a vigorous resistance, until, seeing the slaughter that the artillery was working among them, and recognizing the superiority of European arms, they were again thrown into confusion. The greater part of them sought escape in flight; some resorted to the superstition of scraping the walls of the temple in order to flood the city; but finding such effort wholly useless, they tried to fortify themselves in the houses and in the temples. Even this was of no avail; for behold, their enemies set fire to all the buildings where they meet with any resistance. The houses burn, and the towers of the temples: nothing is seen in the streets but bloodied or half-burned corpses, nor is anything heard but the insulting and threatening clamors of the confederates, the feeble sighs of the dying, imprecations against the victors, and laments to their gods for having abandoned them in so great a calamity. Of the many who took refuge in the towers of the temples there was but one who surrendered to the victors: all the others either perished, burned to ashes, or procured a less painful death by throwing themselves down from that height.

By this horrible massacre (F), in which there perished

more

(F) In the writings of Monsignor de las Casas this event at Cholula is read very much distorted. It is true that the vengeance was too rigorous and the massacre made there horrible; but the Spaniards did not lack, for punishing the Cholulans, those reasons that we have adduced, and this prelate makes no mention of any, nor did those odious circumstances attend it which he recounts, and which are not found in any ancient historian. To make us believe that the Spaniards carried out that massacre merely out of caprice, and that while the soldiers made rivers of blood flow the general cheerfully sang I know not what verses, it would be necessary at least that the same prelate testify to it as an eyewitness, or that he allege such documents as would suffice to efface that idea of Cortés which all who knew him give us, and make probable what is wholly incredible. But Monsignor de las Casas was neither present, nor does he adduce proofs sufficient to merit our belief. Without due caution he availed himself of some information given him by one of the many enemies of Cortés. I am no panegyrist of this Conqueror, to excuse [p. 64] more than six thousand Cholulans, the city was left depopulated. The

Bk. VIII. temples and the houses were sacked, the Spaniards seizing the gems,

the gold, and the silver, and the Tlaxcalans the clothing, the feathers,

and the salt. Scarcely was the tragedy of Cholula ended when there

appeared there an army of twenty thousand men, sent to the rescue

by the republic of Tlaxcala under the command of General

Xicotencatl. It is to be believed that this was the effect of some no‐

tice dispatched the night before to that senate by the chiefs of the

Tlaxcalan troops who were encamped outside the city. Cortés wel‐

comed the assistance, made a gift to Xicotencatl and to his captains

of a part of the booty, and begged him to return with his army to

Tlaxcala, since it was no longer necessary, and kept with him those six

thousand men who had helped him in the punishment of Cholula,

that they might accompany him on the journey to Mexico. Thus the

alliance of the Spaniards with the Tlaxcalans was further

consolidated.

§ 28.

Submission of the      Cortés having returned to his lodging, where forty Cholulans of Cholulans and of the the first nobility had remained as prisoners, these begged him to people of Tepeyacac

to the crown of make room for clemency amid so much rigor, and to permit one or

Spain.

two of them to go and recall the women, the children, and the other

fugitives, who were wandering dismayed and full of terror among the

mountains. Cortés, moved to pity, caused the fury of arms to cease,

and a general pardon to be proclaimed. On the announcement of this

decree there were seen to rise at once from among the slain some

who had feigned death in order to escape it, and troops of fugitives

came toward the city, weeping, some for the death of a son or a

brother, some for that of a husband. Cortés had the corpses, which by

now were beginning to stink, removed from the temples and the

streets, and set the noble prisoners at liberty.

his faults; but I am a historian, I am a man, and I am a Christian, and therefore I will

not affirm what I do not believe, nor believe so great a wickedness in an individual of

my species without grave grounds. I describe the event at Cholula as I find it in the

most truthful historians, who were present or informed themselves as well from the

ancient Spaniards as from the Indians.

[p. 65]

Within a few days that city was so well populated again that not one of its inhabitants seemed to be missing. There Cortés received the Bk. VIII. compliments of the Huexotzincas and of the Tlaxcalans, and the oath of fidelity to the Crown of Spain from the Cholulans themselves and from the people of Tepeyacac; he settled the differences that existed between the two republics of Tlaxcala and Cholula, and restored their ancient alliance and friendship, which from then on never failed. Finally, to satisfy the duties of humanity and of religion, he had the cages in the temples broken open, and set at liberty all the prisoners and slaves who were destined for sacrifice. He also had the great temple cleaned, and raised upon it the standard of the cross, after having given the Cholulans, as he did to all the other peoples among whom he stayed, some idea of the Christian religion.

Elated by such happy successes, or perhaps eager to frighten §New    29.

embassy and Moctezuma, the Spanish general charged the Mexican ambassadors gift from the King of to tell their lord that, whereas he had formerly wished to enter Mexico. Mexico peacefully, now, seeing what had happened to him at Cholula, he had determined to enter as an enemy and do him as much harm as he could. The ambassadors replied that before taking such a resolution he should make more diligent inquiries into the affair of the Cholulans, to be certified of the good intentions of their sovereign; that if it seemed good to him, one of them would go to the court to present to the king his complaints. Cortés consented, and after six days the ambassador returned, bringing to the general a great gift consisting of ten dishes of gold, which were worth five thousand sequins, of fifteen hundred garments, and of a great abundance of provisions, thanking him in the name of his sovereign for the punishment inflicted on the perfidious Cholulans, and protesting that the army raised to surprise the Spaniards on the road belonged to the people of Acatzinco and of Itzocan, allies of Cholula, who, though subjects of the Crown, had nevertheless taken up arms without any order from their sovereign. This was confirmed by the oath of the ambassadors,

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.       I                    and [p. 66] and Cortés made a show of remaining fully satisfied. It is not easy to

Bk. VIII.

clear up the truth in this particular, nor can we refrain from blaming

the levity of our authors in affirming so confidently what they alto‐

gether did not know. Why should credence be given to the Cholulans,

men double-dealing and dissembling, as all confess, and not to the

Mexicans, and to Moctezuma himself, who by the eminence of his

character was more worthy of trust? The constantly peaceful conduct

of that monarch toward the Spaniards, in that he did them no harm

on so many and such fine occasions as he had to overwhelm them,

and the moderation with which he always spoke of them (which the

historians themselves do not deny), make the excuse of the Cholulans

improbable; but on the other hand certain indications, though ob‐

scure, of Moctezuma’s displeasure, and above all the hostilities com‐

mitted at this same time on the garrison of Veracruz by a powerful

feudatory of the Crown of Mexico, give it some appearance of truth.

§ 30. Uprisings in     Cuauhpopoca, (*) lord of Nauhtlan (called by the Spaniards Almería), Totonacapan. a city situated on the coast of the Mexican gulf, thirty-six miles north

of Veracruz and near the borders of the Mexican empire on that side,

had orders from Moctezuma to reduce the Totonacs to due obedi‐

ence as soon as Cortés should withdraw from that coast. In order to

do his duty he demanded with threats from those peoples the tribute

which they were bound to pay to their sovereign. The Totonacs,

grown insolent through the favor of their new allies, replied arrogant‐

ly that they owed no homage to one who was no longer their king.

Now Cuauhpopoca, seeing that his demands availed nothing to

restore to subordination men who had too much confidence in their

allies and no respect for their sovereign, put himself at the head of

the Mexican troops that were in the garrison of those frontiers, and

began to make raids through

the

(*) Bernal Díaz calls him Quetzalpopoca, which is also a Mexican name. [p. 67] the districts of Totonacapan, chastising their rebellion with arms. The Totonacs carried their complaints to Juan de Escalante, governor Bk. VIII. of the garrison of Veracruz, and begged him to oppose the cruelty of the Mexicans, offering to help him with a good number of troops. Escalante sent to the chief of the Mexicans a courteous embassy to dissuade him from those hostilities, which could not, as he believed, be agreeable to the King of Mexico, who had so committed himself to favoring the Spaniards, protectors of the Totonacs. Cuauhpopoca replied that he knew better than Escalante whether or not the punishment of those rebels was displeasing to his king; that if the Spaniards wished to support them, he with his troops would await them in the plains of Nauhtlan, so that arms might decide their fate. The governor could not endure such a reply; so he marched at once toward the appointed place with two horse and two small cannon, with fifty Spanish foot, and with about ten thousand Totonacs. At the first attack of the Mexicans the Totonacs were at once thrown into confusion, and the greater part took to flight; but in spite of their cowardice the Spaniards continued the fight courageously, doing no little harm to the Mexicans. These, as men who had never experienced the violence of artillery and the manner of fighting of the Europeans, withdrew in fright to the nearby city of Nauhtlan. The Spaniards pressed them furiously, and set fire to some buildings; but this victory cost them the life of the governor, who within three days died of his wounds, and of six or seven soldiers, and of many Totonacs. One of these soldiers, who had a large head and a fierce aspect, was taken prisoner and sent to Mexico by Cuauhpopoca; but having died of his wounds on the road, they brought nothing to Moctezuma but the head, whose appearance caused such horror in that king that he would not allow it to be offered to his gods in any temple of the court.

Cortés received news of these uprisings before

I2                  leaving [p. 68] leaving Cholula; (G) but he would say nothing then, nor reveal the

Bk. VIII.

disquiet it caused him, so as not to discourage his soldiers.

§ 31.   Now, having no more to do in Cholula, he resumed the journey to Journey of the

Spaniards to Mexico with his Spaniards, with six thousand Tlaxcalans, and with Tlalmanalco. some Huexotzinca and Cholulan troops. At Izcalpan, a village of

Huexotzinco, fifteen miles distant from Cholula, the Huexotzinca

lords came again to pay him their compliments, and advised him that

from that place there were two roads to Mexico: one open and well

kept, which led to certain ravines where some ambush of the enemy

was to be feared, and the other recently obstructed and encumbered

with trees purposely cut down, which was also the shorter and the

safer. Cortés profited by the advice, and in spite of the Mexicans had

the obstructions removed from the road, under the pretext that the

difficulty of the way was rather an incentive to the courage of the

Spaniards; and he continued his journey through that great forest of

pines and oaks until he climbed to the summit of a high mountain

called Ithualco, between the two volcanoes Popocatepetl and

Iztaccihuatl, where they found large houses intended for the lodging

of Mexican merchants. Here they became aware of the daring feat of

Captain Diego de Ordaz, who a few days before, to make known to

those peoples the courage of his nation, had climbed, together with

nine other soldiers, almost to the very summit of Popocatepetl, al‐

though he could not observe the mouth, or vent, of that great vol‐

cano, on account of the deep snow that was there, and of the clouds

of smoke and ashes that it hurled from its entrails. (H)

From

(G) All, or almost all, the historians say that the news of those uprisings reached

Cortés when he was in Mexico; but Cortés himself affirms that he had it in Cholula.

(H) Bernal Díaz, and almost all the historians, say that Ordaz climbed to the sum‐

mit of Popocatepetl and observed the mouth of that famous mountain; but Cortés,

who knew it better, says the opposite. Nevertheless Ordaz obtained from the

Catholic King the right to put a volcano on his coat of arms. This great [p. 69] From the summit of Ithualco the Spaniards observed the most beau‐

Bk. VIII. tiful valley of Mexico, but with very different feelings; for some took delight in the sight of its lakes, its pleasant plains, its verdant mountains, and the many beautiful cities that were in and around those lakes; in others there revived the hope of enriching themselves with the spoils of so great an extent of country as was seen from there; but some more prudent men, looking upon so great a population, thought it rashness to face such grave risks, so that they were seized by such fear that they would have returned from there to Veracruz, if Cortés, making use of his authority and of the reasons suggested to him by his good judgment, had not encouraged them to the enterprise.

Meanwhile Moctezuma, dismayed by the event at Cholula, retired to the palace Tlillancalmecatl, set apart for the time of mourning, and stayed there eight days, fasting and practicing his usual austerities to obtain the protection of his gods. From this same retreat he sent to Cortés four personages of his court with a gift, and with fresh entreaties and pretexts to turn him aside from the journey, offering to pay a certain tribute annually to the King of Spain, and to give the Spanish general four loads of gold, (*) and one to each of his captains and soldiers, if from the place where they then were they would return to their own country. So great was the horror that the Spaniards caused in that superstitious prince! He would not have made more anxious efforts to avoid their sight, had he foreseen all the harm they were to do him. The ambassadors reached Cortés at Ithualco:

enterprise was reserved for Montagno and other Spaniards, who after the conquest of Mexico not only observed the fearful mouth of that volcano, but even entered it, not without evident peril of their lives, and drew from it a great quantity of sulfur to make powder for firearms.

(*) Since the ordinary load of a Mexican is fifty Spanish pounds, or eight hundred ounces, we may conjecture, given the number of the Spaniards, that what Moctezuma wished to give them to turn them aside from the journey to the court was more than three million sequins. [p. 70] the gift they brought consisted of several works of gold, which were Bk. VIII.

worth fifteen hundred sequins. Cortés paid them every possible cour‐

tesy, and replied, thanking the king for his gift and for his magnificent

promises, to which he hoped to respond with good services; but at

the same time protesting that he could not turn back without making

himself guilty of disobedience to his sovereign, and promising to do

the state not the slightest harm by his coming: that if, after he had set

forth by word of mouth to His Majesty the embassy he bore, and

which he could not entrust to any other person, he should judge that

the sojourn of the Spaniards at the court was not conducive to the

good of his kingdom, he would without delay take the road again to

return to his own country.

Moctezuma’s disquiet increased through the suggestions of the

priests, and especially through the report they gave him of certain or‐

acles of their lying gods, and of I know not what frightful visions

which they said they had had in those days. This put his mind in such

consternation that, without awaiting the outcome of the last embassy

sent to the Spaniards, he held council again with the King of

Texcoco, with his brother Cuitláhuac, and with the other personages

whom he was accustomed to consult, all of whom held to their first

opinion: Cuitláhuac in that of not permitting the Spaniards to enter

the court, and of making them leave the kingdom, willingly or by

force, and Cacamatzin in that of receiving them as ambassadors, since

the King of Mexico did not lack forces to repress them, should they

plot anything against the royal person or against the state.

Moctezuma, who until then had always adhered to the opinion of his

brother, now embraced that of the King of Texcoco, but at the same

time charged this same king to go to meet the Spaniards and to en‐

deavor to dissuade the general from the journey to the court. Then

Cuitláhuac, turning to the king his brother, said to him, “May the

gods grant, lord, that you not receive into your house one who will

drive you out of it, and that when you wish to remedy the evil you

may have time and means to

do [p. 71] do so.” “What are we to do,” replied the king, “when our friends, and

Bk. VIII. what is more, our very gods, instead of favoring us, make our enemies prosper? I am now resolved, and I could wish that all would resolve not to flee, nor to show any cowardice, come what may; but I pity the old men and the children, who have no strength to defend themselves.”

Cortés, having dismissed the Mexican ambassadors, set out with his troops from Ithualco, and took the road by Amaquemecan and Tlalmanalco, two cities about nine miles apart from each other, situated on the slopes of those great mountains. Amaquemecan, together with the neighboring hamlets, contained twenty thousand inhabitants. (I) In these places the Spaniards were well received, and several lords of that province visited Cortés and presented him with a certain quantity of gold and some slave women: they complained bitterly of the vexations they suffered from the King of Mexico and his ministers, in the same terms in which those of Cempoala and of Chiahuitztla had already done, and at the suggestion of the Cempoalans and of the Tlaxcalans who accompanied Cortés, confederated with the Spaniards for the sake of liberty. So that the farther the Spaniards advanced into that country, the more their forces went on increasing, like a stream which, with the waters it receives in its course, goes on swelling until it becomes a great river.

From Tlalmanalco the army marched to Ayotzinco, a village situated on the southern shore of Lake Chalco, (L) where was the port for the merchant boats which traded

with

(I) Amaquemecan, called by the Spaniards Mecameca, is at present a village renowned for no other reason than that there was born there the most celebrated nun Juana Inés de la Cruz, a woman of prodigious intelligence and of no common learning.

(L) Solís confuses Amaquemecan with Ayotzinco. Amaquemecan was never situated, as he says, on the shore of the lake, but more than twelve miles from it on the slope of a mountain. The visit of the King of Texcoco was without doubt at Ayotzinco, as the historians affirm who were well informed, [p. 72] with the countries that lie south of Mexico. Curiosity to observe the

Bk. VIII. quarters of the Spaniards cost several Mexicans dear; for the Spanish

sentinels, taking them for spies, through the fear that they still had of

some treachery, killed about fifteen of them that night. The next day,

§ 32. Visit of the King of when they were ready to march, four Mexican nobles arrived with the Texcoco to Cortés.

news that the King of Texcoco was coming to visit the Spanish gener‐

al in the name of the King of Mexico, his uncle. Not long after, the

king himself arrived, carried in a litter adorned with handsome feath‐

ers on the shoulders of four of his servants, and followed by a numer‐

ous and brilliant retinue of Mexican and Texcocan nobility. As soon as

he came in sight of the Spanish general he alighted from the litter,

and began to walk on foot, preceded by some of his servants, who

went along removing from the road everything that might offend his

eyes or his feet. The Spaniards were amazed at such grandeur, and

from it conjectured how great that of the King of Mexico would be.

Cortés came out to meet him at the door of the lodging, and greeted

him with a profound bow, to which the king responded by touching

the ground with his right hand and then carrying it to his mouth. He

entered with a lordly and majestic air one of the halls, and having sat

down, congratulated that general and his captains on their happy ar‐

rival, and professed the great desire which the King of Mexico, his

uncle, had to form a friendship and to have good relations with the

monarch of the East, by whom they had been sent to that country;

but at the same time he exaggerated the difficulties that would have

to be overcome in order to go to the court, and begged Cortés to

change his purpose, if he wished to please the king. Cortés replied

that if he were ever to turn back without delivering his embassy, he

would fail in his duty, and would give great displeasure to his sov‐

ereign, who had sent him

and

and it is plainly seen from the description of Cortés, and no one before Solís has said

that it took place at Amaquemecan. Bernal Díaz says that this visit took place at

Iztapalatenco; but this is a manifest mistake caused by a lapse of memory. [p. 73] and all the more since he found himself now so near to the court, af‐

Bk. VIII. ter having overcome the perils of so long a journey. If it is so, then, said the king, we shall meet at the court: and taking courteous leave, after having been presented with some European trinkets, he left behind a part of his nobility to accompany Cortés on the journey.

From Ayotzinco the Spaniards marched to Cuitláhuac, a city founded on a small island in Lake Chalco, which, although small, was nevertheless, according to Cortés, the most beautiful he had seen until then. This city communicated with the mainland by two broad and convenient causeways built on the lake: one to the south, two miles long, and the other to the north, which was more than two miles in length. The Spaniards went along very cheerful at seeing the multitude and the beauty of the cities that were on the lake, the temples and the towers that rose above the other buildings, the groves that adorned the inhabited places, the kitchen gardens and gardens floating on the waters, and the innumerable boats that plied them, but no less fearful at seeing themselves surrounded by an immense throng of people who came from all those places to observe them: whereupon Cortés commanded his men to go well ordered and ready, and warned the Indians not to encumber the road, nor approach the files, if they did not wish to be treated as enemies. In Cuitláhuac they were well lodged and given gifts. The lord of that city complained secretly to Cortés of the tyranny of the King of Mexico, confederated with him, and made known to him the convenience of the road for going to the court, and the consternation into which the oracles of the gods, the phenomena of the sky, and the success of the Spanish arms had thrown Moctezuma.                                                            § 33.

From Cuitláhuac they set out by the other causeway toward Visit of the princes of

Texcoco, and entry of Iztapalapa; but on the way Cortés was detained by a new piece of the Spaniards into good fortune. Prince Ixtlilxochitl, seeing that Cortés had not wished that court. to make the journey by way of Calpulalpan, where he was awaiting him, resolved to come to meet him on the road to Iztapalapa. He marched, however, with a good number of troops

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.           K                       and [p. 74] passed near Texcoco. Aware of this, Prince Coanacochtzin, his brothBk. VIII.

er, who after those quarrels that took place three years earlier, of

which we have made mention, was still living estranged from him, ei‐

ther impelled by fraternal love or moved by the hope of greater ad‐

vantages that he would have by uniting his interests with those of his

brother, came to meet him on the road. There they communicated

their sentiments to each other, were reconciled, and united in order

to confederate with the Spaniards. They went on together as far as

Iztapalatenco, where they joined those strangers. Cortés, seeing so

many armed troops coming, felt some uneasiness; but when he was

informed of the quality of the personages who were coming to find

him, and of the motive of their coming, he went out to meet them,

and, the due compliments having been paid on both sides, both

princes invited Cortés to betake himself to the court of Texcoco, and

he allowed himself to be easily persuaded by the great advantage he

hoped to derive from Prince Ixtlilxochitl, whose attachment to the

Spaniards had already been made sufficiently evident.

Texcoco was at that time, although somewhat inferior to Mexico

in magnificence and splendor, the largest and most populous city of

the country of Anahuac. Its population, including that of the cities of

Huexotla, Coatlichan, and Atenco (which, being contiguous to it,

might be reckoned its suburbs), was, according to Torquemada, a

hundred and forty thousand houses. To the Spaniards it seemed twice

as large as Seville. The grandeur of the temples and of the royal

palaces, the beauty of the streets, the fountains, and the gardens fur‐

nished abundant matter for their admiration.

Cortés entered this great city (M), accompanied

by

(M) Neither Cortés, nor Bernal Díaz, nor Acosta, nor Gómara, nor Torquemada

makes mention of the entry of the Spaniards into Texcoco; but it is clearly perceived

from a passage in the letter written by Cortés to Charles V in the year 1522. Herrera

and Solís also make mention of it, but intermingle some circumstances contrary to

the truth. They say that the Spaniards went first to Texcoco, and then to Cuitláhuac,

in which they show themselves to have been ignorant of the situation [p. 75] by the two princes and by many Acolhua nobles, amid an immense concourse of people. He and his whole army were lodged in the prin‐ Bk. VIII. cipal palace of the king, where the treatment of his person was equal to the lodging. Here Prince Ixtlilxochitl set before him his claimed rights to the kingdom of Acolhuacan, and his complaints against his brother Cacamatzin and against the King of Mexico, his uncle. Cortés promised to put him in possession of the crown as soon as he should have concluded his negotiations in Mexico, and, without lingering at that court, marched toward Iztapalapa. (N)

Iztapalapa was a large and beautiful city, situated toward the point

§ 34. of that small peninsula which lies between the two lakes, that of Entry of the Chalco to the south and that of Texcoco to the north. From this Spaniards into

Iztapalapa. peninsula one went to the islet of Mexico by a paved causeway more than seven miles long, which had been built on the lake many years before. The population of Iztapalapa was then more than twelve thousand houses, built for the most part on a number of small islands very near one another and contiguous to the same peninsula, near which there were innumerable floating kitchen gardens and gardens. The city was then governed by Prince Cuitláhuac, brother of Moctezuma and his immediate successor on the throne of Mexico, who together with his other brother Matlatzincatzin, lord of the city of Coyoacán, received Cortés with the same demonstrations as had already been used by the other lords through whose places he had passed. He addressed him with a speech well

K2                       devised

of those places. They also assert that Cacamatzin accompanied Cortés to Texcoco; but it is clear to us that this is not true, both from Cortés’s account and from the ancient manuscripts cited by Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl. They say nothing of the reconciliation of those two princes, nor do they state the reason Cortés had for going to Texcoco, turning aside from the road that led to Mexico. In what concerns the circumstances of the journey to Texcoco I adopt the account of Vetancurt, who wrote from the memoirs of Alva and of Sigüenza.

(N) An Indian historian cited by Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl says that on this occasion Prince Ixtlilxochitl was baptized, together with two hundred other nobles of that court; but this is a story so improbable that it needs no refutation. [p. 76] devised, and lodged him and all the troops that accompanied him in Bk. VIII. his own palace. This was a vast edifice of stone and lime, newly built

and not yet furnished. Besides its many halls and rooms convenient

for living in, whose roof was of cedar and whose walls were covered

with handsome cotton hangings, and besides the large courts where

the troops allied with the Spaniards had their quarters, it had a gar‐

den of surprising size and beauty, described by us above, where we

treated of the agriculture of the Mexicans. After dinner the prince led

his guests to this garden, in which they greatly enjoyed themselves,

and there formed a great idea of Mexican magnificence. In this city

the Spaniards observed that, in place of the complaints and murmurs

which they had heard elsewhere, nothing was heard but praise of the

government, because the nearness of the court made people more

cautious in speaking.

The next day, very early, the Spaniards marched along that great

causeway which joined, as we have said, Iztapalapa with Mexico, and

which was cut by seven small canals for the passage of boats from one

lake to the other; over these there were bridges of beams for the con‐

venience of travelers, which were easily raised whenever they wished

to bar the passage to enemies. After passing through Mexicaltzinco,

and seeing Colhuacan, Huitzilopochco, Coyoacán, and Mixcoac,

cities all founded on the shore of the lake, they arrived, amid an in‐

credible throng of people, at a place called Xoloc, where the causeway

from Coyoacán joined this one. In the angle formed by these two

causeways, not more than half a league distant from the capital, there

was a good bulwark with two small towers, surrounded by a wall more

than ten feet high with its crenellated parapet, with two gates and a

drawbridge: a place memorable in the history of Mexico because the

camp of the Spanish general was there during the siege of that great

city. Here the army halted to receive the compliments of more than a

thousand Mexican nobles, all uniformly dressed, who as they passed

before the Spanish general bowed to him with the

usual [p. 77] usual ceremony of touching the ground and kissing the hand.

When these compliments were over, in which they spent more Bk. VIII. than an hour, the Spaniards continued their march as well ordered as § 35. if they were going to give battle. A short time before arriving at the Entry of the

Spaniards into city Cortés received word that the King of Mexico was coming to Mexico, and demonmeet him, and shortly afterward he let himself be seen with a distin‐ strations     of the king

and of the nobility. guished and numerous retinue. Three nobles went before, carrying in their hands gold rods held aloft, as insignia of majesty, by which the people were warned of the presence of the sovereign. Moctezuma came richly dressed, on a litter covered with plates of gold, which four nobles carried on their shoulders, and under an umbrella or sunshade of green feathers adorned with a number of gold ornaments. He wore hanging from his shoulders a mantle adorned with very rich jewels of gold and gems, on his head a thin crown of the same metal, and on his feet soles, likewise of gold, tied with straps of leather studded with gold and gems. Two hundred lords, much better dressed than the other nobles but all barefoot, accompanied him two by two, keeping close on either side to the walls of the houses to show the respect which they bore to their sovereign. As soon as the king and the Spanish general saw each other, both dismounted, the latter from his horse and the former from his litter, and the king began to walk leaning on the arms of the King of Texcoco and of the lord of Iztapalapa. Cortés, after making the king a deep bow, drew near to him to put around his neck a gold cord on which were strung little glass balls that looked like gems, and the king bowed his head to receive it; but when Cortés wished to embrace him, he was not permitted by those two lords, who served as his supporters. (O) The general declared to him in a brief

speech

(O) Solís, in his account of that meeting, makes four mistakes. 1. He says that Cortés’s gift was a band, or a chain of glass. 2. That the two lords who accompanied Moctezuma did not permit Cortés to put the band around his neck. 3. That they did this with some disdain. 4. That they were, however, reproved by the king. All this is false, invented at will, and contrary to the account of Cortés himself. [p. 78] speech, as the circumstances required, his benevolence, his respect, Bk. VIII. and the pleasure he had in having come to know so great a monarch.

Moctezuma answered him in a few words, and having made the usual

ceremony of touching the ground and kissing the hand, he repaid the

gift of the glass beads with two necklaces of fine mother-of-pearl

shells, from which hung some large prawns of gold made true to na‐

ture. He charged Prince Cuitláhuac with conducting Cortés to his

lodging, and himself returned with the King of Texcoco.

Both the nobility and the immense crowd, who from the flat roofs,

doors, and windows of the houses were watching everything that hap‐

pened, were amazed and bewildered no less at the novelty of so many

extraordinary objects presented to their eyes than at the unprece‐

dented condescension of their king, which contributed greatly to en‐

hancing the reputation of the Spaniards. These marched, themselves

full of wonder at the sight of the greatness of the city, the magnifi‐

cence of the buildings, and the multitude of the inhabitants, along

that great and broad street which, without deviating at all from the

straight line, continued that causeway of Iztapalapa built on the lake,

as far as the south gate of the great temple, while in their minds there

alternated with admiration fear for their fate, seeing themselves so

few in the center of a foreign kingdom. Thus they walked a mile and a

half into the city to the palace that had once been King Axayacatl’s,

not far from the west gate of the said temple, which was assigned to

them as their lodging. There Moctezuma was awaiting them, having

however gone on ahead. When Cortés arrived at the gate of that

palace, the king took him by the hand and led him into a great hall,

made him sit on a dais similar in form to those of our altars and cov‐

ered with a handsome cotton rug, beside a wall likewise covered with

a rug adorned with gold and gems, and, taking courteous leave, said

to him: “You and your companions are now in your own house: dine

and rest, for I shall shortly return.”

The king went off to his palace; and Cortés at once caused

a [p. 79] a salvo of all the artillery to be fired, to frighten the Mexicans with

Bk. VIII. such a noise. Meanwhile he went to see all the rooms of the palace in order to assign quarters to his men. This building was so large that both the Spaniards and their allies were comfortably lodged in it, who together with the women and the servants whom they brought with them numbered more than seven thousand. Everywhere there was great cleanliness: almost all the rooms had beds of rush and palm mats, according to their custom, with other mats rolled up for pillows, and under canopies of cotton, and seats made of a single piece. Some rooms had the floor covered with mats, and the walls likewise covered with cotton hangings of various colors. The walls were very thick, and at certain intervals there were some towers. So the Spaniards found there all that they could wish for their security. The diligent and cautious general at once posted the guards, formed a battery of his cannon facing the gate of the palace, and used as much diligence in fortifying himself as if he expected some assault from his enemies that very day. A magnificent dinner, served by the nobility, was set out that day for Cortés and his captains, and for the rest of the army various and plentiful dishes were brought, though of inferior quality. This day, so memorable no less for the Spaniards than for the Mexicans, was November 8, 1519, seven months after the Spaniards had arrived in the country of Anahuac.

Book

[p. 80]

## BOOK IX

Conferences of King Moctezuma with the Spanish general. Imprisonment of

the king of Mexico, of the king of Acolhuacan, and of other lords. The cruel ex‐

ecution of Cuauhpopoca. Attempts of the governor of Cuba against Cortés, and

defeat of Pánfilo de Narváez. Killing of many nobles, and rising of the

Mexican people against the Spaniards. Death of King Moctezuma. Fights, per‐

ils, and defeat of the Spaniards. Battle of Otompan, and retreat of the

Spaniards into Tlaxcala. Election of King Cuitláhuac. Victory of the

Spaniards at Tepeyacac, at Xalatzinco, at Tecamachalco, and at

Cuauhquechollan. Havoc wrought there by the smallpox. Death of King

Cuitláhuac, and of the princes Maxixcatzin and Cuicuitzcatzin. Election in

Mexico of King Cuauhtémoc.

fter the Spaniards had dined and arranged everything that

A

§ 1. First conference, and new gifts from King               was fitting for their security, the king returned to visit

Moctezuma.                them, accompanied by many nobles. Cortés came to meet

him with his captains, and the two, side by side, entered the principal

hall, where they promptly prepared another dais beside that of the

Spanish general. The king presented to him many curious works of

gold, silver, and feathers, and more than five thousand very fine cot‐

ton garments. Having sat down, he had Cortés sit as well, all the oth‐

ers remaining on their feet. Cortés expressed to him his gratitude in

grand terms, and, wishing to continue his speech, was interrupted by

Moctezuma with these words.

“Valiant general, and you his companions, all my domestics and

courtiers are witnesses of the pleasure which I have had at your hap‐

py arrival at this court: and if until now I had made a show of reluc‐

tance, it was only

to [p. 81] to humor my subjects. Your fame has magnified things and disturbed

Bk. IX. minds. It was said that you were immortal gods; that you came mounted on beasts of portentous size and ferocity, and that you wielded thunderbolts with which you made the whole earth tremble. Others spread the report that you were monsters cast up by the sea; that the insatiable hunger for gold had made you abandon your country; that you were too much given to delights, and such gluttons that one of you ate as much as ten of our own people. But all these errors have vanished through the acquaintance that my subjects have had with you. It is now known that you are mortal men like us, although somewhat different in color and in beard. We have already seen with our own eyes that those beasts, so renowned, are nothing but deer more corpulent than ours, and that your so-called thunderbolts are nothing but a better-contrived kind of blowpipe, whose balls are driven with greater noise and do greater harm. As for your personal qualities, we are well informed by those who have had dealings with you that you are kindly and generous, that you bear hardships patiently, that you use severity only against those who provoke your indignation by their hostilities, and that you use arms only for the just defense of your persons.”

“I have no doubt that you likewise will already have driven from your minds, or will soon drive out, those false ideas which the flattery of my vassals or the malevolence of my enemies has made you conceive. There will also be someone who has told you that I am one of the gods, and that I take at my pleasure the form of a lion, or a tiger, or some other animal; but you see already (here, as he said this, he took the skin of his arm between his fingers) that I am of flesh and bone like other mortals, though more noble by my birth and more powerful by the height of my dignity. The Cempoalans, who with your protection have withdrawn from my obedience (but their rebellion will not remain unpunished), will have made you believe that the walls and the roofs

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.        L                       of [p. 82] of my palaces are of gold, but your own eyes can give them the lie. Bk. IX.

This is one of my palaces, and you see already that the walls are of

stone and lime, and the roofs of wood. I shall not deny that my riches

are great; but the exaggerations of my subjects make them greater.

Some will have complained to you of my cruelty and tyranny; but

they call tyranny the legitimate use of supreme authority, and they

call cruelty the necessary severity of justice.”

“Having set aside, then, both on our side and on yours, every dis‐

advantageous idea caused by false information, I accept the embassy

of that great king who sends you, I hold his friendship dear, and I of‐

fer to his obedience my whole kingdom; since, considering the signs

which we have observed in the heavens, and what we see in you, it

seems to us that the time has now arrived for the oracles of our an‐

cestors to be fulfilled, namely, that there should come from the east

certain men, different from us in dress and in customs, who should be

lords of all these countries; for we are not natives of this land: it is

not many years since our ancestors came here from the northern re‐

gions, and we have not until now ruled these peoples otherwise than

as lieutenants of Quetzalcoatl, our god and lawful lord.”

Cortés replied, thanking him greatly for the singular favors he had

until then received from him, and for the honorable opinion which

he had formed of the Spaniards. He told him that he was sent by the

greatest monarch of Europe, who, although he might aspire to some‐

thing more, being a descendant of Quetzalcoatl, nevertheless was

content to establish a confederation and perpetual friendship with

His Majesty and with his successors; that the purpose of his embassy

was not to take from others what they possessed, but rather to an‐

nounce to them the true religion, and to communicate to him certain

important counsels for improving the government and making his

vassals happy: which he would do on another occasion, if His

Majesty

[p. 83]

Majesty would deign to hear him. The king agreed to it, and having been informed of the rank and condition of each of the Spaniards, he Bk. IX. took leave, and shortly afterward sent them a great gift, namely, for each of the captains certain works of gold and three bales of precious garments of feathers, and for each soldier two bales of cotton garments. This happy beginning might have assured the Spaniards the peaceful possession of that vast monarchy, if they had been guided more by prudence than by courage. (a)

The next day, Cortés, wishing to return the king’s visit, sent to ask § 2. him for an audience, and obtained it so promptly that those who Cortés’s visit to the

king. brought him the answer were the very men who served as introducers of ambassadors, and who were to conduct him and instruct him in the ceremonial of that court. Cortés dressed in the most splendid finery he had, and took with him the captains Alvarado, Sandoval, Velázquez de León, and Ordaz, and five soldiers besides. They went to the royal palace amid an immense crowd of people, and as soon as they reached the first gate, those who accompanied them drew up in two files, one on each side; for to enter it crowded together was reckoned a lack of respect to His Majesty. After passing through three courts and some halls to the last antechamber leading to the audience hall, they were there courteously received by a number of lords who kept guard, and were obliged to remove their shoes and to cover their showy garments with coarse robes. When they entered the audience hall, the king took a few steps toward Cortés, and taking him by the hand, and turning an affectionate glance to the others, had them all sit down. The conversation

L2                         was

(a) The learned and judicious Father Acosta, speaking of this first conference of Moctezuma, in bk. VII, ch. 25 of his History, says thus: “Many are of the opinion that, given the state of things on that first day, it would have been easy for the Spaniards to do with the king and with the whole kingdom whatever they wished, and to communicate to them the law of Jesus Christ with great peace and the contentment of all; but the judgments of God are profound, and many were the sins of both parties; whence it came about otherwise, although in the end the Lord accomplished His design of showing mercy to that nation, after having judged and punished those who in His sight deserved it.” [p. 84] was long, and on diverse subjects. The king put several questions, as Bk. IX.

much on the political government as on the natural things of Spain,

and Cortés, after having satisfied him in everything, cunningly en‐

tered upon matters of religion. He set forth to him the unity of God,

the Incarnation of the divine Word, the creation of the world, the

severity of God’s judgment, the glory with which He rewards the just,

and the eternal pains to which He condemns sinners. Then he spoke

of the rites of Christianity, and in particular of the bloodless sacrifice

of the Mass, in order to compare it with the inhuman sacrifices of the

Mexicans, declaiming strongly against the barbarous cruelty of immo‐

lating human victims and of feeding on their flesh. Moctezuma an‐

swered that on the creation of the world they were in agreement,

since the very thing that Cortés had said they had already heard from

their elders; that for the rest he had already been informed by his am‐

bassadors of the religion of the Spaniards. “For my part,” he added, “I

do not doubt the goodness of the God whom you worship; but if He

is good for Spain, our gods are likewise good for Mexico, as the expe‐

rience of so many centuries has shown us. Spare yourselves, therefore,

the trouble of wishing to induce me to abandon their worship. As for

the sacrifices, I do not know why it should be blamed to sacrifice to

the gods those men who, whether by their own crimes or by their for‐

tune in war, are already destined to death.” But although Cortés did

not succeed in persuading him of the truth of the Christian religion,

he obtained at least, so they say, that human flesh should no longer be

served at the royal table; either because by Cortés’s arguments the

natural loathing for such food was awakened in his mind, or else be‐

cause he wished at least in this to please the Spaniards. He also made

his royal beneficence toward them conspicuous on this occasion, giv‐

ing to Cortés and to the four captains several works of gold and ten

bales of very fine cotton garments, and to each soldier a necklace of

gold.

Cortés having returned to his quarters (so we shall henceforth call

the palace of King Axayacatl, where were lodged

the [p. 85] the Spaniards) he began to reflect on the danger in which he found himself in the center of a city so strong and populous, and resolved to Bk. IX. win the minds of the nobility by good conduct and by courteous and affectionate manners, and ordered his men to behave in such a way that the Mexicans could have no complaint of them; but while he seemed to attend with the greatest diligence to peace, he was turning over in his mind bold and rash designs, entirely contrary to peace, and, since to ripen them it was necessary for him first to inform himself with his own eyes about the fortifications of Mexico and the forces of the Mexicans, he asked the king’s leave to see the royal palaces, the great temple, and the market square. The king kindly granted it, as one who suspected nothing of the cunning general, nor foresaw the consequences of his excessive indulgence. The Spaniards also saw everything they wished, finding everywhere abundant matter for admiration.

At that time the city of Mexico was situated, as we have already §Description

3.

of the said, on an islet in the lake of Texcoco, fifteen miles west of that city of Mexico. court, and four east of that of Tlacopan. (*) One passed from the mainland to the islet by three great causeways of stone and earth, built expressly on the lake itself: that of Iztapalapa on the south, more than seven miles long, that of Tlacopan on the west, about two miles, and that of Tepeyacac on the north, of three miles (b); and all three so wide that ten horsemen could ride abreast. Besides these three causeways

there

(*) In the geographical map these cities are shown, by mistake, closer to one another.

(b) Dr. Robertson puts in place of the causeway of Tepeyacac that of Texcoco, which, in the place where he describes Mexico, he situates toward the northwest, and where he speaks of the posts of the Spanish army in the siege of that capital, toward the east, having already said that toward the east there was no causeway on the lake; but there was not, nor could there ever be, any causeway on the lake from Mexico to Texcoco, because of the very great depth of its bed in that part, and had there been one, it would not be of three miles only, as this author affirms, but of fifteen, which is the extent of the lake between. [p. 86] there was another somewhat narrower one for the two aqueducts of Bk. IX.

Chapultepec. The circuit of the city, not including the suburbs, was

more than nine miles, and the number of houses at least sixty thou‐

sand. (c) The city was divided into four quarters, and each quarter

into several wards, the Mexican names of which are still preserved

among the Indians. The dividing lines of the four quarters were the

four broad streets corresponding to the four gates of the court of the

great temple. The first quarter, called Tecpan (today San Pablo), com‐

prised all that part which lay between the two streets corresponding

to the south and east gates. The second, Mojotla (today San Juan), be‐

tween the south and west streets. The third, Tlaquechiuhcan (today

Santa María), between the west and north streets, and the fourth,

Atzacualco (today San Sebastián), between the north and east streets.

To these four parts, into which that city was divided from its very

foundation, there was added, as a fifth part, the city of Tlatelolco, sit‐

uated to the northwest, which after the conquest of King Axayacatl

remained united to that of Tenochtitlan, and the two together made

up that of Mexico.

There

(c) Torquemada affirms that the population of the capital was 120,000 houses; but

the Anonymous Conqueror, Gómara, Herrera, and other historians agree on the

number of 60,000 houses, not of 60,000 inhabitants, as Robertson says; since there

was no ancient author who estimated it so small. It is true that in the Italian transla‐

tion of the account of the Anonymous Conqueror one reads sixty thousand inhabitants;

but this was without doubt a mistake of the translator, who, having perhaps found in

the original sesenta mil vecinos, translated 60,000 inhabitants, when he should have

said 60,000 hearths; for otherwise it would have to be said that Cholula, Xochimilco,

Iztapalapa, and other such cities were larger than that of Mexico. But in the afore‐

said number the houses of the suburbs are not included. We know from the testimo‐

ny of Herrera and of Bernal Díaz that toward the west the houses continued on one

side and the other of the causeway of Tlacopan all the way to the mainland, that is,

for a space of 2 miles. Toward the southwest there was the suburb of Aztacalco, to‐

ward the south those of Acatlan, Malcuitlapilco, Atenco, and Iztacalco, and toward

the northwest Zancopinca, Huitznahuac, Xocotitlan, Coltonco, and others. It is to

be believed that Torquemada meant to count the houses of the suburbs as well; but

for all that the number of 120,000 houses seems to me excessive.

[p. 87]

There were around the city many dikes and sluices to hold back the waters when need required, and within it so many canals that there Bk. IX. was hardly a ward where one could not go by boat: which contributed no less to the beautifying of the city, and to making easier the transport of provisions and of all the other goods of their trade, than to making the citizens still more secure against the attempts of their enemies. The principal streets were wide and straight. As to the others, some were mere canals, where one could not go otherwise than in boats, others were paved and without any water, and others had a little canal between two embankments, which served for the convenience of passersby and for the unloading of the boats, or else were little gardens, with trees planted in them, and flowers.

As for the buildings, besides the many temples and the magnificent royal palaces of which we have spoken elsewhere, there were also other palaces or large houses which the feudatory lords had built for their dwelling at the time when they were obliged to reside at court. On top of all the houses, except those of the poor, there were flat roofs with their parapets, and on some even battlements and towers, though much smaller than those of the temples: so that the Mexicans provided for their defense no less in the streets and in the houses than in the temples.

Besides the great and renowned square of Tlatelolco, where the principal market was held, there were other small squares distributed throughout the city, where ordinary provisions were sold. There were also in several places fountains and fishponds, especially near the temples, and many gardens, partly planted at ground level and partly on high flat roofs.

The many large buildings, neatly whitewashed and polished, the high towers of the temples scattered through the quarters of the city, the canals, the avenues of trees, and the gardens formed a whole so beautiful that the Spaniards never seemed to tire of looking at it, especially when they viewed it from the upper court of the great temple, which overlooked not only the habitations of that court but also the lakes, and the fine and large

cities [p. 88] cities that were round about. They were no less amazed at the sight

Bk. IX.

of the royal palaces, and of the surprising variety of plants and ani‐

mals that were kept there; but nothing else filled their minds with

greater astonishment than the great market square. There was not a

Spaniard who did not celebrate it with singular praises, and some of

them, who had traveled through almost all of Europe, declared, as

Bernal Díaz attests, that they had never seen in any square in the

world either so great a number of merchants, or so great a variety of

merchandise, or so fine an order and arrangement in everything.
