§ 4.    When the Spaniards went up on the great temple, they found the Outbursts of Cortés’s

zeal for religion.

king there, who had gone there beforehand in order to prevent by his

presence any attempt that might be made against his gods. After hav‐

ing observed the city from that height, at the urging of the king him‐

self, Cortés asked his permission to see the sanctuaries, and he grant‐

ed it after consulting the priests. The Spaniards entered them, and

contemplated, not without compassion and loathing, the blindness of

those peoples, and the horrible slaughter that the cruel superstition

of their sacrifices wrought among them. Cortés then, turning to the

king, said to him: “I wonder, my lord, that a monarch as wise as you

are should worship as gods these abominable figures of the Devil.”

“Had I known,” replied the king, “that you were to speak with such

contempt of our gods, I should never have yielded to your requests.”

Cortés, seeing him so indignant, asked his pardon, and took leave to

return to the quarters. “Go in peace,” said the king, “for I shall re‐

main here to appease the anger of our gods, provoked by your

blasphemies.”

Despite this displeasure, Cortés obtained from the king not only

permission to build within the enclosure of the quarters a chapel to

the honor of the true God, but also the workmen and the materials

for the building, in which Holy Mass was celebrated as long as there

was wine, and daily there gathered

the [p. 89] the soldiers to perform their devotions. He also planted in the principal court a great cross, so that the Mexicans might see the supreme Bk. IX. veneration in which that holy instrument of our redemption was held among the Spaniards. He wished, moreover, to consecrate to the worship of the true God the very sanctuary of Huitzilopochtli, and was then restrained by respect for the king and the priests; but he carried it out some months afterward, having acquired greater authority through the imprisonment of the king and other actions no less rash, as we shall presently see. He smashed the idols that were venerated there, had the sanctuary cleaned and repaired, placed in it a crucifix and an image of the Mother of God, (d) and, kneeling before these sacred images, thanked the Most High for having granted them to adore Him in that place, so long devoted to the most abominable and cruel idolatry. This same zeal for religion led him to repeat frequently to the king his discourses on the holiest truths of Christianity; but although Moctezuma was not disposed to embrace them, nevertheless, moved by his arguments, he commanded that from then on human victims should no longer be sacrificed; and although he did not comply with the Spanish general in renouncing idolatry, he was ever affectionate toward him, and there was no day on which he did not show the Spaniards new kindnesses and gifts. The order which the king gave to the priests concerning the sacrifices was not constantly observed; and the great harmony that had existed until then was disturbed by the unheard-of attempt of the Spanish general.

Not more than six days had passed since the entry of the Spaniards into Mexico when Cortés, finding himself isolated in

(d) The image of the Mother of God which Cortés placed in the sanctuary of Huitzilopochtli is believed to be the very same one that is now venerated under the title of Our Lady of los Remedios, or of Succor, in a magnificent temple about eight miles distant from the capital toward the west. It is said that a soldier of Cortés named Villafuerte brought it with him to Mexico, and that on the day after that terrible night in which the Spaniards were defeated he hid it in the place where some years later it was found, that is, in that very place where it is now venerated. Bk. IX.

would be in peril if the mind of the king should ever change, as might

§ 5. happen, was persuaded that there was no other counsel to be taken Imprisonment of King Moctezuma. for their security than to seize the very person of the king; but since

such a counsel was so repugnant to reason, which demanded of him

both respect for the majesty of that monarch and gratitude for his

beneficence, he sought pretexts to quiet his conscience and to shelter

his honor, (e) and found none more fitting than that of the uprisings

at Veracruz, the news of which, received already at Cholula, he had

kept in his breast until then; but now wishing to use it, he disclosed it

to his captains, that they might seriously consider what should be

done to escape from such grave perils: and to justify his attempt still

further, and to move his Spaniards to carry it out, he summoned sev‐

eral principal persons among his allies (whose information should

always

(e) That Cortés’s aim was to seize Moctezuma by whatever means, and that the

uprisings at Veracruz were nothing but a mere pretext for putting his design into ef‐

fect, is evident from his letter to Charles V of October 30, 1520. “Six days having

passed, most invincible Prince” (he says in ch. 23 of this letter, or account), “since I

entered the great city of Temistitan (he should have said: Tenochtitlan), and having

seen some things of it, though few, in comparison with what there is to see and note:

from those, and even from what I had seen of the land, it seemed to me that it was

fitting for the royal service, and for our security, that that lord (Moctezuma) should

be in my power, and not in complete freedom; lest he should change the purpose

which he showed of serving Your Highness, the more so as we Spaniards are some‐

what intolerable and importunate, and because, if angered, he could do us much

harm, so much that no memory of us would remain, given his great power; and also

because, having him with me, all the other lands that were subject to him would

more readily come to the knowledge and service of Your Majesty, as afterward hap‐

pened.” Still more plainly he reveals it in ch. 2 of the same letter, citing another writ‐

ten by him to the same king from Veracruz: “I assured Your Highness,” he says, “that

I would have him (Moctezuma) either captured, or dead, or subject to the Royal

Crown of Your Majesty; and with this purpose and this aim I left the city of

Cempoala.” Now when Cortés left Cempoala the uprisings at Veracruz had not yet

occurred, nor had he received any wrong from the king, but rather singular kindness‐

es and the most magnificent gifts. [p. 91] always be suspect to him because of their mortal enmity toward the Mexicans), and asked them whether they had observed any novelty Bk. IX. among the inhabitants of that court. They answered that the Mexican commoners were at that time entertained in the public rejoicings which the king had ordered to celebrate the arrival of such noble strangers; but that among the nobility they noticed a certain suspicious demeanor, and that among other things they had heard them say that it would be easy to remove the bridges from the canals, which seemed to indicate some secret conspiracy against the Spaniards.

Cortés could not sleep that night for anxiety, and spent it all walking about the quarters, lost in thought. A sentry then informed him that in one room there was a door that had been freshly walled up. Cortés had it opened, and on entering found many rooms in which was deposited the treasure of the late King Axayacatl. He saw there many idols, a great quantity of works of silver, gold, gems, feathers, and cotton, and several other things, which the tributary provinces paid to the Crown, or the feudatory lords presented to their sovereign. After observing with amazement so great a wealth, Cortés had the door walled up again, leaving everything then as it was before.

The next morning he assembled his captains, and represented to them the hostilities committed by the lord of Nauhtlan against the garrison of Veracruz and against the Totonacs, their allies, which, according to what the allies themselves said, would not have been committed without the order or permission of the King of Mexico. He set forth with the greatest energy the most grave peril in which they found themselves, and declared to them his design, exaggerating the advantages that were to be expected from its execution, and minimizing the evils that might result from it. Their sentiments were various. Some rejected the general’s counsel as rash and impracticable; and said that it would be better to ask the king’s permission to withdraw from the court; since he who with so much eagerness, and with such great gifts, had sought to dissuade them from the resolution of going to Mexico would readily

M2                  grant [p. 92] grant them leave to depart. Others thought departure indeed necesBk. IX. sary, but believed that it should be done secretly, so as not to give the

Mexicans occasion for some treachery; but the greater part of them,

drawn beforehand by the general himself to his side, as is to be be‐

lieved, consented to his counsel, rejecting the others as ignominious

and more perilous. “What will be said of us,” they said, “seeing us

leave at once a court where we have been loaded with honors? Who

will not be persuaded that it is fear that drives us out? And if once we

lose our reputation for courage, what security can we promise our‐

selves either in those places of the Mexicans through which we shall

necessarily have to pass, or among our allies, who will no longer be re‐

strained by respect for our arms?” The resolution was finally taken to

seize Moctezuma in his palace and take him prisoner to their quar‐

ters: a resolution barbarous and extravagant to excess, suggested by

fear for their fate and by experience of their own good fortune, which

more than anything else encourages men to advance to the most ar‐

duous enterprises, and often sends them headlong.

For the execution of so perilous an attempt Cortés put all his

troops under arms and distributed them in the suitable places. He

commanded five of his captains and twenty-five soldiers, in whom he

most trusted, to proceed to the palace two by two, but in such a man‐

ner that all should arrive at the same time, as if by chance; and he

went there with Doña Marina as interpreter, having first obtained the

king’s consent for that hour at which he was accustomed to visit him.

He was introduced with the other Spaniards into the audience hall,

where the king, very far from foreseeing what was to happen to him,

received them with his usual affability. He had them sit down, gave

them some gifts of gold, and moreover presented to Cortés one of his

daughters. Cortés, after expressing to him his gratitude in the briefest

terms, excused himself from accepting her, alleging that he was mar‐

ried in Cuba, and that according to the law

of [p. 93] of God among the Christians he was not permitted to have two wives; but in the end he admitted her into his company, so as not to Bk. IX. displease the king, and to have an opportunity of making her a Christian, as indeed he obtained. To the other captains the king likewise gave some daughters of Mexican lords from among those that he had in his seraglio. They then conversed for some time on various subjects; but Cortés, seeing that these discourses distracted him from his purpose, said to the king that that visit was directed to informing him of the conduct of the lord of Nauhtlan, his vassal: he complained of the hostilities committed by that lord against the Totonacs on account of their friendship with the Spaniards, of the war made on the Spaniards of Veracruz, and of the death of the governor Escalante and of six soldiers of that garrison. “I must,” he added, “give an account to my sovereign of the death of these Spaniards, and in order to be able to give him a suitable satisfaction, I have made inquiry into so irregular a conduct. All blame you as the principal author of such uprisings; but I am far from imagining such perfidy in so great a monarch as that of persecuting us as an enemy in that province at the same time that he loads us with favors at court.” “I do not doubt,” replied the king, “that those who blame me for the war of Nauhtlan are the Tlaxcalans, my sworn enemies; but I assure you that I had no influence in it. Cuauhpopoca ventured to make it without my order, indeed against my own will: and so that you may be assured of the truth, I shall have him come at once to the court, and shall deliver him into your hands.” He called at once two of his courtiers, and, handing over to them a certain gem in which was carved the image of the god of war, which he wore always hanging from his arm, and which served as a seal for the mark of his commands, ordered them to set out with the greatest possible speed toward Nauhtlan, and then to bring to the court Cuauhpopoca and the other principal persons who had taken part in the death of the Spaniards, and gave them authority to raise troops, and

to [p. 94] to seize them by force, in case they should refuse to obey. Bk. IX.

The two courtiers set out at once to carry out their commission,

and the king said to Cortés: “What more can I do to assure you of my

sincerity?” “I do not doubt it,” replied Cortés; “but to dispel the error

into which even your own vassals have fallen, that the outrage of

Nauhtlan was carried out by your order, an extraordinary demonstra‐

tion is required, which shall make manifest your benevolence toward

us; and no other seems to me more fitting to this end than that of

deigning to come and live with us until the culprits are brought, and

by their confession your innocence is made manifest. This will serve

to satisfy my sovereign, to justify your conduct, and to honor us, and

to put us under cover in the shadow of Your Majesty.” In spite of the

artful words with which Cortés tried to gloss over his bold and injuri‐

ous pretension, the king perceived it at once, and was disturbed.

“Where has it ever been seen,” he said, “that kings let themselves be

led off prisoners? And even though I were willing to degrade my per‐

son and my dignity in such a manner, would not all my vassals at once

take up arms to deliver me? I am not a man who could hide or flee to

the mountains. Without submitting to such an infamy, I am here still

ready to satisfy your complaints.” “The house, my lord,” said Cortés

then, “to which we invite you is one of your own palaces: nor will it

be a wonder to your subjects, accustomed already to seeing you fre‐

quently change your abode, to see you now come to dwell in the

palace of your late father Axayacatl under the pretext of showing us

your benevolence. And if your subjects should dare to do anything

against you or against us, we have courage enough, strong arms, and

good weapons to repress their rashness. For the rest, I pledge you my

word that you will be as much honored by us, and as well served, as

by your own subjects.” The king persevered in his reluctance, and

Cortés in his urgings, until one of the Spanish captains, too bold and

inconsiderate,

taking [p. 95] taking it ill that the execution of their design was being delayed, said

Bk. IX. angrily that they should drop the talk and resolve to drag him off by force, or kill him. The king, who in the Spaniard’s countenance perceived his purpose, asked Doña Marina what that furious stranger was saying. “My lord,” she replied discreetly, “as your subject I desire your welfare, and as the confidante of these men I know their secrets and know their temper. If you deign to do what they require, you will be treated by them with all that honor and distinction which is due to your royal person; but if you persist in your refusal your life is in danger.” That unhappy king, who ever since he had the first news of the arrival of the Spaniards had been overcome by a superstitious fear, and became more pusillanimous from day to day, seeing himself now in such straits, and persuaded that before his guards could arrive to succor him he might already have perished at the hands of those men so bold and resolute, yielded at last to their entreaties. “I will trust you,” he said, “let us go, then, let us go; since the gods so will it”; and he at once ordered that they should prepare his litter, and got into it to be carried to the quarters of the Spaniards.

I do not doubt that the readers will feel, in reading and in considering the circumstances of this extraordinary event, that same distress which I feel in writing it; but it is needful, no less in this than in other events of this history, to raise the mind to Heaven and to revere with the deepest respect the most high counsels of Divine Providence, which took the Spaniards as instruments of its justice and its mercy, chastising in some the superstition and cruelty, and enlightening others with the light of the Gospel. We shall never cease to inculcate this truth, and to make known even in the most irregular actions of creatures the goodness, wisdom, and omnipotence of the Creator.

Moctezuma finally left his palace, never to return

again [p. 96] again. He left, protesting to his courtiers that for certain reasons al‐

Bk. IX.

ready consulted with the gods he was going of his own good will to

live for some days with those strangers, commanding them to make it

so known throughout the court. He was borne with all that train and

magnificence which he was accustomed to display whenever he let

himself be seen in public, the Spaniards walking beside him guarding

him under color of honoring him. The news of this extraordinary

event was at once spread throughout the city, and the people flocked

together in crowds: some, moved to tenderness, wept, and others

threw themselves on the ground like people in despair. The king tried

to calm them, signifying to them the pleasure with which he was go‐

ing to stay among those friends of his; but fearing some great distur‐

bance, he gave orders to his ministers to drive the rabble from the

street, and imposed the penalty of death on anyone who should cause

any disquiet. On arriving at the quarters he greeted warmly the

Spaniards who came to meet him, and took for his dwelling the apart‐

ment which pleased him most, which his servants promptly furnished

with the finest hangings of cotton and of feathers and with the best

furnishings of the royal palace. Cortés posted a guard at the entrance

of that apartment, and doubled those that ordinarily were there for

the security of the quarters. He ordered all the Spaniards and allies to

treat him and serve him with all that respect that was due to His

Majesty, and permitted Mexicans to enter to visit him, as many as

wished, provided they were few at a time: so that nothing of what he

had in his palace was lacking to him except liberty.

Moctezuma gave audience there freely to his vassals, heard their

§ 6. The king’s life in disputes, pronounced sentences, and ruled the kingdom with the help

prison.

of his ministers and councilors. His servants served him with the

same diligence and punctuality that they were accustomed to show. A

troop of nobles, arranged in fours, served him at table, carrying the

dishes aloft for greater display. After choosing what pleased him, he

divided the rest among the Spaniards who attended

him, [p. 97] him, and among the Mexican nobles who served him. Not content

Bk. IX. with this, his generosity made frequent and magnificent gifts to the Spaniards.

Cortés, on his part, showed such zeal that his men should show him the respect that was due to him, that he had a Spanish soldier flogged because he answered him brusquely, and would have had him hanged, as some historians affirm, if the king himself had not interposed in favor of the culprit. But if he was worthy of such a punishment for having failed, by such an answer, in the respect due to the majesty of that king, what punishment did he deserve who had rashly deprived him of his liberty? Whenever Cortés went in to visit him, he showed him the same reverence, and the same compliments, as he was wont to do when he went to the palace. To divert him in prison he had the soldiers perform the exercise of arms, or even had them play games before him, and the king himself sometimes deigned to play with Cortés and with Captain Alvarado at a game which the Spaniards called the bodoque, and showed pleasure in losing for the sake of having occasion to exercise his liberality. Once he lost after dinner forty pieces of unworked gold, that is, as far as we can conjecture, at least a hundred and sixty ounces. Thus easily do those squander their riches who have acquired them without their own labor.

Cortés, seeing his liberality, or rather prodigality, said to him one day that some rascally soldiers had taken from the treasure of his late father Axayacatl certain pieces of gold; but that he would have them put back at once where they had been. “Provided,” said the king, “that they do not touch the images of the gods, nor what is destined for their worship, let them take what they wish.” Having this permission, the Spaniards then drew from it more than a thousand fine cotton garments. Cortés commanded that they be put back; but Moctezuma opposed, saying that he never took back what he had once given. Cortés also had some soldiers imprisoned because they had taken from the same treasure a certain quantity of liquidambar;

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.        N                      but [p. 98] but at the king’s request they were soon set at liberty. Not content

Bk. IX. with granting his riches to the Spaniards, Moctezuma presented to

Cortés another daughter of his, whom this general accepted in order

to marry her to Cristóbal de Olid, master of the camp of the Spanish

troops. This princess, like the other already presented to him, was

promptly instructed and baptized without any objection from their

father.

Cortés, no longer doubting the king’s good will, which had been

made plain no less in the extraordinary demonstrations of liberality

than in the pleasure he showed at being among the Spaniards, allowed

him after some days of imprisonment to leave the quarters, and urged

him to go whenever he wished to amuse himself with hunting, of

which he was exceedingly fond. That humbled monarch did not

refuse this wretched use of the liberty that was granted him: he went

out often, and betook himself now to the temples to perform his de‐

votions, now to the lake to hunt waterfowl, now to the wood of

Chapultepec, or to some other place of recreation, always guarded by

a good number of Spanish soldiers. When he went out on the lake,

very many boats escorted him, or else two brigantines which Cortés

had had built as soon as he entered that court. (*) When he went to

some wood, two thousand Tlaxcalans accompanied him, besides the

numerous retinue of Mexicans that always attended him for his ser‐

vice; but he never spent the night outside the quarters.

§ 7.     More than fifteen days had already passed since the imprisonment Execution of the lord of Moctezuma when the two courtiers who had been sent to

of Nauhtlan, and a Nauhtlan returned, bringing with them Cuauhpopoca, a son of his,

new insult to the

king’s majesty. and fifteen other nobles, accomplices in the death of Captain

Escalante. Cuauhpopoca came richly dressed, on a litter. When he ar‐

rived at the quarters, he took off his shoes according to the ceremoni‐

al of the palace, and covered

himself

(*) In order to set forth the whole of Moctezuma’s life in prison at one time, we

recall here some events much later than those we are about to relate. [p. 99] himself with a coarse garment; he was brought into the king’s audience, and after making the customary ceremonies of respect said to Bk. IX. him: “Behold, most great and powerful lord, your servant, obedient to your orders and ready to fulfill your will in everything.” “You have conducted yourself very ill this time,” the king answered with indignation, “in treating as enemies those strangers whom I have received in a friendly manner at my court, and your rashness was very great in accusing me as the author of such an attempt; you shall therefore be punished as a traitor to your sovereign.” And when Cuauhpopoca wished to excuse himself, he would not listen to him, but had him immediately handed over to Cortés together with his accomplices, so that after examining the crime he might punish them with the penalty that was fitting. Cortés put the due questions to them, and they frankly confessed the deed without at first accusing the king, until, seeing themselves threatened with torments and believing their execution unavoidable, they declared that all they had done had been commanded them by the king, without whose orders they would never have dared to attempt anything whatever against the Spaniards.

Cortés, having heard their confession, and making a show of not believing their excuse, condemned them to be burned alive before the royal palace as guilty of lese majesty. He went at once to the king’s apartment with three or four of his captains and a soldier who carried irons in his hand, and without omitting even this time the usual ceremonies and compliments, said to the king: “The accused have now been examined, lord, and all have confessed their crime, and they accuse you as the author of the death of my Spaniards. I have condemned them to the punishment they deserve, and which you also deserve, in view of their confession; but having regard, on the other hand, to the great benefits which you have done us until now, and to the affection which you have shown toward my sovereign and toward my nation, I wish to grant you the favor of life; but I cannot avoid making you feel a part of the punishment you deserve for your

N2                  crime. [p. 100] crime.” So saying, he angrily ordered the soldier to put the irons on Bk. IX. his feet, and, without wishing to hear anything, turned his back and

went away. So great was the king’s amazement at seeing his person

subjected to such an outrage that it left him neither any movement of

resistance nor any word to express his grief. He remained for a good

while almost senseless. The servants who attended him declared their

grief with silent tears, and, throwing themselves at his feet, lightened

the weight of the irons with their hands, and took pains to spare him

the contact of them by interposing bands of cotton. Having recov‐

ered from his stupor, he broke out into some acts of impatience, but

promptly regained his calm, ascribing his misfortune to the sovereign

ordinance of the gods.

Scarcely had he done this so bold a deed when Cortés passed on to

carry out another undertaking no less rash. After giving orders to the

guards of the quarters not to permit any Mexican to enter to see the

king, he commanded that Cuauhpopoca be led to execution with his

son and the other culprits. The same Spaniards led them, armed and

drawn up in battle order to hold the people in check in case they

should wish to prevent the execution; but what could that little troop

have done against the immense multitude of Mexicans who were to

be spectators of that great event, had not God, who disposed every‐

thing for the execution of his most lofty designs, restrained the ef‐

fects which the unheard-of attempt of those few men ought to have

caused? The fire was kindled before the principal palace of the king.

The wood used for it was a great quantity of bows, arrows, darts,

lances, swords, and shields that were in an armory; for Cortés had

asked it of the king in order to free himself from the disquiet which

the sight of so many weapons caused him. Cuauhpopoca, his feet and

hands bound and himself placed on the wood on which he was to be

burned, protested anew his innocence, and said again that all he had

done had been by the express command of his king; he then made

prayers to his gods,

and [p. 101] and encouraged his companions to suffer death. The fire was lit, and

Bk. IX. in a few minutes they were consumed (f) in the sight of an immense people, who did not stir, because they were persuaded, as is to be believed, that this punishment was being carried out by order of the king; and it is very probable that the sentence was published in his name.

Cortés’s conduct cannot be justified in any way, since, besides arrogating to himself an authority that did not belong to him, if he truly believed that the king had been the author of the uprisings at Veracruz, why condemn to death, and to so bitter a death, men whose only crime was that of punctually carrying out the orders of their sovereign? If he did not believe the king guilty, why subject him to so great an ignominy, setting at naught the respect due to his character, the gratitude that was fitting toward his beneficence, and the immunity that was owed to his innocence? I conjecture that Cuauhpopoca had received from the king the precise order to bring the Totonacs back under obedience to that crown; and, unable to carry out such an order without becoming entangled with the Spaniards, who protected the rebels, he carried things to the extremes that we have seen.

As soon as the culprits had been executed, Cortés went to Moctezuma’s apartment, and greeting him affectionately, and boasting of the favor he did him in granting him his life, had the irons removed. The joy that Moctezuma then felt was proportioned to the vexation which the ignominy

had

(f) Solís, where he mentions Cortés’s sentence against Cuauhpopoca, says thus: “The case was tried by military procedure, and sentence of death was passed on them, with the provision that their bodies should be burned publicly.” In which, without stating the kind of punishment to which they were condemned, he gives it to be understood that the culprits were not burned, but only their corpses: which does not befit the truthfulness that is required of a historian. He took pains to dissemble what did not accord with the panegyric of his hero; but his dissembling avails little, since not only the other historians but Cortés himself candidly affirms it in his letter to Charles V. See especially the chronicler Herrera in his Decades, Dec. 2, bk. 8, ch. 9. [p. 102] had caused him. His fear of losing his life vanished entirely, and he re‐

Bk. IX.

ceived his liberty as an incomparable benefit. To such a degree had

the spirit of that king been debased! He embraced Cortés with the

utmost affection, expressed his gratitude to him with singular demon‐

strations, and that day showed extraordinary kindnesses to the

Spaniards and to his own vassals. Cortés withdrew the guards who

were there, and told the king that whenever he wished he might re‐

turn to his palace, well assured that the king would not do so; for he

had often heard from him that it did not suit him to return to his

palace while the Spaniards were at the court. Indeed he would not

leave the quarters, protesting the danger which the Spaniards would

run if he abandoned them; but it may be believed that his own danger

also deterred him from resuming his liberty, since he was not unaware

how ill his vassals took the abasement of his spirit and his excessive

compliance toward the Spaniards.

It is very probable that the execution of Cuauhpopoca caused

§ 8. some ferment among the nobility; for within a few days Cacamatzin, Attempts of the King king of Acolhuacan, no longer able to bear the authority which the of Acolhuacan against

the Spaniards. Spaniards were acquiring at the court of Mexico, and ashamed to see

King Moctezuma, his uncle, so wretched, sent word to him that he

should remember he was a lord, and not wish to make himself a slave

to those strangers; but seeing that Moctezuma refused to yield to his

counsel, he resolved to make war on the Spaniards himself. Their ruin

would have been inevitable if the esteem in which Cacamatzin was

held among the Mexicans and the Texcocans had been equal to his in‐

trepidity and resolution; but the Mexicans suspected that under color

of zeal for the honor of his uncle he concealed some subtle ambition,

and the design of usurping the crown of Mexico. Among the

Texcocans, his subjects, he was not much liked, on account of his

pride and the harm he had done to his brother Prince Cuicuitzcatzin,

who, to escape persecution, had taken refuge in Mexico, and was bet‐

ter liked for his agreeable and popular character.

Cacamatzin

[p. 103]

Cacamatzin therefore betook himself to Texcoco, and having convened his councilors and the most distinguished persons of his court, Bk. IX. laid before them the lamentable state in which the court of Mexico found itself through the excessive daring of the Spaniards and the pusillanimity of the king his uncle; the authority which those few strangers were arrogating to themselves; the most grievous insults they had done to the person of the king by imprisoning him as if he were a vile slave, and even to the gods themselves, by introducing into that kingdom the worship of other, foreign deities: he exaggerated the evils that might result from such beginnings against the court and against the kingdom of Acolhuacan. “It is time now,” he said, “to fight for our religion, for our country, for our liberty, and for our honor, before the power of these men increases, whether through new reinforcements come from their own country, or through new alliances contracted in ours.” Finally he bade them declare their opinion freely. The councilors for the most part declared for the party of war, either to please the king or because in fact they were of the same mind; but certain elders of great authority told the king frankly that he should not let himself be carried away so easily by youthful ardor; that before taking any resolution he should consider that the Spaniards were warlike and resolute men, and fought with far superior arms; that he should bear in mind not so much his kinship with Moctezuma as that king’s alliance and friendship with the Spaniards; for such a friendship, of which there were certain and clear proofs, would make him sacrifice to the ambition of those strangers all the interests of blood and of country.

In spite of these representations war was resolved on, and preparations were immediately begun with the greatest secrecy; not with so much, however, that the news did not reach both Moctezuma and Cortés. This general fell into a most grave disquiet, but considering, on the other hand, that all rash undertakings succeeded for him, he resolved to forestall the blow by marching with his troops to make an

assault [p. 104] assault on Texcoco. Moctezuma dissuaded him from so bold a plan, Bk. IX.

informing him of the forces of that court, and of the immense multi‐

tude of its inhabitants. Cortés therefore determined to send an em‐

bassy to that king, reminding him of the friendship contracted al‐

ready at Ayotzinco, when he went there to meet him in the name of

the king his uncle, and bidding him consider that as easy as it is to

undertake a war, so difficult is it to come out of it: that it would be

more to his advantage to maintain good relations with the King of

Castile and with the Spanish nation. Cacamatzin replied that he

could not regard as friends those who took his honor from him, who

oppressed his country, who outraged his blood, and who despised his

religion: that he neither knew who the King of Castile was, nor cared

to know: that if they wished to avoid the blow that was about to fall

upon their heads, they should depart at once from Mexico and return

to their own country.

In spite of so harsh an answer, Cortés repeated the embassy; but

having been answered in the same tone, he complained to

Moctezuma, and in order to engage him in that affair he pretended to

suspect that he had some influence in the hostile designs of his

nephew. Moctezuma cleared himself of the suspicion with the most

sincere protestations, and offered to interpose his authority. He sent

word therefore to Cacamatzin to come to Mexico to visit him: that

he would find a way to settle those differences. Cacamatzin, indignant

to see Moctezuma more committed to the cause of those who were

oppressing his liberty than to that of the one who was exerting him‐

self to restore it to him, replied that if after so much infamy any sen‐

timent of honor had remained in him, he would be ashamed to see

himself made a slave by four rascals, who while they lured him with

fair words outraged him by their deeds: that since neither zeal for the

Mexican religion and for the Acolhua gods, despised by those

strangers, nor the glory of his ancestors, obscured and debased by his

cowardice, was enough to move him, he wished to give aid to the reli‐

gion, avenge

the [p. 105] the gods, preserve the kingdom, and recover his honor and liberty and those of the whole nation: that therefore he would in fact come, Bk. IX. as Moctezuma begged him, to Mexico, not with folded arms, but with sword in hand, to wipe out with the blood of the Spaniards the disgrace of the Mexicans.

King Moctezuma was dismayed at such an answer, fearing to be

§ 9. the victim in that storm either of the vengeance of the Spaniards or Imprisonment of the of the fury of King Cacamatzin; whence he resolved to apply an ex‐ King     of Acolhuacan

and of other lords, treme remedy to prevent it, and to save his life by a treachery. He and elevation of

Prince therefore secretly gave orders to certain Mexican officers who served Cuicuitzcatzin. in the guard of the king his nephew to endeavor with all diligence and without delay to seize his royal person and bring him with the greatest caution to Mexico; because this was very important to the public good of the kingdom. He suggested to them the manner of doing it, and perhaps also gave them some gift and promised them some reward to encourage them to the undertaking. These then solicited other officers or servants of King Cacamatzin whom they found more disposed to that plot, and with their help obtained all that Moctezuma wished. Among his other palaces the King of Acolhuacan had built one on the shore of the lake in such a way that boats could enter and leave by a canal that ran beneath it. Here, where King Cacamatzin was then residing, they arranged a good number of boats with armed men, and in the darkness of night, with which the greatest crimes are covered, fell suddenly upon the king, and before any men could come to defend him, put him in a boat and with the greatest speed carried him to Mexico. Moctezuma, without any regard for his character as a sovereign or for his kinship with Cacamatzin, at once handed him over to Cortés. This general, who, as appears from his conduct, had no idea of that respect which is owed to royal majesty even in the person of a barbarian, put him in irons and shut him up under the custody of a strong guard. The reflections to be made on this, as on other extraordinary

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.        O                   events [p. 106] events of our History are too easy and trivial for us to dwell on in the Bk. IX. course of the narrative.

Cacamatzin, who had begun his unfortunate reign with the dissen‐

sions with his brother Ixtlilxochitl, and with the dismemberment of

the state, ended it with the loss of his crown, of his liberty, and of his

life. Moctezuma, with Cortés’s consent, determined that the crown

of Acolhuacan should be given to Prince Cuicuitzcatzin, who had

been kept by Moctezuma in his palace, since, to escape the persecu‐

tions of his brother Cacamatzin, (g) he had taken refuge in Mexico

and placed himself under his protection. In this election wrong was

done to the princes Coanacochtzin and Ixtlilxochitl, who, being born

of Queen Xocotzin, had a greater right to the crown. The motive

which the King of Mexico had for rejecting Coanacochtzin cannot be

known, since as regards Ixtlilxochitl it appears that he did not wish to

increase the power of so terrible an enemy. However that may be,

Moctezuma had Cuicuitzcatzin acknowledged king by the Texcocan

nobility, and accompanied him together with Cortés as far as the boat

in which he was to cross the lake, and recommended to him the con‐

stant friendship of the Mexicans and of the Spaniards; for to the one

and the other he was indebted for the crown.

Cuicuitzcatzin set out for Texcoco accompanied by many nobles of

both courts, and was received there with acclamations, with tri‐

umphal arches, and with dances; the nobility conveying him on a lit‐

ter from the boat to the royal palace, where the oldest noble made

him a long speech in the name of the whole nation, congratulating

him, exhorting him

to

(g) Cortés in his letter to Charles V says that Cuicuitzcatzin was a son of

Cacamatzin; but this was a mistake of the copyist of that letter, or perhaps of Cortés

himself, since we know that they were brothers, though only on the father’s side: nor

could it be what Cortés says; for he himself affirms in another place that Cacamatzin

was a young man of twenty-five years, and moreover represents Cuicuitzcatzin as of

an age to govern the kingdom. Besides, Cortés himself in another letter, written on

May 15, 1522, says plainly that those two lords were brothers. [p. 107] to the love of his vassals, and offering to love him as a father and to revere him as their lord. No one is able to express the grief that such Bk. IX. news caused Cacamatzin, seeing himself, in the freshest youth (for he was not more than twenty-five years old), deprived of the crown which three years before he had inherited from his father, and reduced to the narrowness and solitude of a prison by the very King whom he had wished to free, and by the very strangers whom he had wished to drive from the kingdom.

Cortés now had in his power the two most powerful kings of Anahuac, and it was not long before he seized also the King of Tlacopan, the lords of Iztapalapa and of Coyoacán, both brothers of King Moctezuma, two sons of this same king, Itzcuauhtzin, lord of Tlatelolco, a high priest of Mexico, and several others of the most distinguished persons, although we do not know the circumstances of these imprisonments; but it is to be believed that he took them one by one as they came in to pay a visit to King Moctezuma.

Encouraged by such prosperous successes, and seeing the King of § 10. Mexico wholly sacrificed to his wishes, that general told him that it Submission of was now time to have his subjects acknowledge the King of Spain as Moctezuma     and of

the Mexican nobility their lawful sovereign, as one who descended from the king and god to the King of Spain. Quetzalcoatl. Moctezuma, who had not the heart to contradict him, summoned the principal nobility of the court and of the neighboring cities. They all came promptly to receive his orders, and when they had assembled in a great hall of the quarters, with Cortés and some other Spaniards present, the king made them a long speech, in which he declared the love which he bore them as a father, from whom they need not fear that he would propose to them anything that was not just and advantageous. He recalled to them the ancient tradition concerning the devolution of the Mexican empire to the descendants of Quetzalcoatl, whose lieutenants he and his predecessors had been, and the phenomena observed in the elements, which, according to the interpretation of the priests

O2                      and [p. 108] and diviners signified that the time had now come for the oracles to be fulBk. IX.

filled. I do not doubt that he made mention also of the memorable event

and prophecy of his sister Papantzin, recalled by us in Book V; which proba‐

bly was the principal cause of the abasement of his spirit. Then he went on

to compare the signs observed in the Spaniards with those of the tradition:

whence he concluded that the King of Spain was precisely the lawful de‐

scendant of Quetzalcoatl, to whom therefore he ceded the kingdom, and

gave obedience, and exhorted all to do the same. (h) In calling himself the

subject of another king he felt so great a pain

that

(h) The circumstances of the aforesaid assembly, of the homage rendered to the King of

Spain, and of the order given by Moctezuma to Cortés to leave the court, are related by the

historians with such variety that not even two of them will be found who are perfectly in

agreement. In the narration of these events I follow chiefly the accounts of Cortés and of

Bernal Díaz, both eyewitnesses. Solís asserts that Moctezuma’s acknowledgment was a mere

artifice: that he never had any intention of fulfilling what he promised: that his purpose was to be rid of

the Spaniards, and to temporize in order to come to terms afterward with his ambition, without minding

his word. But if Moctezuma’s act was a mere artifice, and he did not intend to carry out what

he promised, why, in calling himself the vassal of another monarch, did he feel so much grief

that his voice failed him and tears were drawn from him, as the same author says? If he

thought of nothing else than getting rid of the Spaniards, there was no need of such a feint.

How many times could he, with a single nod to his vassals, if he had wished, either have sac‐

rificed all the Spaniards to his gods, or else, leaving them their lives, have had them conduct‐

ed bound to the port, so that from there they might retake the road to Cuba? The whole

conduct of Moctezuma was altogether opposed to the intentions which Solís supposes in

him; but nothing gives the lie to his accusation so much as the clear testimony given by the

court of Spain, which in several rescripts issued in favor of the royal descendants of

Moctezuma, granting them exemptions and extraordinary privileges, declares that such privi‐

leges cannot serve as an example to any other house; because no other, it adds, has done

Spain so great a service as that which the Emperor Moctezuma did her in incorporating into

that crown, by his voluntary cession, a kingdom as rich and as great as that of Mexico. If the

obedience given by Moctezuma to the Catholic King had been such as Solís represents it to

us, it would be said that the court of Spain believed the kingdom of Mexico incorporated

into the Crown of Castile by means of a feigned and deceitful cession, and of a mere artifice

of Moctezuma: which would be a most grave wrong to the Christian rectitude of the

Catholic Kings. Vetancurt, in pt. 2, treatise 1 of his Teatro mexicano, cites the aforesaid re‐

scripts, the originals of which are without doubt in the archive of the Counts of Moctezuma

and of Tula. [p. 109] that it cut short his speech and made him substitute tears for words.

Bk. IX. The King’s weeping was followed by such bitter sobs from the whole assembly that they moved the Spaniards to tenderness and pity. The sobs were then succeeded by a melancholy silence, which was broken by one of the most authoritative Mexican lords with these words: “Since, lord,” he said to the king, “the time has come when the ancient oracles are to be fulfilled, and the gods will, and you command us, that we be subjects of another lord, what have we to do but submit to the sovereign dispositions of Heaven, made known to us through your mouth?”

Cortés then thanked the king, and all the lords who were present, for their prompt and sincere submission, and protested that his sovereign did not claim to take the crown from the King of Mexico, but only to have his high dominion over that kingdom acknowledged; that Moctezuma would not only continue to command his subjects, but would even exercise the same authority over all those peoples who should submit to the Spaniards. The assembly being dismissed, Cortés had a public instrument made of that act, with all the solemnities that seemed to him necessary, in order to send it to the court of Spain.

Having thus happily taken this step, he represented to Moctezuma § 11. that since he had already acknowledged the dominion of the King of First       homage of the

Mexicans to the Castile over those countries, it was necessary to show his subordina‐ crown of Castile. tion by some contribution of gold and silver, alleging for this the right which sovereigns had to exact such homage from their vassals in order to maintain the splendor of the crown, to support their ministers, for the expenses of war, and for the other needs of the state. Moctezuma with royal magnificence gave him the treasure of his father Axayacatl, which was kept, as we have already said, in that same palace, from which Cortés had taken nothing until then, although he had been expressly permitted by the king to take from it whatever he wished. All this treasure came into the hands of the Spaniards together with

all [p. 110] all that the lords, feudatories of that crown, contributed: which was

Bk. IX.

so much that with it, after setting aside the fifth part for the King of

Spain, Cortés had what was needed to pay the debts he had incurred

in Cuba in fitting out the fleet, and to reward his officers and soldiers,

with still enough left for the expenses to be incurred in the future.

For the king there were set apart, besides the fifth part of the gold

and silver, certain works which were kept whole on account of their

marvelous workmanship, which according to Cortés’s reckoning were

worth more than a hundred thousand ducats; but the greater part of

this wealth was lost, as we shall see hereafter.

§ 12.      The Spaniards triumphed at seeing themselves masters, at so small

Disquiet of the Mexican nobility, and

a cost, of so many riches, and at having subjected to their sovereign,

new fears of without any toil, so great and opulent a kingdom; but their felicity

Moctezuma.

was now excessive, and it was necessary, according to the condition of

human affairs, that prosperous events should alternate with adverse.

The Mexican nobility, who until then had kept a respectful silence

out of their supreme deference to the will of their lord, seeing him

now in such abasement, the King of Acolhuacan and other distin‐

guished persons put in irons, and the nation subjected to the domin‐

ion of a foreign monarch whom they did not know, began first to

whisper, and then to speak with greater freedom, to blame their own

toleration, to hold assemblies, and even, as was said, to raise troops to

free their king and their nation from that ignominious oppression.

Some of his favorites spoke to Moctezuma, representing to him the

grief which his vassals felt at his misfortune, considering his power di‐

minished and the splendor of his dignity dimmed, and the ferment

which was now beginning to be felt no less among the nobility than

among the common people, impatient at seeing themselves subjected

to a foreign king and condemned to sacrifice to him the fruit of their

labors. They exhorted him to shake off the fear that had taken posses‐

sion of him, and to recover his authority: for

if

[PDF 111]

Bk. IX.

111 if he did not do so, his vassals would do it, who had determined to drive from the court and from the kingdom those guests so insolent and pernicious. On the other hand the priests exaggerated to him the harm that religion was suffering, and frightened him with the threats which, they said, were being made by their gods, angered and resolved to deny rain to the fields, and their protection to the Mexicans, if he did not send away those men so contrary to their worship. Several historians, too ready to believe apparitions of spirits, add that the Devil himself made himself visible to the king, threatening him with many evils that would fall upon his person and upon the kingdom if he suffered the Spaniards any longer, and promising him, if he drove them out, to perpetuate the crown of Mexico in his family and to make his vassals supremely happy.

Moved by so many representations and threats, ashamed that his cowardice should be reproached to him, and softened by the misfortune of his nephew Cacamatzin, whom he had always loved with singular tenderness, and by that of his brother Cuitláhuac and of other persons of the first nobility, although he did not consent to the course of taking the lives of the Spaniards, as some counseled him, he nevertheless resolved to tell them openly that they should leave that kingdom. One day, therefore, he sent for Cortés, who, aware of the secret conferences which in the preceding days the king had had with his ministers, with the nobles, and with the priests, felt a great disturbance in his mind; but dissembling as much as he could, he went at once to the king, accompanied by twelve Spaniards. Moctezuma received him with less affection than he was wont to show him, and frankly disclosed to him his resolution. “You cannot call in doubt,” he said to him, “the great love I have borne you, after so many and such clear demonstrations. Until now I have kept you gladly in my court, indeed I have chosen to remain here to live with you, for the singular pleasure I take in your familiarity and conversation. As far as concerns me,

I

[p. 112]

## I would keep you still without any change, every day furnishing you Bk. IX.

ever greater proofs of my benevolence; but it cannot be; for neither

do my gods permit it, nor do my vassals tolerate it. I find myself

threatened with the most terrible punishments of Heaven if I let you

remain any longer in my kingdom, and among my vassals there has

begun to be felt such disquiet that if I do not promptly remove the

cause I shall afterward find it altogether impossible to quiet them:

wherefore it is necessary, as much for my good as for yours, and for

that of this whole state, that you prepare to return to your country.”

Cortés, although pierced with the bitterest grief, nevertheless affect‐

ing great serenity in his countenance, replied that his mind was ready

to obey him; but that, since he lacked ships for the passage, because

those in which he had come from Cuba had been ruined, he needed

time, workmen, and materials to make others. Moctezuma then, full

of joy at the readiness with which he offered to obey, and embracing

him, told him that there was no need to hasten the departure: that he

should build his ships, and that he would furnish him the necessary

timber, and the people to cut it and to carry it to the port. In fact he

at once gave orders to a good number of carpenters to cut the neces‐

sary timber from a pine wood that was not far from the port of

Chiahuitztla, and Cortés for his part sent some Spaniards there to

oversee the cutting, awaiting meanwhile a change in the state of

things in Mexico, or the arrival of new reinforcements of Spaniards

sent to him from the islands, or from Spain. (i)

Eight

(i) Almost all the Spanish historians say that when the king had Cortés summoned

to give him the order to depart, he had prepared an army to make himself obeyed by

force, should there be any resistance; but there is great variety among them, for some

affirm that there were a hundred thousand men under arms, others reduce this num‐

ber by half, and others finally reduce it to five thousand. I am persuaded that there

was in fact some troop prepared, though not by the king’s order, but only by some of

those nobles who had committed themselves more deeply in this affair.

[p. 113]

Eight days after this resolution had been taken, Moctezuma again sent for Cortés, and this general fell into fresh disquiet. The King Bk. IX. told him that it was no longer necessary to build ships, because a short time before eighteen vessels like his own, already destroyed, had put in at the port of Chalchiuhcuecan, in which he might embark with his men: that he should therefore hasten his departure, since this was in the interest of the kingdom. Cortés concealed the joy that he felt at such news, and, thanking God in his heart for having sent him so opportune a relief, replied to the king that if those ships were to make the voyage toward Cuba, he was ready to depart; but that otherwise it would be necessary to continue the building of his own ships. He also saw and examined the paintings of that fleet sent to the king by the governors of the coast, and did not doubt that it was of Spaniards; but, very far from thinking that it had been sent against himself, he rather persuaded himself that his procurators, whom he had sent the year before to the court of Spain, had returned, and that they were bringing with them the royal dispatches and conducting a good number of troops for the conquest.

This great consolation lasted until the letters arrived from

§ 13. Gonzalo de Sandoval, governor of the colony of Veracruz, in which he Fleet of the governor let him know that that fleet, composed of eleven ships and seven of        Cuba against

Cortés. brigantines, with eighty-five horse, eight hundred foot, and more than five hundred seamen, with twelve pieces of artillery and ample munitions of war, under the command of General Pánfilo de Narváez, had been sent by Diego Velázquez, governor of Cuba, against Cortés himself, as a rebel vassal and a traitor to his sovereign. He received this heavy blow in the presence of King Moctezuma; but without showing any disturbance in his countenance, he gave the king to understand that those who had put in at Chalchiuhcuecan were new companions sent to him from Cuba. He used the same dissimulation toward his Spaniards, until he had prepared their minds.

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                P                       It [p. 114] It is beyond doubt that this occasion was one of those in which Bk. IX.

Cortés made his invincible constancy and magnanimity shine. He

found himself on the one hand threatened with the whole power of

the Mexicans if he remained at the court; and on the other he saw

prepared against himself an army of his own countrymen far superior

to his own; but his shrewdness, his singular industry, and his mar‐

velous courage changed into good all the evil that hung over him. He

endeavored, both by letters and by certain mediators in whom he

most trusted, to win over the mind of Narváez and bring him to rea‐

son, making him various offers, and representing to him the advan‐

tages that the Spaniards would have if the two armies were united

and acted in concert; and, on the contrary, the evils which discord

must cause to the one and the other. Narváez, on the advice of three

deserters from Cortés, had already landed with his whole army on the

coast of Cempoala, and had taken up quarters in that city; the lord of

which, knowing that his new guests were likewise Spaniards, and be‐

lieving that they came to join his friend Cortés, or else fearing their

power, received them with the greatest honor, and provided them

with everything they needed. Moctezuma, believing at first the same,

sent Narváez rich gifts, and gave orders to his governors to show him

the same attentions that they had already shown to Cortés; but a few

days later he perceived the discord that was between them, in spite of

Cortés’s great dissimulation and his efforts to prevent such news

from reaching the king or his vassals.

Moctezuma then had the finest opportunity in the world to de‐

stroy the one party and the other, if he had harbored in his heart

those bloody counsels which several historians have wished to impute

to him. Narváez exerted himself to alienate him from Cortés and

from those of his party, accusing them all of treachery, and promising

to punish their unheard-of rashness in imprisoning so great a king,

and to free both the king himself and the whole nation from their op‐

pression; but Moctezuma

was [p. 115] was so far from plotting anything against Cortés on such suggestions

Bk. IX. that, on the contrary, when this general let him know of his expedition against Narváez, Moctezuma showed great regret at the risk to which he was exposing himself with such inferior troops, and offered promptly to raise a good army to send to his aid.

Cortés had already used every possible diligence to come to a peaceful accommodation advantageous to both armies, with no other effect than that of receiving fresh contempt and threats from the arrogant and haughty Narváez. Seeing himself therefore compelled to make war on his own countrymen, and not wishing, through his distrust, to avail himself of the help which the King of Mexico offered him, he asked the senate of Tlaxcala to equip four thousand men of war to lead with him, and sent to Chinantla one of his soldiers called Tobilla, a man well versed in the trade of war, to ask two thousand men from that warlike nation, and to procure three hundred pikes of the sort which the same Chinantecs used to resist the cavalry of Narváez, since they were longer and stronger than those of the Spaniards. He left in Mexico a hundred and forty (l) Spaniards with all their allies under the command of Captain Pedro de Alvarado, charging them to guard and treat the king well, and to endeavor to keep on good terms with the Mexicans, especially with the royal family and with the nobility. In taking leave of the king he told him that he was leaving in his place Captain Tonatiuh (by this name of the Sun the Mexicans called Alvarado, because he was blond), charged with serving His Majesty in everything: that he begged him to continue his protection of the Spaniards: that he was going to

P2                          find

(l) Bernal Díaz says that the Spaniards who remained in Mexico were eighty-three. In the modern editions of Cortés’s letters it is said that they were five hundred, but in an old edition the number 140 is given, which seems to me the true one, considering the total number of the Spanish troops. The number 500 is evidently false, and contradicts the account of Cortés himself. [p. 116] find that newly arrived commander, and to do all that he could to put

Bk. IX.

his royal orders into effect. Moctezuma, after making him fresh

protestations of his good will, had him abundantly supplied with pro‐

visions, and with carriers for the transport of the baggage, and dis‐

missed him with the greatest affection.

Cortés left Mexico at the beginning of May of the year 1520, after

having been six months at that court, with seventy Spaniards and a

number of Mexican nobles, who wished to accompany him for some

distance on the road. Several historians have convinced themselves

that the Mexicans went to act as spies, and to give the king an ac‐

count of whatever happened there; but Cortés did not believe them

such, although otherwise he did not trust them. He made the journey

by way of Cholula, where Captain Velázquez joined him, who was re‐

turning from Coatzacoalco, having been sent there with some troops

by Cortés to look for a more convenient port for the ships. Here also

Cortés received a good supply of provisions sent him by the senate of

Tlaxcala; but he did not get the four thousand men he had asked for:

either because they did not dare to enter into new fights with the

Spaniards, as Bernal Díaz affirms; or because they would not go so far

from their own country, as other historians say; or because, seeing

Cortés with forces so inferior to those of his enemy, they feared being

vanquished in that expedition. Some days’ marches before arriving at

Cempoala Cortés was overtaken by the soldier Tobilla with the three

hundred pikes of Chinantla, and at Tapanacuetla, a village about thir‐

ty miles distant from that city, the famous Captain Sandoval joined

him with sixty soldiers of the garrison of Veracruz.

§ 14.    Finally, after making fresh demands upon Narváez, and having disVictory of Cortés tributed some gold among the partisans of that arrogant general,

over Narváez.

Cortés entered Cempoala at midnight with two hundred and fifty

men, (m) without horses, nor with

other

(m) Bernal Díaz says that Cortés went to Cempoala with 206 men. Torquemada

counts 266, besides five captains; but Cortés, who knew it better than they, affirms

that there were 250. [p. 117] other arms than pikes, swords, bucklers, and daggers, and advancing

Bk. IX. quietly and without any noise to the great temple of that city, where the enemy had their quarters, he made so furious an assault upon it that before the break of daylight he had already made himself master of the quarters, of all his enemies, of the artillery, of the arms, and of the horses, only four of his soldiers being killed, and fifteen of the enemy, and many on both sides wounded. (n) He had himself acknowledged by all as captain general and supreme magistrate; he put in irons in the fortress of Veracruz both Narváez and Salvatierra, a distinguished person and a sworn enemy of Cortés, and had the sails, the rudders, and the compasses removed from the ships. Scarcely had the light of that day begun (it was Pentecost Sunday, May 27) when the two thousand Chinantecs arrived in good order and well armed, (o) who came only to be witnesses of the triumph of Cortés and of the shame of the partisans of Narváez, seeing themselves vanquished by so few enemies, and less well armed than themselves. The success of this expedition was owing in great part to the incomparable bravery of Sandoval, who with eighty men climbed the temple in the midst of a storm of arrows and balls, assailed the sanctuary where Narváez had fortified himself, and made himself master of his person.

Cortés, now finding himself with eighteen ships, with nearly two thousand men of Spanish troops, with about a hundred horse, and with a sufficient supply of munitions of war, thought of making new expeditions on the coasts of the Gulf of Mexico, and had already appointed the leaders who were to command them, and the men

who

Whoever wishes to inform himself of all the circumstances of this glorious expedition of Cortés may consult the historians of the conquest; for we omit them, as not befitting our History.
