§ 15.

At that time, when Cortés was absent from Mexico, there came

Massacre of the the festival of the incensing of Huitzilopochtli, which was held in the Mexican nobility, and

rising of the people month Toxcatl, which that year began on the 13th of our May. This against the Spaniards. festival, the most solemn of those that were held every year, was cele‐

brated with dances of the king and of the nobility, of the priests, and

of the people. The nobility begged Captain Alvarado to consent that

the king should go to the temple to perform his duty. Alvarado ex‐

cused himself, either on account of the orders given him by Cortés,

or because he feared that the Mexicans might plot some novelty with

the king among them, well aware how easily public rejoicings turn

into tumults. It was therefore decided to hold that religious dance in

the court of the palace, (p) or quarters of the Spaniards, either by the

arrangement of that captain or by order of the king himself, so that

he might attend it according to their custom. When the day of the

festival came, many men of the highest nobility (whose number (q) is

not known) assembled in that court, richly adorned with various or‐

naments of gold, gems, and feathers. They began to sing and to dance

to the sound of the instruments, and meanwhile Alvarado had some

soldiers occupy the gates. When he then saw the Mexicans

more

(p) The historians of the conquest commonly say that the dance took place in the

court of the great temple, but it is not probable that the immense throng of people

who gathered there would have allowed so horrible a massacre of the nobility to be

committed, especially since the armory was there, whence they could take as many

arms as they wished to oppose the rashness of those few strangers; nor can it be be‐

lieved that the Spaniards would have been willing to expose themselves to so evident

a risk of perishing. Cortés and Bernal Díaz make no mention of the place of the

dance. Father Acosta says that it took place in the palace, and it could not have been

any other than that where the king was then living. The improbability that is seen in

the account of those historians, and the judgment and early date of Father Acosta,

make us prefer the testimony of this writer alone to that of all the others.

(q) According to Gómara the nobles who took part in the dance were six hundred;

according to other historians, more than a thousand; and according to Monsignor de

las Casas, more than two thousand. [p. 119] more heated, and perhaps even wearied by the dance, he gave the signal to his men to assail them, and these threw themselves at once Bk. IX. with fury upon those unfortunate men, who could neither offer resistance, since they were unarmed and weary, nor was it possible for them to escape by flight, because the gates were occupied. Terrible was the massacre, lamentable the cries which the dying sent up to Heaven, and copious the blood that was shed there. This fatal blow was one of the most painful to the Mexicans; for they lost in it the flower of their nobility, and to perpetuate its memory among their descendants they composed upon such a theme mournful songs, or elegies, which were preserved for many years after the conquest. When that tragic and horrible business was ended, the Spaniards stripped the corpses of all the wealth with which they were adorned.

The motive that induced Alvarado to so rash and inhuman a deed is not known. Some say that it was none other than that accursed hunger for gold. (r) Others affirm, and this seems more probable, that having been told that the Mexicans meant at that festival to strike a master stroke against the Spaniards, in order to free themselves from their oppression and to restore to liberty their king and lord, whom the Spaniards held in prison, he forestalled them, thinking true the common saying, he who attacks wins. (s) Whatever the case, his conduct cannot be excused of imprudence and of cruelty.

The

(r) The Mexican historians, Father Sahagún in his manuscript history, Monsignor de las Casas in his formidable work on the Destruction of the Indies, and Gómara in his Crónica de la Nueva España affirm that Alvarado’s avarice was the cause of the massacre of the Mexican nobility; but I cannot believe it without more effective proofs. Gómara and Monsignor de las Casas simply followed Sahagún, and he followed the information of the Mexicans, who, being enemies of the Spaniards, are not in this worthy of credit.

(s) It is altogether incredible that the Mexicans should have plotted against the Spaniards on the occasion of the dance that treachery which several historians suppose, and much more that they had already prepared the pots to cook the flesh of the Spaniards, as Torquemada says. These are fables invented to justify Alvarado. What seems to me most probable is [p. 120] The common people, irritated by so painful a blow, treated the Bk. IX. Spaniards from then on as capital enemies of their country. Some

Mexican troops assailed the quarters with such impetuosity that they

ruined a part of the wall, undermined the palace in various places, and

burned the munitions; but they were repulsed by the fire of the ar‐

tillery and of the muskets, whereby the Spaniards had leisure to re‐

pair the wall. That night they rested from the labor of the day, but the

next day the assault was so furious that the Spaniards believed they

must perish, and in fact not even one of them would have remained

alive, as six or seven had already been killed, if the king, showing him‐

self to the throng of the assailants, had not by his authority restrained

their fury. Respect for the majesty of their sovereign held the com‐

moners back from assailing the quarters with arms; but it did not

turn them from other sorts of hostility: they also burned the four

brigantines which Cortés had had built in order to save himself in

them, in case he could not escape by the causeways made on the lake,

and resolved to destroy the Spaniards by famine, denying them provi‐

sions, and endeavoring to prevent their introduction by means of a

ditch dug around the quarters.

In such a state were the Spaniards in Mexico when Alvarado in‐

formed Cortés, begging him by two Tlaxcalan messengers to hasten

his return, if he did not wish to find them all dead. King Moctezuma

asked the same of him, letting him know how vexatious that rising of

his vassals had been to him, caused by the rash and bloody outrage of

Captain Tonatiuh.

Cortés, after giving his orders to transfer the colony of Veracruz to

another site nearer the port of Chalchiuhcuecan (although this plan

was not then put into execution), marched with his men by forced

marches toward the capital. In Tlaxcala he was magnificently lodged

in

that the Tlaxcalans, from the great hatred which they bore the Mexicans, put into

Alvarado’s head the alleged treachery. In the history of the Conquest we have several

examples of such suggestions of the Tlaxcalans against their enemies. [p. 121] in the palace of Prince Maxixcatzin. There he reviewed his troops, Bk. IX. and found ninety-six horse and one thousand three hundred Spanish foot, to whom were added two thousand Tlaxcalans furnished by that republic. With this army he entered Mexico on June 24. He met with no opposition at his entry; but he at once perceived the popular ferment, as much from the small number of people to be seen in the streets as from certain bridges that had been raised from the canals. When he entered the quarters, with that jubilation on both sides which is easy to imagine, Moctezuma came to meet him in the court with the most obliging demonstrations of friendship; but Cortés, either made insolent by the victory won over Narváez and by the great force he had under his orders, or persuaded that it was necessary to feign a belief that the king was to blame for the disquiet of his vassals, passed on without so much as heeding him. The king, pierced with the keenest grief at seeing himself so unworthily slighted, withdrew to his apartment, where his sorrow was increased by the news which his servants brought him at once, of the insulting words that the Spanish general had spoken against His Majesty. (t)

Cortés reproved Captain Alvarado with the utmost severity, and would at once have given him the punishment he deserved, had the circumstances of the time and of the culprit permitted it. He foresaw the great storm that was about to fall upon

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                          Q                           their

(t) The historian Solís refuses to believe the slight shown to Moctezuma by Cortés, and in order to defend this general he wrongs Bernal Díaz, who affirms it as an eyewitness, and the chronicler Herrera, who relates it on good documents. He accuses Bernal Díaz undeservedly of partiality against Cortés, and says of Herrera that it may be suspected that he wished to adopt Díaz’s account in order to be able to apply to it a maxim of Tacitus: Ambition, he adds, dangerous among historians; but in no one more than in Solís himself; for every impartial person well instructed in the history of Mexico will see, on reading that of Solís, that this author, instead of fitting his maxims to the narrative, on the contrary fits the narrative to his maxims. Finally, unless he alleges better reasons than those he employs against Bernal Díaz, we must give credit to the testimony of this author, who was present at the event. [p. 122] their heads, and it did not seem to him wise counsel to make an ene‐

Bk. IX.

my, on the occasion of so great a peril, of one of the most valiant cap‐

tains he had in his army.

With the new troops brought to Mexico by Cortés there was an

army of nine thousand men, and as they could not all be lodged in the

quarters, they occupied also some of the buildings that lay within the

enclosure of the great temple, in the part nearest the quarters. With

the multitude there grew likewise the scarcity of provisions, already

caused by the lack of a market; for out of hatred of the Spaniards the

Mexicans would not hold one. Cortés therefore sent word to

Moctezuma, with great threats, that he should give orders for the

market to be held, so that they might supply themselves there with

everything they needed. Moctezuma replied that the most authorita‐

tive persons, in whom he could trust to have such an order carried

out, were, like himself, in prison; that he should set some of them at

liberty, so that they might attend to it. Cortés took from prison

Prince Cuitláhuac, Moctezuma’s brother, being very far from foresee‐

ing that the liberty of that prince would cause the ruin of the

Spaniards. Cuitláhuac never returned to the quarters, nor did he

restore the market, either because he would not favor the Spaniards,

or because the Mexicans did not consent to it; indeed, they forced

him to exercise his office of general. In fact it was he who from then

on commanded the troops, and directed the hostilities against the

Spaniards, until on the death of his brother he was elected king of

Mexico.

§ 16.    On the day on which Cortés entered Mexico the commoners made Fights between the Mexicans and the   no  move; but on the next day they began to hurl from slings so many

Spaniards in the stones against the Spaniards that it seemed, as Cortés said, a tempest,

capital.

and so many arrows that they covered the whole pavement of the

court and the flat roofs of the palace, and the assailants were so many

that the ground of the streets could not be seen. It did not seem well

to Cortés to remain on the defensive, lest it be ascribed to cowardice,

whereby the enemy would take greater courage. He therefore made

a [p. 123] a sortie against them with four hundred men, part Spaniards and part

Bk. IX. Tlaxcalans. The Mexicans retired with little loss to themselves, and Cortés, after setting fire to some houses, returned to the quarters; but seeing that the enemy continued their hostilities, he sent out Captain Ordaz with two hundred soldiers. The Mexicans pretended to be thrown into confusion and to flee, in order to draw them away from the quarters, as in fact they succeeded in doing; but suddenly the Spaniards saw themselves surrounded on every side by enemies, and attacked by one body of troops in front and another in the rear, though so tumultuously and in such disorder that they got in one another’s way. At the same time a great rabble showed itself upon the flat roofs, which incessantly hurled stones and arrows. The Spaniards then found themselves in great peril, and this occasion was beyond doubt one of those in which the valiant Ordaz made his bravery most conspicuous. The fight was very bloody, but without great loss to the Spaniards, who with their muskets and crossbows cleared the flat roofs, and with their pikes and swords drove back the multitude that flooded the street, and so were at length able to withdraw to the quarters, leaving many Mexicans dead, and of their own not more than eight; but nearly all came out wounded, and Ordaz himself as well. Among the injuries that the Mexicans did the Spaniards on this day was that of setting fire to several parts of the quarters, and in one of them the fire was such that the Spaniards were forced to throw down the wall, and to defend that breach with the artillery and with many men, whom they posted there until night, when the enemy gave them leisure to rebuild the wall and to tend the wounded.

The next day (June 26) the assault was more terrible and the fury of the Mexicans greater. The Spaniards defended themselves with twelve pieces of artillery, which made great slaughter in the throng of the assailants; but since these were in so great a number, they hid the dead under the feet of those who stepped into their place. Cortés, on seeing the

Q2                 obstinacy [p. 124] obstinacy they showed, sallied forth with the greater part of his Bk. IX. troops,  and made his way, fighting, along one of the three main

streets of the city; he stormed some bridges, set fire to some houses,

and after fighting almost the whole day returned to the quarters with

more than fifty Spaniards wounded, leaving innumerable Mexicans

dead.

Experience showed Cortés that the greatest harm came to his

troops from the flat roofs; wherefore, to avoid it, he had three en‐

gines of war built (called by the Spaniards Mantas), so large that each

could carry twenty armed soldiers, covered with strong planking to

protect the soldiers from the stones thrown from the roofs, furnished

with wheels to ease their movement, and with their little windows or

loopholes for the firing of the muskets.

While these engines were being built, great changes took place at

that court. Moctezuma, having gone up on a tower of that palace, had

from there observed one of the aforesaid fights, and had seen in the

throng of the people his brother Cuitláhuac commanding the

Mexican troops. At the sight of so many lamentable objects his mind

was assailed by a crowd of melancholy thoughts. He saw on the one

hand the danger he ran of losing crown and life, and on the other

there presented themselves to him the destruction of the buildings of

the capital, the killing of his vassals, and the happiness of his ene‐

mies; and he found no remedy for so many evils save the prompt de‐

parture of the Spaniards. In such thoughts he passed that night, and

the next day, early, he called Cortés, and spoke to him on the same

subject, begging him urgently not to put off any longer his departure

from that court. Cortés had no need of such entreaties to resolve

upon the journey. He found himself in extreme want of provisions:

food was already being given out by measure to the soldiers, and was

so little that it sufficed only to sustain life, not the strength necessary

to oppose so many enemies, by whom they were incessantly harassed.

Finally he saw that it was so impossible for him to make himself mas‐

ter of that city, as he would have wished, that he could not even main‐

tain himself in it. On the other

hand [p. 125] it greatly grieved him to abandon the enterprise begun, losing in a moment by his departure all the advantages which he had procured Bk. IX. for himself by his courage, his industry, and his good fortune; but yielding to the times, he answered the king that he was ready to depart for the peace of the kingdom, provided that his vassals laid down their arms.                                                              § 17.

Hardly was this conference ended when the cry to arms was raised The king’s address to

the people, and its in the quarters for a general assault by the Mexicans. On all sides they effects. sought to climb the wall in order to enter, while some troops of archers posted at a convenient distance shot an immense multitude of arrows to overcome the resistance of the besieged, and some of the assailants pressed forward so far that, in spite of the fire of the artillery and the muskets, they set foot inside the quarters and fought hand to hand with the Spaniards. These, thinking themselves by now almost beaten and overwhelmed by the multitude, fought like desperate men. Moctezuma, on seeing their conflict and his own peril, resolved to show himself, in order to restrain by his presence and his voice the fury of his vassals. Having therefore put on the royal insignia, and escorted by some of his ministers and by two hundred Spaniards, he went up on the flat roof and presented himself to the people, ordering the ministers to call for silence, so that the voice of the sovereign might be heard. At his appearance the assault ceased, all fell silent, and some knelt to do him reverence. He spoke in a loud voice, and made them in substance this brief speech: “If the motive which induces you to take up arms against these strangers is zeal for my liberty, I hold dear the love and fidelity which you show me; but you are mistaken in believing me their prisoner, since it is in my power to leave this palace of my late father and go to my own whenever I please. If, however, your indignation is caused by their stay in this court, I let you know that they have given me their word to depart, and I assure you that they will certainly depart as soon as you lay down your arms. Let your disquiet cease, then; show me in this your fidelity, unless what I have

heard [p. 126] heard said be true, namely that you have sworn to another lord that Bk. IX. obedience which you owe to me; which I cannot persuade myself to

believe, nor could you do it without drawing down upon yourselves all

the wrath of Heaven.”

The people remained for a little while silent, until a man bolder (u)

than the rest raised his voice, calling the king a coward and effemi‐

nate, and fitter to handle the spindle and the distaff than to rule so

courageous a nation as the Mexican, and reproaching him that

through his cowardice he had made himself so basely the prisoner of

his enemies; and, not content with insulting him in words, taking his

bow in hand he shot an arrow at him. The commoners, being so easily

moved by the first impulse that is given them, promptly followed his

example: insults began to be heard everywhere, and stones and arrows

to rain toward the place where the king was. The Spanish historians

say that although the royal person was covered with two bucklers, he

was nevertheless wounded by a stone on the head, by another on a

leg, and by an arrow in an arm. He was then carried by the ministers

to his chamber, tormented far more by indignation and rage than by

his wounds.

Meanwhile the Mexicans persisted in their assault, and the

Spaniards in their defense, until some nobles called Cortés to that

very place where the king had been wounded, to discuss with him

several articles which we do not find set forth by the historians.

Cortés asked them why they wished to treat him as an enemy, having

received no wrong from him. “If you wish,” they said to him, “to

avoid our hostilities, leave this city at once; if not, we are resolved to

die, or else to make you all die.” Cortés added that he did not com‐

plain of them because he was afraid of their arms, but because it

grieved him to be

forced

(u) Father Acosta says that the Mexican who spoke those insolent words to the

king was Cuauhtémoc, his nephew, and afterwards the last king of Mexico; but I do

not believe it. [p. 127] forced by themselves to put them to death, and to destroy so fine a city. The nobles went away repeating their threats.                 Bk. IX.

The three engines of war being at last completed, Cortés went out with them early on June 28 or 29 (x), and took his way along one of the three principal streets of the city with three thousand Tlaxcalans and other auxiliary troops, with the greater part of the Spaniards, and with ten pieces of artillery. When they arrived at the bridge that was over the first canal, they set the engines and the ladders against the houses in order to dislodge the rabble that was on the flat roofs; but the stones hurled thence against the engines were so many and so large that they shattered them. The Spaniards fought courageously until midday without ever being able to storm the bridge; for which reason they returned shamefaced to the quarters, leaving one of their number dead, and bringing with them many wounded.

Encouraged accordingly, five hundred of the Mexican nobles forti‐

§ 18. fied themselves in the upper court of the great temple, well supplied A terrible fight in the

temple. with arms and provisions, and began from there to do great damage to the Spaniards with stones and arrows, while other Mexican troops assailed them in the street. Cortés sent a captain thither with a hundred soldiers to drive the nobles from that place, which, being too high and near, commanded the quarters; but having tried the ascent three times, they were vigorously repulsed. That general therefore determined to deliver the assault himself, although from the first fight he had had a good wound in his left hand: he bound his buckler to his arm, and having had the temple surrounded by a competent number of Spaniards and Tlaxcalans, he began to climb the stairs with a great part of his men. The besieged nobles disputed the ascent with great courage, and overthrew

some

(x) The variety of the authors regarding the order and the circumstances of the fights that took place in those days is beyond description. I follow the account of Cortés, which seems to me in this the most authoritative. [p. 128] some Spaniards; meanwhile other Mexican troops who had entered Bk. IX.

the court fought furiously with those who were surrounding the tem‐

ple. Cortés, though with the utmost toil and difficulty, set foot with

his men in the upper court. Here was the greatest force, and the

greatest danger, of the fight, which lasted more than three hours. Of

the Mexicans, some died there by the sword, and some threw them‐

selves down to the lower stages of the temple, where they continued

the fight until all were killed. Cortés had the sanctuaries set on fire,

and returned in good order to the quarters. Forty-six Spaniards per‐

ished there, and all the rest came out wounded and bathed in blood.

This famous fight was one of those in which there was fighting with

the greatest courage on both sides; and for that reason both the

Tlaxcalans and the Mexicans represented it in their paintings after

the conquest.

Some historians add to this the circumstance of the great danger

in which, as they say, Cortés found himself of being thrown down by

two Mexicans, who, resolved to sacrifice their own lives for the good

of their country, seized Cortés at the edge of the upper court to drag

him with them in their fall, believing that they would put an end to

the war by the death of that general; but this deed, of which neither

Cortés, nor Bernal Díaz, nor Gómara, nor any other of the older his‐

torians makes mention, has become more improbable through the

circumstances added to it by some modern writers. (y)

Cortés

(y) Solís says that the two Mexicans came up to Cortés on their knees, as if to implore

his clemency, and without delay hurled themselves down with their prey in their hands, increas‐

ing the violence of the impulse with the natural force of their own weight; that Cortés freed

himself from them, and thrust them back with some difficulty. I too find the difficulty very

great in believing so surprising a strength in Cortés. The most humane gentlemen,

M. Raynal and Dr. Robertson, moved to pity, as it appears, by Cortés’s peril, supplied

him, the latter with I know not what battlements, and the former with a grating, to

which he might cling in order to disengage himself from the Mexicans; but neither

did the Mexicans ever make gratings, nor were there battlements on that temple. It

is a wonder that these authors, so incredulous with regard to what is attested both by

the Spaniards and by the Indians, should then believe what is neither found in the

ancient authors nor even verisimilar.

[p. 129]

Cortés, having returned to the quarters, conferred anew with cer‐

Bk. IX. tain notable Mexicans, representing to them the damage they were receiving from the Spanish arms. They answered that this mattered not at all, provided the Spaniards were ruined; that if they were not killed by the Mexicans, they would infallibly perish of hunger shut up in the quarters. Cortés, having observed that night some carelessness among the citizens, sallied forth with some companies of soldiers, and making his way along one of the three principal streets, set fire to more than three hundred houses. (*)

The next day, when the engines had been repaired, he sallied out with them and with the greater part of his troops, and set out along the great causeway of Iztapalapa with better success than the first time; for in spite of the vigorous resistance which the enemy made in the entrenchments built by them against the fire of the Spaniards, he stormed the first four bridges, and set fire to some houses of that causeway, and took advantage of their materials to fill the ditches or canals, so that there might be no difficulty in the passage in case the Mexicans should take up the bridges. He left in the stormed posts a sufficient garrison, and returned to the quarters with many soldiers wounded, leaving ten or twelve dead.

On the following day he continued his attacks along the same causeway, stormed the three bridges that remained to him, and, pressing hard upon the enemy who defended them, reached the mainland. While he was busy having the last ditches filled in order to facilitate, as one must believe, his retreat from the court by that same causeway, by which he had already entered seven months before, he was told that the Mexicans wished to capitulate, and to hear their proposals he returned in haste to the quarters with the cavalry, leaving all the infantry to guard the

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.              R                   captured

(*) Cortés says that he burned the houses; but this does not mean that they all burned and were reduced to ashes, but only that he set fire to them, which in some did great damage, in others little, and in others none. Bernal Díaz says that it was hard work to make them burn, because they were covered with flat roofs and separated one from another. [p. 130] captured bridges. The Mexicans proposed to him that they were Bk. IX. ready to cause all hostilities to cease; but that to make the capitula‐

tion they had need of the person of a high priest, who had been taken

prisoner by the Spaniards when they delivered the assault on the tem‐

ple. Cortés immediately set him at liberty, and an armistice was

agreed upon. This seems to have been a mere stratagem of the elec‐

tors to recover that head of religion, whose person they needed for

the anointing of the new king, whom they had already elected, or

were by now about to elect; for hardly had Cortés enjoyed the plea‐

sure of the suspension of arms when some Tlaxcalans arrived there

bringing the news that the Mexicans had retaken the bridges and

killed some Spaniards, and that a great throng of warriors was coming

against the quarters. Cortés went to meet them with the cavalry, and

breaking through them with extreme toil and peril in order to make

his way, he recovered the bridges; but in the time in which he was

storming the last, the Mexicans had already taken from the Spaniards

the first four, and had begun to pull out the materials with which they

had filled the ditches. Cortés finally returned to storm them, and

withdrew to the quarters with all his men weary, battered, and

wounded.

Cortés in his letter to Charles V represents to him the great peril

he ran that day of losing his life, and ascribes to a particular provi‐

dence of the Lord his having escaped it amid so great a multitude of

enemies. It is certain that from the moment in which the Mexicans

rose against the Spaniards, they could have ruined them all together

with their allies, if they had observed a better order in fighting, and if

there had been greater concord among the subordinate chiefs who di‐

rected the attacks; but the chiefs were not in agreement, as we shall

see later, and the rabble was borne along only by the impulse of its tu‐

multuary fury. On the other hand it cannot be doubted that the

Spaniards seemed to be made of iron, since they yielded neither to

the rigor of hunger, nor to the need of sleep, nor to continual toil, nor

to wounds. After having spent the whole day

in [p. 131] in fighting with their enemies they passed the night burying the dead, tending the wounded, and repairing the damage caused by the Bk. IX. Mexicans during the day in the quarters, and even in the little time that they gave to necessary rest they never laid down their arms, ready at every moment to present themselves to the enemy. But the hardiness of those men will appear still more plainly in the terrible fights which we shall shortly relate.

In one of these days, probably June 30, King Moctezuma died

§ 19. within the quarters of the Spaniards, in the fifty-fourth year of his Death of King

Moctezuma II. and age, in the eighteenth of his reign, and in the seventh month of his of other lords. imprisonment. Concerning the cause and the circumstances of his death there is such variety and contradiction among the historians that it is utterly impossible to trace the truth. The Mexican historians blame the Spaniards for it, and the Spaniards blame the Mexicans. (z) I cannot persuade myself that the Spaniards resolved to take the life of a king to whom they owed so many benefits, and from whose death they could expect nothing but many evils. His loss was lamented, if we give credit to Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness and a most truthful author, no less by Cortés than by every one of the captains and soldiers, as that of their own father would be. He favored them infinitely, whether altogether out of his own inclination, or partly also from fear; he always showed himself well disposed toward them; at least there is no reason to believe the opposite, nor is it known that he ever spoke ill of the Spaniards, as they themselves declared.

R2                         His

(z) Cortés and Gómara affirm that Moctezuma died of the blow from a stone which his vassals dealt him on the head. Solís says that his death was caused by his not having been willing to have the wound treated. Bernal Díaz adds to such an omission voluntary fasting. The chronicler Herrera says that the wound was not mortal, but that he died of heartbreak and rage. Father Sahagún, and the Mexican and Texcocan historians, affirm that the Spaniards killed him, and one of them states the circumstance that a soldier ran him through the groin with a sword. Among these last historians some say that his death occurred on the night of the defeat of the Spaniards, others affirm that it happened earlier. Acosta, Torquemada, and Vetancurt leave it to the Divine judgment.

[p. 132]

His good and bad qualities may be understood from the account of Bk. IX.

his actions. He was circumspect, magnificent, liberal, zealous for jus‐

tice, and grateful for the services of his subjects; but his aloof circum‐

spection made the throne inaccessible to the complaints of his vas‐

sals. His magnificence and his liberality rested on the burdens of the

people, and his justice degenerated into cruelty. He was exact and

punctual in what concerned religion, and very zealous for the worship

of his gods and the observance of the rites. (A) In his youth he was

inclined to war, and courageous, and remained victor, so it is said, in

nine battles; but in the last years of his reign domestic delights, the

fame of the first victories of the Spaniards, and above all superstition

so abased his spirit that he seemed to have changed his sex, as his

subjects said. He took great delight in music and in hunting, and was

as skillful in the use of the bow and arrows as in that of the blowgun.

He was of good stature, of not very good complexion, with a longish

face and lively eyes.

At his death he left several children, of whom three perished in

the ill-fated night of the defeat of the Spaniards, either at the hands

of the Spaniards themselves, as the Mexicans affirm, or at the hands

of the Mexicans according to what the Spaniards say. Of those who

survived, the eldest was Yohualicahuatzin, who at baptism was named

Don Pedro Moctezuma, and from him descended the Counts of

Moctezuma and of Tula. Moctezuma had this son by Miahuaxochitl

(*)

daughter

(A) Solís says that Moctezuma scarcely bent his neck, that is, bowed his head, to his

gods, that he had a greater idea of himself than of the gods, etc. But these and other

like things that this historian affirms are altogether contrary to the truth and to the

testimony of the Indian and Spanish authors who knew that king. The same Solís

adds that the Devil favored him with frequent visits. But how could he favor so greatly

one who despised him? Such credulity does not befit a Chief Chronicler of the

Indies.

(*) Solís, altering, as is his wont, the name of this queen, calls her Niagua Suchil.

She survived the conquest, and took at baptism the name of Doña María

Miahuaxochitl. [p. 133] daughter of Ixtlilcuechahuac, lord of Tollan. By another wife he had Tecuichpotzin, a beautiful princess, from whom descend the two most Bk. IX. noble houses of Cano Moctezuma and of Andrada Moctezuma. Besides these we know that he had another son, who was lord of Tenayuca, who, having escaped and taken refuge in Tepotzotlán when the Spaniards came out defeated from Mexico, was afterwards solemnly baptized, being near death, toward the end of the year 1524 or at the beginning of 1525. (B) The Catholic Kings granted singular privileges to the posterity of Moctezuma in consideration of the incomparable service done them by that monarch in incorporating into the Crown of Castile, by his voluntary cession, a kingdom so great and so rich as that of Mexico. Happy he, if after having ceded his kingdom to the King of Spain he had gained for himself the kingdom of Heaven; but neither the repeated entreaties made to him by Cortés during the whole time of his imprisonment, nor the continual exhortations employed by Father Olmedo, especially in the last days of his life, sufficed to induce him to embrace the faith of Jesus Christ, (C) which was soon afterwards so readily embraced by his vassals. Most lofty counsels of predestination, which cannot be fathomed by mortals.

As

(B) This son of Moctezuma, lord of Tenayuca, took at baptism the name of his godfather Rodrigo de Paz, cousin of the Conqueror Cortés. The Spanish magistrates of that court attended the baptism, and the body of the said prince was buried with the pomp that befitted, in the church of St. Joseph of the Franciscan Fathers, the first parish of New Spain.

(C) Diego Muñoz Camargo, a Tlaxcalan noble, says in his manuscripts that Moctezuma was baptized a little before dying, and even names those who held him at baptism, namely, Cortés, Alvarado, and Olid; but this is without doubt false; for it cannot be believed that Cortés would not have made mention of it in his letter to Charles V, when it was of so much importance for his justification. Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, recalls the sorrow of Father Olmedo at not having been able to bring that king to Christianity. Gómara says that Moctezuma asked for baptism at Carnival of that year; that it was put off until Easter in order that it might be more solemn, and that it was then prevented by the arrival of Pánfilo de Narváez; but it is beyond doubt that by Easter the news of the fleet of Narváez had not yet reached Mexico: baptism could not therefore have been prevented by it.

[p. 134]

As soon as the king died, Cortés made it known to Prince Bk. IX.

Cuitláhuac by means of two illustrious prisoners who had been

present at his death, and shortly afterward had the royal corpse put

outside by six Mexican nobles, accompanied by several priests who

were likewise prisoners. (D) The sight of it excited a great lamenta‐

tion among the people (the last homage that they paid to their sov‐

ereign), who raised with praises his virtues to the very stars, the same

who a little before had found in him nothing but vices to be blamed.

The nobility, after shedding copious tears over the cold body of their

unfortunate king, carried it to a place in the city called by them

Copalco (E), where they burned it with the customary ceremonies, and

buried the ashes with the utmost reverence, although there were not

lacking some unworthy and abandoned men who insulted it with vile

abuse.

On this same occasion, if there is any truth in what several histori‐

ans relate, Cortés had thrown from the quarters into a place called

Tehuayoc the bodies of Itzcuauhtzin, lord of Tlatelolco, and of other

lords who were prisoners, whose names we do not know, all killed, ac‐

cording to what those historians affirm, by order of Cortés, although

no one states the motive of such a resolution, which, supposing it to

have been just, could not however be excused of imprudence; for the

sight of that massacre must necessarily irritate the indignation of the

Mexicans, and lead them to suspect that the Spaniards had likewise

killed

their

(D) Torquemada and other authors say that the body of Moctezuma was thrown

into the Tehuayoc together with the other bodies; but from the account of Cortés,

and from that also of Bernal Díaz, it appears that it was put outside the quarters on

the shoulders of the nobles.

(E) Herrera conjectures that Moctezuma was buried in Chapultepec, because the

Spaniards heard a great lamentation toward that quarter. Solís affirms positively that

he was buried in Chapultepec, and that the sepulcher of the kings was there; but all

this is entirely contrary to the truth, because Chapultepec was not distant less than

three miles from the quarters; so that it was impossible that the Spaniards should

hear the lamentation that was made there, especially as they were in the center of so

populous a city, and in a time of so much turbulence and noise. Moreover, the kings

had no fixed place for their sepulture, and in particular it is known to us from the

testimony of the Mexicans that the ashes of Moctezuma were buried in Copalco. [p. 135] their sovereign. (F) Whatever the truth of that deed, the Tlatelolcas Bk. IX.

carried the corpse of their lord upon a boat, and celebrated his obse‐

quies with great lamentation of that people.

Meanwhile the Mexicans continued their attacks with greater ar‐

dor. Cortés, although he made great slaughter of them, and had been

almost always victorious, nevertheless saw well that more was the

blood shed by his soldiers than the advantages arising from his victo‐

ries, and that in the end the want of provisions and munitions, and

the multitude of the enemy, must necessarily prevail over the bravery

of his troops and the superiority of his arms. Believing, therefore, the

prompt departure of the Spaniards to be absolutely necessary, he

called his captains to council to deliberate on the time and the man‐

ner of executing it. Their opinions were various. Some said that they

ought to depart by day, forcing their way with arms if the Mexicans

opposed them. Others wanted it done by night. This was precisely

the opinion of a soldier named Botello, who boasted of astrology, to

whom Cortés deferred more than was fitting, deceived by having seen

certain predictions of his accidentally fulfilled. It was resolved, then,

preferring the vain observations of that wretched soldier to the light

of military prudence, to go out by night with the greatest possible se‐

crecy, as if all their pains could suffice to conceal from the vigilance of

so great a number of enemies the march of nine thousand men with

their arms, their horses, their artillery, and their baggage. This was

fixed for the night of July 1 (G), a night ill-fated and memorable for

the Spaniards

on

(F) Concerning the death of those lords, Cortés, Bernal Díaz, Gómara, Herrera,

and Solís say nothing; but Sahagún, Torquemada, Vetancurt, and the Mexican histori‐

ans report it as certain. For my part, out of regard for these, and for the fidelity I

owe to the public, I report it too; but with some misgiving on account of the im‐

probability that I find in it.

(G) Bernal Díaz says that the defeat of the Spaniards took place on the night of

July 10; but I believe that this was a mistake of the printer [p. 136] on account of the great slaughter they suffered, for which they gave it

Bk. IX.

the name of noche triste, by which it is known to this day in their his‐

tories. Cortés had a wooden bridge made, which could be carried by

forty men, to use for the passage of the ditches. He then had all the

gold, the silver, and the gems that they had until then amassed

brought out; he took from it the fifth part, which belonged to their

king, and delivered it to the officers of His Majesty, protesting the im‐

possibility in which he found himself of keeping and saving it. He left

the rest to his officers and soldiers, that each might take what he

wished; but at the same time he warned them how much better it

would be to abandon everything to the enemy; for, free of that

weight, they could save their lives with less difficulty. Many of them,

not wishing to remain deprived of the principal object of their de‐

sires, and of the sole fruit of their labors, loaded themselves with that

precious burden, under whose weight they perished, victims no less

of their own avarice than of the vengeance of their enemies.

§ 20.    Cortés ordered his march in the greatest silence of the night, Terrible defeat of the which was made darker by the clouds that were there, and more trou‐

Spaniards in their

retreat. blesome and dangerous on account of a little rain that did not cease.

He gave the vanguard to the invincible Sandoval with other captains,

and with two hundred foot and twenty horse, the rear guard to Pedro

de Alvarado with the greater part of the Spanish troops. In the main

body of the army were conveyed the prisoners, the servants, the bag‐

gage; and Cortés with five horse and a hundred foot to give prompt

aid wherever the need should be greater. The auxiliary troops of

Tlaxcala, of Cempoala, and of Cholula, in which there were then

more than seven thousand men, were divided among the three parts

of the army. Having then implored the protection of Heaven, they

began to march along the causeway of Tlacopan. The greater

part

; for Cortés affirms that in their retreat they arrived at Tlaxcala on July 10, and from

the diary of their march written by this Conqueror it is plain that the defeat could

not have happened except on July 1. [p. 137] part of them passed the first ditch, or canal, happily, with the help of the bridge that they carried with them, without meeting any resis‐ Bk. IX. tance but the little that the sentinels, by whom that post was guarded, were able to make; but the priests, who were keeping watch in the temples, having become aware of it, cried to arms, and with their horns roused the people. In a moment the Spaniards found themselves assailed both by water and by land by an infinite number of enemies, who with their very multitude and disorder hindered one another in the attack. The fight at the second ditch was very terrible and bloody, the danger extreme, and extraordinary the efforts of the Spaniards to escape. The darkness of the night, the din of arms, the threatening cries of the combatants, the lamentable wailings of the prisoners, and the languid sighs of the dying formed a whole no less pitiable than horrible. Here was heard the voice of a soldier who called on his comrades for help, and there that of another who asked God for mercy. All was confusion, outcries, wounds, and slaughter. Cortés, like a good general, ran intrepidly hither and thither, passing and repassing the ditches often by swimming, encouraging some, helping others, and giving to the remnants of his scattered army all the order that could be given, not without great risk of being killed or taken prisoner. The second ditch was filled to such a degree with bodies that those of the rear guard passed over them. Alvarado, who commanded it, found himself at the third ditch so furiously pressed by the enemy that, being unable to face them, or to pass by swimming without evident danger of perishing at their hands, he planted his lance in the bottom of the ditch, and clasped its foot with his arms, and giving an extraordinary impulse to his body, leaped with one bound to the other side of the ditch. This leap, esteemed by all a prodigy of agility, gave to that place the name which it still keeps, of the Leap of Alvarado. (I)

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                      S                         The

(I) Bernal Díaz mocks those who believed such a leap of Alvarado’s; and says that it was altogether impossible, considering the breadth and depth [p. 138] The loss of the Mexicans on this ill-fated night was undoubtedly Bk. IX. great. Of that of the Spaniards, as in other computations, the authors

speak with the utmost variety. (L) I deem true the computation

which Gómara reports, as one who shows that he made diligent in‐

quiries and informed himself both from Cortés himself and from oth‐

er Conquerors, namely, that there perished more than four hundred

and fifty Spaniards, more than four thousand men of the auxiliary

troops, and among them, according to what Cortés says, all the

Cholulans. Killed also were all, (M) or nearly all, the prisoners, all the

men and women who were in the service of the Spaniards, and forty-

six horses, and there were lost all the riches that they had amassed, all

the artillery, and all the manuscripts of Cortés containing the account

of what had happened to the Spaniards until then. Among the

Spaniards who were lost the most notable were the captains Juan

Velázquez de León, an intimate friend of Cortés, Amador de Lares,

Francisco de Morla, and Francisco de Saucedo, all three men of great

courage and merit. Among the prisoners there perished the unfortu‐

nate King Cacamatzin, and a brother, a son, and two daughters of

King Moctezuma. (N) Accompanying

these

of that ditch; but the other authors report it as certain, and we find it confirmed by a

constant tradition.

(L) Cortés says that 150 Spaniards perished; but either he deliberately diminished

the number for private interests, or there was a mistake of the copyists, or of the first

printer of that letter. Bernal Díaz counts 870 Spaniards dead; but in this number he

includes, as he himself affirms, not only those who were killed on that ill-fated night,

but also those who perished in the following days until their arrival at Tlaxcala. Solís

numbers no more than 200, and Torquemada 290. As to the number of auxiliary

troops who perished there, Gómara, Herrera, Torquemada, and Vetancurt are in

agreement. Solís says only that more than a thousand Tlaxcalans were lost; but this

does not agree with the computation of Cortés, nor with that of other authors.

(M) Cortés affirms that all the prisoners were killed; but Cuicuitzcatzin must be

excepted, whom Cortés had already set on the throne of Acolhuacan; for we know

from the account of Cortés himself that this man was a prisoner, although we do not

know the reason, and on the other hand it is established for us that he was after‐

wards killed in Texcoco, as we shall see below.

(N) Torquemada affirms, as a thing well established, that a few days [p. 139] these princesses in their misfortune was Doña Elvira, daughter of the Tlaxcalan prince Maxixcatzin.                                             Bk. IX.

Cortés, in spite of the greatness of his heart, could not restrain his tears at the sight of such a calamity. He sat down upon a stone in Popotla, a village near Tlacopan, not to rest from his labors, but to weep for the loss of his friends and companions. Amid so many disasters he had at least the comfort of hearing that his bravest captains, Sandoval, Alvarado, Olid, Ordaz, Ávila, and Lugo, his interpreters Aguilar and Doña Marina, and his engineer Martín López were safe, in whom he chiefly trusted to be able to repair his honor and conquer Mexico.

The Spaniards found themselves in so sorry a state, weakened by fatigue and wounds, that if the Mexicans had pursued them, not one would have been left alive; but hardly had they arrived at the last § 21. ditch that was on that causeway on the lake, when they returned to The toilsome march

of the Spaniards. the city, either because they were content with the slaughter already made, or because, having found the bodies of the king of Acolhuacan, of the royal princes of Mexico, and of other lords, they occupied themselves in mourning their death and in performing their obsequies. The same without doubt they will have done with their relatives and friends who were dead, leaving that day the streets and the ditches clean, and burning the bodies before they should infect the air with their corruption.

At daybreak the Spaniards found themselves at Popotla, scattered, weary, and distressed, and Cortés having gathered and reordered them, they marched through the city of Tlacopan, incessantly harassed by some troops of that same city, and of Azcapotzalco as well, as far as Otoncalpolco, a temple situated on the top of a small mountain nine miles to the west of the capital, where at present is the

S2                  celebrated

after Cortés seized Cacamatzin, he had him strangled in prison. Cortés, Bernal Díaz, Vetancurt, and others say that he was killed together with the other prisoners on that memorable night. [p. 140] celebrated sanctuary and magnificent temple of Our Lady called de los

Bk. IX.

Remedios, or of Succor. Here they fortified themselves as far as their

means allowed, in order to defend themselves with less toil against

the enemy troops, who harassed them all day. At night they rested a

little, and had some refreshment furnished them by the Otomis of

two neighboring hamlets, who lived impatient under the yoke of the

Mexicans. From this place they began to make their way toward

Tlaxcala, their only refuge in so great a calamity, by Cuauhtitlan,

Citlaltepec, Xoloc, and Zacamolco, pressed along the whole road by

several flying bands of the enemy. At Zacamolco they found them‐

selves so famished, and reduced to such misery, that they ate for sup‐

per a horse killed that same day by the enemy, and the general himself

had his share. The Tlaxcalans threw themselves on the ground to eat

the grass that they found there, imploring the aid of their gods.

§ 22.      The next day, hardly had they set out on the road over the mounFamous battle of tain of Aztaquemecan when they saw from afar, in the plain of Tonan,

Otompan.

not far from the city of Otompan, a numerous and brilliant army, ei‐

ther of Mexicans, as the writers commonly say, or else composed, as I

believe, of the troops of Otompan, of Calpulalpan, of Teotihuacan,

and of other neighboring places gathered there at the suggestion of

the Mexicans. Some historians make this army amount to two hun‐

dred thousand men, a number judged by the Spaniards only by eye,

and perhaps magnified by their fear. These men persuaded them‐

selves, as Cortés himself attests, that that day was to be the last of

their lives. This general drew up his languishing troops, extending the

front of that wretched army, so that its flanks might remain in some

way covered by the small wings of the little cavalry that was left to

him, and with a countenance full of fire said to them: “We find our‐

selves in such straits that it is necessary to conquer or die. Take heart,

Castilians, and trust that He who has until now delivered us from so

many dangers will save us from this one also.” At last battle was

joined, which

was [p. 141] was very bloody, and lasted more than four hours. Cortés, seeing his

Bk. IX. troops diminish and in great part lose courage, and his enemies come on more proud notwithstanding the damage they received from the Spanish arms, took a bold and most perilous resolution, by which he obtained the victory, and put in safety the remnants of his wretched army. There came to his mind what he had often heard said, namely, that the Mexicans fell into disorder and fled whenever they saw their general dead, or the standard lost. Cihuacatzin, general of that army, clad in a rich military dress with a fine plume on his head, and with a gilded shield on his arm, was carried in a litter on the shoulders of some soldiers. The standard, which according to their custom he carried, was a net of gold fixed on the point of a pike, which he had strongly tied on his back, and which rose about ten palms above his head. (*) Cortés observed it in the center of that great multitude of enemies, and, resolved to deal a decisive blow, commanded his brave captains Sandoval, Alvarado, Olid, and Ávila to keep behind him to guard his back, and with others who accompanied him he hurled himself at the point where the enterprise seemed to him less difficult, with such impetus that he overthrew some to the ground with his lance, and others with his stirrups. Thus he advanced through the enemy ranks until he reached the general, who was accompanied by some officers, and with a thrust of his lance stretched him on the ground. Juan de Salamanca, a brave soldier who accompanied Cortés, dismounting promptly from his horse, put an end to his life, and taking from him the plume, presented it at once to Cortés. (O) The enemy army, as soon as it saw its general dead and the standard taken, was thrown into confusion

and

(*) This sort of standard was called by the Mexicans Tlahuizmatlaxopilli.

(O) Charles V granted some privileges to Juan de Salamanca, and among others that of a coat of arms for his house, in which was a plume in memory of the one that he had taken from General Cihuacatzin when he killed him. [p. 142] and began to flee. The Spaniards, encouraged by the glorious deed of Bk. IX. their leader, pressed hard upon them, making a great slaughter of

them.

This victory was one of the most famous that the Spanish arms

had in the New World. The Spanish general distinguished himself in

it above all, of whom his captains and soldiers said afterwards that

they had never seen so great a courage and so great an activity as that

which he displayed that day; but he received a grave wound in the

head, which, growing worse every day, brought his life into extreme

danger. Bernal Díaz justly praises the bravery of Sandoval, and shows

how large a part this famous officer had in the victory, giving courage

to all no less by example than by exhortations. María de Estrada, wife

of a Spanish soldier, has likewise been celebrated by the Spanish his‐

torians; armed with lance and buckler she ran amid the enemy host

wounding and killing with an intrepidity most strange in her sex. Of

the Tlaxcalans Bernal Díaz says that they fought like lions, among

whom Calmecahua, captain of the troops of Maxixcatzin, distin‐

guished himself. He took at baptism the name of Don Antonio, and

became famous, even more than for his bravery, for his very long life

of a hundred and thirty years.

The loss of the enemy was undoubtedly great, but much less than

that which several authors report, who make it amount to twenty

thousand men: a number altogether incredible considering the

wretched state to which the Spaniards were reduced, and the lack of

artillery and other firearms. On the contrary, the loss of the Spaniards

was not so small as Solís represents it; (P) for there perished almost

all

the

(P) Solís, in order to exaggerate the victory of Otompan, says that among those of

Cortés some were wounded, of whom two or three Spaniards died in Tlaxcala; but this au‐

thor, attentive only to polish of language, to eulogies, and to maxims, cared little for

figures. He affirms that Cortés took with him to Mexico after the defeat of Narváez

1100 men, who with another 80 who, as he says, remained with Alvarado, make [p. 143] the Tlaxcalans, and many Spaniards in proportion to the number of

Bk. IX. their troops, and all came out wounded.

The Spaniards, at length weary of pursuing the fugitives, resumed the road toward Tlaxcala by the eastern part of that plain, where they stayed that night in the open, and the general himself, after the fatigue and the wounds he had received that day, kept guard in person for their greater safety. The Spaniards were by now no more than four hundred and forty. Besides those who had been killed in the fights preceding the night of their ill-fated departure from Mexico, there perished in it and in the six following days eight hundred and seventy, as Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, often affirms, many of whom, having been taken prisoner by the Mexicans, were inhumanly sacrificed in the great temple of the capital.

The next day (July 8 (*), 1520) they entered, raising their hands to § 23.

Retreat of the heaven and thanking the Most High, the dominions of Tlaxcala, and Spaniards into arrived at Hueyotlipan (**), a considerable place of that republic. Tlaxcala. They feared also to find some change in the fidelity of the Tlaxcalans, knowing well how common it is among men to be abandoned in

their

1180. In the fights preceding the defeat of the Spaniards in Mexico, he scarcely mentions anyone dead. In the defeat he numbers only 200, and in the account of their journey to Tlaxcala he reports no others than those two or three who died in Tlaxcala of the wounds received at Otompan. Where, then, are, or how did they vanish, the other five hundred and more men who are wanting there to complete the number 1180? Quite another idea of the battle of Otompan is given us by those who were there, as can be seen in the letters of Cortés and in the True History of Bernal Díaz. “Oh, how furious and fearful to behold,” says this author, “was that battle! how they fought hand to hand, and with what fury the dogs (so he calls the enemy by way of insult!) attacked us! What wounds and slaughter they inflicted on us with their lances and swords, etc.,” and after a few lines he says thus: “I repeat that they wounded and killed many of our soldiers.”

(*) Bernal Díaz says that the battle of Otompan was on July 14; but this was a slip of memory; for Cortés affirms that they entered the dominions of Tlaxcala on the 8th, one day after that battle.

(**) Hueyotlipan is called by Cortés and by Herrera Gualipan, by Bernal Díaz Gualiopar, and by Solís Gualipàr. [p. 144] their calamities by their dearest friends; but they were soon undeBk. IX.

ceived, seeing their sincere demonstrations of esteem and of compas‐

sion for the past misfortunes. As soon as the news of their arrival was

had, the four heads of that republic went to Hueyotlipan to compli‐

ment them, accompanied by one of the principal lords of

Huexotzinco and by many nobles. Prince Maxixcatzin, although af‐

flicted by the death of his dear daughter Doña Elvira, tried to console

Cortés with the hope of vengeance, assuring him of finding it in the

courage of the Spaniards and in the forces of the republic, which

from that time he promised him, and the other lords offered the

same. Cortés thanked them for their singular goodwill, and taking the

standard that had been taken the day before from the Mexican gener‐

al, he presented it to Maxixcatzin, and to the other lords he gave

some valuable spoils. The Tlaxcalan women implored Cortés to

avenge the death of their sons and relatives, and vented their grief in

a thousand imprecations against the perfidy of the Mexicans.

Having rested three days in this place, they passed to the capital of

the republic, fifteen miles distant from there, to tend their wounds,

of which eight soldiers died. The concourse of people at their entry

into Tlaxcala was equal to, and perhaps even greater than, that which

there was then, when they first entered it. The reception that

Maxixcatzin gave them, and the care that he took of them, were wor‐

thy of his generous spirit and of his sincere friendship. The Spaniards

recognized themselves every day more obligated to that nation,

whose friendship, constantly cultivated, was the most efficacious

means not only for the conquest of the capital of the Mexican em‐

pire, but also for that of all the provinces that opposed the progress

of the Spanish arms, and for the subjugation of the barbarian

Chichimecs and Otomis, by whom the Conquerors were so much

harassed.

While the Spaniards rested in Tlaxcala from their fatigues, and

tended their wounds, the Mexicans were exerting themselves

to [p. 145] to remedy the ills of the court and of the kingdom. The losses they

Bk. IX. had suffered during the past year were indeed great and lamentable; for besides the great sums of gold, silver, gems, and other precious things which they had spent, partly in gifts made to the Spaniards and § 24. partly in homage to the King of Spain, of which they doubtless recov‐ Election    in Mexico,

and the measures of ered a certain amount, the reputation of their arms had been tar‐ King Cuitláhuac. nished, and respect for the Crown of Mexico diminished; the Totonacs and other peoples had withdrawn from their obedience; their enemies had grown more insolent; the temples were damaged and many houses of the capital ruined, and above all they lacked their king, many persons of the royal house, and a great part of the nobility. To these losses received from the Spaniards were added those which they brought upon themselves by the civil war, of which we owe our knowledge to the manuscripts of a Mexican historian who was at that time at that court and survived the ruin of the empire by some years.

While the Spaniards were in the court, distressed by hunger through the hostility of the Mexicans, several lords of the first nobility, either to favor the Spaniards’ party, or, what seems more probable, to succor their king, who, being among the Spaniards, must have suffered the same want, supplied them secretly with provisions, and perhaps even declared themselves openly in their favor, trusting in their own authority. For this reason so fatal a dissension broke out among the Mexicans that it could not be quenched except by the death of many illustrious persons, and particularly of Cihuacoatl, of Tzihuacpopoca, of Cipocatli, and of Tencuecuenotzin, some of them sons and others brothers of King Moctezuma, according to what the aforesaid historian affirms.

The Mexicans had need of setting at the head of their nation a man capable of restoring its honor and of repairing the losses suffered in the last year of Moctezuma’s reign. Prince Cuitláhuac was elected King of Mexico, either a little before or a little after the defeat of the Spaniards. He was, as we have already said, lord of Iztapalapa,

History of Mexico, Vol. III.                  T                     Privy [p. 146] privy councilor to King Moctezuma his brother, and Tlachcocalcatl, or Bk. IX.

general of the army. He was a wise man and of great talent, as his ene‐

my Cortés bears witness, liberal and magnificent like his brother. He

took great delight in architecture and in the cultivation of gardens, as

was seen in the magnificent palace which he built in Iztapalapa, and

in the celebrated garden which he planted there, both of which are

mentioned with great praise by nearly all the ancient historians. His

bravery and his military skill won him great esteem among his coun‐

trymen; some Spaniards well informed of his character affirmed that

if he had not been cut off by death, the capital would never have been

taken by the Spaniards. (Q) It is to be believed that the sacrifices

which were made at the festival of his coronation were of those

Spaniards whom he himself took prisoner on the night of their

defeat.

No sooner were the festivities of his coronation ended than he ap‐

plied himself to remedying the ills of the court and of the empire. He

gave orders to repair the damaged temples and to rebuild the ruined

houses, enlarged and improved the fortifications of the capital, sent

messengers to the provinces of the empire, encouraging them to the

common defense of the state against those foreign enemies, and

promised to relieve of every tribute those who should take up arms in

favor of the Crown. He sent also ambassadors to the republic of

Tlaxcala with

a

(Q) Solís gives Cuitláhuac the name of Quetlabaca, and says of him that he lived on

the throne but a few days, but such as sufficed to leave among his countrymen the memory of his

name almost effaced by his worthlessness. But this is false, and contrary to the account of

Cortés, Bernal Díaz, Gómara, and Torquemada, authors far better informed than

Solís. How could the memory of his name have been effaced among the Mexicans,

when it remained indelible among the Spaniards, who regarded him as the author of

their terrible defeat of July 1, as they themselves testify? Cortés so remembered

Cuitláhuac, and kept such resentment against him for those disasters, that when he

found himself with forces sufficient to undertake the siege of Mexico, wishing to

avenge himself on that king, and unable to take vengeance on his person, since he

had already died, he took it on the city he had favored. This was the motive, as

Cortés himself says, of his expedition against Iztapalapa. [p. 147] a fine gift of beautiful feathers, cotton garments, and salt, which were received with honor according to the laws established among the civi‐ Bk. IX. lized nations of that country. The purpose of that embassy was to §Embassy25.

of King represent to that senate that although the Mexicans and the Cuitláhuac to the

Tlaxcalans. Tlaxcalans had until then been mortal enemies, it was now necessary for them to unite, as natives of one and the same country, as peoples of one language, and as worshipers of the same gods, against the common enemies of the fatherland and of religion; that they had already seen the bloody slaughter which those inhuman and haughty strangers had made in Mexico and in other places, their sacrilegious attempts against the sanctuaries and against the venerable images of the gods, their ingratitude and perfidy toward his brother and predecessor and toward the most respected personages of the Anahuatlacas, and their insatiable hunger for gold, which led them to violate the most holy laws of friendship; that if the republic continued to second the perverse designs of those monsters, it would receive from them in the end the same recompense that king Moctezuma received for the humanity with which he welcomed them to his court and for the liberality with which he favored them for so long; that the Tlaxcalans would be detested by all nations for having given aid to such wicked usurpers, and that the gods would hurl down upon the republic the whole fury of their wrath for its being confederated with the enemies of their worship. If, on the contrary, the Tlaxcalans declared themselves, as he begged them, enemies of those men hated by Heaven and by Earth, the court of Mexico would make a perpetual alliance with them, and would thereafter have free trade with the republic, whereby the latter could escape the misery to which it had until then been reduced; all the nations of Anahuac would be obliged to them for so important a service; and the gods, appeased by the blood of such victims, would send the rain needed in their fields, would make their arms fortunate, and would render the name of the Tlaxcalans famous throughout the earth.

The senate, after hearing the embassy and dismissing

T2                     from [p. 148] from the audience hall the ambassadors, according to their custom, Bk. IX. remained there to deliberate on that great affair. There were not lack‐

ing some to whom the proposals of the court of Mexico seemed just

and conducive to the happiness of the republic, exaggerating the ad‐

vantages that were offered to them and, on the other side, the disas‐

trous outcome of the Spaniards’ enterprise in Mexico and the slaugh‐

ter made among the Tlaxcalan troops who had been there under their

orders. Among others the younger Xicotencatl raised his voice, who

had always been a mortal enemy of the Spaniards, and endeavored

with every argument he could to persuade them to the alliance with

the Mexicans, adding that it would be much better to preserve the

ancient customs of their nation than to submit to the new and extrav‐

agant usages of that untamed and imperious people; that he could not

imagine another occasion more opportune for freeing themselves en‐

tirely from the Spaniards than now, when they found themselves re‐

duced, weakened, and cast down. Maxixcatzin, who on the contrary

was sincerely attached to the Spaniards, and was endowed with a bet‐

ter mind for knowing the law of nations and a will better disposed to

observe it, rejected the opinion of Xicotencatl, condemning as an

abominable perfidy the counsel to sacrifice to the vengeance of the

Mexicans those men harassed by fortune, who had sought their asy‐

lum in Tlaxcala, trusting in the protestations and the demonstrations

of the senate and of the nation. He added that if they flattered them‐

selves with the advantages which the Mexicans offered, he hoped for

greater ones from the bravery of the Spaniards; that if there was rea‐

son not to trust the Spaniards, there was still less reason to trust the

Mexicans, of whose perfidy they had so many examples; finally, that

no other crime would be capable of so greatly provoking the wrath of

the gods and of obscuring the glory of the nation as such wickedness

against those innocent guests. Xicotencatl pressed his counsel by pre‐

senting to the senators a loathsome portrait of the character and cus‐

toms of the Spaniards. The altercation was so great, and so heated the

minds, that Maxixcatzin, carried away by anger, gave a push to

Xicotencatl

[p. 149]

Xicotencatl, and hurled him down some steps that were there, calling him

Bk. IX. seditious and a traitor to the fatherland. Such a demonstration, made by a man so circumspect and so respected and loved by the nation, obliged the senate to have Xicotencatl imprisoned.

The resolution taken there was to answer the embassy that the republic was ready to accept peace and friendship with the court of Mexico, provided it were not to be made by an action so unworthy and a crime so enormous as that of sacrificing its guests and friends; but when the ambassadors were sought in order to announce to them the senate’s answer, they had already secretly departed from Tlaxcala, because, having observed that rabble somewhat restless at their arrival, they feared that it might attempt something against the respect due to their character. It is to be believed, however, that the senate sent Tlaxcalan ambassadors to carry the answer to that court. The senators took pains to conceal from the Spaniards the motive of the embassy and all that had happened in the senate; but in spite of their diligence Cortés learned of it, and thanked Maxixcatzin, as was just, for his good offices, and offered to live up to the favorable idea he had of the bravery and friendship of the Spaniards.

Not content with such proofs of its great fidelity, the senate once more § 26.

Baptism of the four gave obedience to the Catholic King, and, what is more, the four heads of the lords of Tlaxcala. republic, moved by the grace of the Holy Spirit, renounced idolatry, and after being well instructed were baptized by Father Juan Díaz, chaplain of the Spanish army, with Cortés and his principal captains as their sponsors. This ceremony was celebrated with the greatest demonstrations of joy, both of the Spaniards and of the Tlaxcalans. Maxixcatzin was named at baptism Don Lorenzo, Xicotencatl the elder Don Vicente, Tlehuexolotzin Don Gonzalo, and Citlalpopoca Don Bartolomé. (R)

Some

Bk. IX.

vered in the faith, because they were not, as was needful, intimately

persuaded of the truth of Christianity.

Cortés was already delivered from the danger to which the blow

§ 27. received on the head in the last battle had exposed his life, and the Dismay of some

Spaniards. other Spaniards, except some who died of them, had recovered from

their wounds by the aid of the Tlaxcalan surgeons. During the time of

his illness Cortés had thought of nothing else but the means to be

employed to bring to a good end the great enterprise of the conquest

of Mexico, and to attain this he had caused a great quantity of timber

to be cut for the construction of thirteen brigantines; but while he

was forming these great projects, many of his soldiers were turning

over thoughts very different. They saw themselves diminished in

number, poor, in bad condition, and stripped of arms and horses.

They could not forget the terrible conflict and the tragic night of July

1, nor did they wish to expose themselves again to such misfortunes.

They fed one another’s ideas and fears, and blamed the obstinacy of

their general in so rash an enterprise. From private murmurings they

went on to make him a formal petition, wishing to oblige him with

many arguments to return to Veracruz, where they could procure re‐

inforcements of troops, arms, and munitions to undertake the con‐

quest with greater forces, which they then thought altogether impos‐

sible. Cortés was troubled by this novelty, which upset all his designs;

but with that talent which he had of persuading

his

mentions only that of Maxixcatzin, and Solís adds that of Xicotencatl. Some author

makes Father Olmedo the minister of the baptism, and some say that Maxixcatzin

received it in his last illness. But it is certain that all four heads of that republic were

baptized before the conquest, although Torquemada and Vetancurt do not agree on

the time. It is also known that Maxixcatzin did not put off baptism until his last ill‐

ness, and that the four lords were baptized not by Father Olmedo but by Father

Díaz. Besides other proofs, this is evident from the ancient paintings, made by the

Tlaxcalans, which were in several convents of the Franciscan fathers and were seen

by the historian Torquemada. [p. 151] his soldiers to whatever he wished, he made them an effective speech, by which he induced them to desist from their demand. He re‐ Bk. IX. proached them for that cowardice, awakened in their hearts the sentiments of honor, recalling to them in flattering terms their glorious deeds and the protestations, full of ardor and courage, which they had so often made to him; he made clear to them how much more dangerous their return to Veracruz would prove than their stay in Tlaxcala; he assured them of the fidelity of that republic, of which they still showed themselves doubtful; and finally he begged them to suspend their resolution until they should see the outcome of the war which he meant to make against the province of Tepeyacac, in which he hoped to have new proofs of the sincerity of the Tlaxcalans.

The lords of the province of Tepeyacac, bordering on the republic § 28.

War of the Spaniards of Tlaxcala, had declared themselves friends of Cortés and subjects of against the people of

Tepeyacac. the King of Spain ever since that horrible butchery which the Spaniards made in Cholula; but seeing the Spaniards afterward cast down and the Mexicans victorious, they returned to the obedience of the King of Mexico, and to win his favor they killed some Spaniards who were traveling from Veracruz to Mexico, unaware of the tragedy of their companions; they admitted a Mexican garrison into their towns, and occupied the road that led from Veracruz to Tlaxcala, and, not content with this, made some raids into the lands of that republic. Cortés resolved to make war upon them, no less to punish their perfidy than to secure the road from that port for the reinforcements which he expected from there. He was urged to such an expedition also by the younger Xicotencatl, who had already been restored to liberty through the mediation of the same Spanish general, and who, to remove any suspicion that might exist against him on account of what had happened in the senate, offered to aid him in that war with a good army. Cortés accepted the offer; but before taking up arms, he amicably requested some satisfaction from the people of Tepeyacac, and exhorted them to leave the Mexican party, promising to pardon them

the [p. 152] the crime committed in the killing of those Spaniards; but their proposals Bk. IX.

having been rejected by them, he marched against that province with four

hundred and twenty Spaniards and six thousand Tlaxcalan archers, while

Xicotencatl was gathering an army of fifty thousand men. At Tzimpantzinco,

a city of Tlaxcala, there joined them so many troops of that republic, of

Huexotzinco, and of Cholula, that it was believed there were as many as a

hundred and fifty thousand men.

Their first expedition was against Zacatepec, a place belonging to the con‐

federation of the people of Tepeyacac. Its inhabitants laid an ambush for the

Spaniards. They fought on both sides with great courage and obstinacy; but

the people of Zacatepec were in the end defeated, very many of them remain‐

ing dead on the field. (S) Then the army marched against Acatzinco, a city ten

miles southeast of Tepeyacac, which the Spaniards entered in triumph after

winning a battle little less difficult than that of Zacatepec. From Acatzinco

Cortés sent several detachments to burn some places in those surroundings

and to bring others under his obedience, and when it seemed to him time to

attack the principal city, he set out with his whole army toward Tepeyacac,

which he entered without any resistance from its citizens. There he declared

slaves many prisoners taken in that province, and had them branded with a

red-hot iron according to the barbarous usage of that century, applying the

fifth part to the King of Spain, as was done with everything they acquired,

and dividing the rest among the Spaniards and the allies. He founded there,

according to the manner of speaking of the Spaniards of that time, a city,

which he called Segura de la

Frontera
