The Xochimilcas, persuading themselves that their departure was an effect of fear, attacked the rear guard with loud cries; but they were so thoroughly beaten by the Spaniards that they did not dare to assail them again.

Cortés advanced with his army as far as Coyoacán, a large city situ‐ § 12. ated on the shore of the lake, six miles distant from Mexico toward March of the the south, with the intention of observing all those positions the bet‐ Spaniards   around the

lakes to Texcoco. ter to arrange the siege of the capital. He found the city deserted, and the next day went out from it to reconnoiter the road that led from that city as far as the causeway of Iztapalapa. He found there an entrenchment made by the Mexicans, and ordered the infantry to attack it, and in spite of the terrible resistance of the enemy who defended it they stormed it, ten Spaniards being wounded and some Mexicans killed. Having climbed the entrenchment, Cortés saw the causeway of Iztapalapa crowded with an innumerable multitude of enemies, and the lake with some thousands of boats, and after having observed

all [p. 190] all that suited his plan, he returned to the city, whose temples and

Bk. X.

houses he had set on fire. From Coyoacán the army marched to

Tlacopan, being harassed on the way by some flying troops of the ene‐

my, who attacked the baggage. In one of these fights, in which Cortés

ran great danger, two of his servants were taken prisoner, who, being

led to Mexico, were at once sacrificed. Cortés arrived at Tlacopan af‐

flicted by such a misfortune; but his grief was increased when from the

upper court of the great temple of that court he contemplated, togeth‐

er with other Spaniards, that fatal causeway on which some months be‐

fore he had lost so many of his friends and soldiers, and considered at‐

tentively the great difficulties that must be overcome in order to make

himself master of the capital. Some suggested to him that he should

send his troops along that causeway to commit some act of hostility

against the Mexicans; but he did not wish to expose them to so great a

risk, and so, without remaining longer in that city, he returned by way

of Tenayuca, Cuauhtitlan, Citlaltepec, and Acolman to Texcoco, having

in this journey gone around all the lakes of the Mexican valley, and ob‐

served all that was needed in order to carry out successfully the great

enterprise which he was meditating.

§ 13.    In Texcoco Cortés continued all the preparations for the siege. The Conspiracy against

Cortés. brigantines were already put in order, a canal a mile and a half long

completed, sufficiently deep, and on either side furnished with piles to

receive the water of the lake, into which the brigantines were to be

launched, and a machine built for launching them. (p) The troops

which Cortés had under his orders were innumerable, and the number

of the Spaniards also had considerably increased

with

(p) Gómara says that in that canal four hundred thousand men of the court and

kingdom of Texcoco worked; for in the fifty days that the work on that canal lasted,

eight thousand fresh laborers were employed every day. He adds that the aforesaid

canal was half a league in length, 12 feet in breadth, and where least, four Spanish

braccia in depth; but I believe that there is some mistake about the breadth, and I do

not doubt that it was more than 12 feet. [p. 191] with those who a few days before had come from Spain in a ship that

Bk. X. put in at the port of Veracruz, laden with horses, arms, and munitions of war. Everything seemed to point to a happy outcome, when the enterprise fell into the greatest danger of being ruined. Certain Spanish soldiers, partisans of the governor of Cuba, moved by hatred of Cortés, or by envy of his glory, or, what seems more likely, by fear of the perils that awaited them in the siege of the capital, agreed secretly to take the life of Cortés, of his captains Alvarado, Sandoval, and Tapia, and of all those whom they saw more attached to the party of that general. The conspirators had not only determined the time and the manner of carrying out the blow safely, but had even chosen those on whom the vacant offices of general, judge, and captains were to be conferred; but one of the accomplices, repenting of his crime, opportunely revealed the treachery to Cortés. The general immediately had Antonio de Villafaña, the head of the conspiracy, seized and committed the examination of the culprit to a judge; and when the culprit frankly confessed his crime, he was hanged by sentence of justice from a window of the quarters. Toward the accomplices Cortés prudently dissembled, pretending not to believe them guilty and attributing to the malice of Villafaña the infamy that resulted against them from his confession; but so that in the future his life might not be so exposed to such dangers, he created a bodyguard composed of several soldiers, of whose fidelity and courage he was well assured, who accompanied him by day and by night and kept constant watch over the preservation of his person.

The conspiracy having thus been prevented by the punishment of § 14. its principal culprit, Cortés applied himself with greater activity to Last   preparations for

the siege of Mexico. giving the last touch to his great enterprise. On April 28, after the Mass of the Holy Spirit had been celebrated, at which all the Spaniards received communion, and after the brigantines had been blessed by a priest, these were launched into the water, and, spreading their sails at once, began

to [p. 192] to sail over the lake to the firing of the artillery and muskets, which Bk. X.

was followed by the singing of the Te Deum with the music of the mil‐

itary instruments. All these demonstrations were owing to the great

confidence that Cortés had in the brigantines for the success of his

enterprise, without which, perhaps, he could never have brought it to

a good end. He then held a review of his army, and found in it eighty-

six horse and more than eight hundred Spanish foot, three large iron

cannon, fifteen smaller ones of copper, a thousand Castilian pounds

of musket powder, and a great quantity of balls and of arrows, the

number and the strength of his little army having been doubled by

the reinforcements that had come to him that year from Spain and

from the islands of the Antilles. To encourage them he made them an

address like the one he had already made when he left Tlaxcala. He

sent messengers to this republic, to Cholula, to Huexotzinco, and to

other cities, letting them know that the work on the brigantines was

already finished and begging them to send him within ten days as

many picked troops as they could, since the time had now come to

lay siege to that proud court, which for so many years had oppressed

their liberty. Five days before the feast of Pentecost the army of

Tlaxcala arrived at Texcoco, consisting, according to Cortés himself,

of more than fifty thousand men, under the command of several fa‐

mous chiefs, among whom came the younger Xicotencatl and the

valiant Chichimecatl, whom Cortés went out to meet with his men.

The troops of Huexotzinco and of Cholula passed there through the

mountains of Chalco, according to the order given them. In the two

following days other troops came from Tlaxcala and from other

neighboring places, which together with those mentioned made more

than two hundred thousand men, according to the testimony of their

leader Alonso de Ojeda.

On Whit Monday (May 20) Cortés assembled his men in the main

square of that court, to make the division of the army, to name the

commanders, and to assign to each of them the place where he was to

pitch

his [p. 193] camp, and the troops that were to be under him, and to publish anew the military edict already published in Tlaxcala. He ordered Pedro de Bk. X. Alvarado to encamp in the city of Tlacopan, to prevent any relief

§ 15. from entering by that side for the Mexicans, and assigned him thirty Disposition of the horse, a hundred and sixty-eight Spanish foot, distributed in three army     in the siege of

the capital. companies under as many captains, and twenty-five thousand Tlaxcalans with two cannon of artillery. Cristóbal de Olid was named master of the camp and head of the division destined for the city of Coyoacán, and there were assigned to him thirty-three horse, a hundred and sixty-eight Spanish foot, under three other captains, with two cannon, and more than twenty-five thousand allies. To Gonzalo de Sandoval were given twenty-four horse, a hundred and sixty-three Spanish foot under two captains with two cannon, and the allies of Chalco, Huexotzinco, and Cholula, who numbered more than thirty thousand; and Cortés ordered him to go first to ruin the city of Iztapalapa, and then to encamp in whatever place should seem to him most suitable for the purpose of pressing the Mexicans. Cortés, in spite of the remonstrances made to him by his captains and soldiers, took command of the brigantines, since he judged his own assistance more necessary in them. He distributed among the thirteen brigantines three hundred and twenty-five Spaniards and thirteen falconets, assigning to each brigantine a captain, twelve soldiers, and as many rowers; so that the whole army destined to begin the siege of the capital consisted of nine hundred and seventeen Spaniards and more than seventy-five thousand men of auxiliary troops (q), whose number shortly after

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.                   Bb                     increased

(q) Herrera and Solís count a hundred thousand allies assigned to the three camps; Bernal Díaz, on the contrary, counts no more than twenty-four thousand, eight thousand for each camp. We give greater credence to Cortés, as one who better than anyone else knew the number of troops assigned to each commander. Solís says that Bernal Díaz complains many times that the allies gave them more hindrance than help; but this is false, for on the contrary the said Díaz often boasts of the great help they had from the allies, and of their courage in fighting against the Mexicans. Our friends the Tlaxcalans, he says in ch. 151, helped us very well throughout the whole war, like courageous men. His whole History is full of such expressions, as may also [p. 194] increased, as we shall see, to two hundred thousand and more. All the other

Bk. X.

troops that had come to Texcoco either remained there to be employed

whenever need should arise, or returned to their own places, since they were

not so far distant from the capital that they could not come promptly to the

siege whenever they were summoned.

§ 16.

Olid and Alvarado departed together from Texcoco with their troops to go Execution of to the posts assigned them by the general. Among the most distinguished Xicotencatl. Tlaxcalans who accompanied Alvarado were the younger Xicotencatl and his

cousin Piltecuhtli. The latter was wounded in a quarrel that arose by a

Spaniard, who, imprudently disregarding the orders given them by the general

and the respect due to that personage, might have brought about by his at‐

tack the desertion of the Tlaxcalans. The outrage greatly vexed them, and

they made their indignation plain in certain demonstrations. Ojeda, their

leader, tried to calm them, and gave Piltecuhtli leave to go and be treated in

his native city. Xicotencatl, to whom, no less because of his position than be‐

cause of his kinship, such an injury was more keenly felt than to anyone else,

finding at the time no other way of avenging himself, secretly abandoned the

army, and together with other Tlaxcalans took the road to his native city.

Alvarado promptly gave notice of it to Cortés, and the latter ordered Ojeda

to overtake and seize him, and when he had been seized had him hanged pub‐

licly, either in the same city of Texcoco, (r) as Herrera and Torquemada say,

or

be seen from the letters of Cortés and the narrative of other historians. Bernal Díaz only says

that in their retreats along the causeway of Tlacopan they were hampered by the auxiliary

troops; but any numerous troop that wishes to make its retreat along a narrow road is ham‐

pered by its very multitude.

(r) Cortés makes no mention of this event of Xicotencatl: it may be believed that he had

some particular reason for passing over it in silence. Bernal Díaz affirms that Xicotencatl was

going to Tlaxcala to seize the state of Chichimecatl while the latter was at the war; but this is

altogether improbable. There are authors who say that he was drawn to Tlaxcala by the love of

a lady. In my account of this event I follow Torquemada and Herrera, because they wrote on

the manuscripts of Ojeda and of Camargo, who were quite well informed. To Solís it seems

incredible [p. 195] or in a place near it, as Bernal Díaz affirms, a crier having first published the cause of his sentence, which was that of having deserted Bk. X. and of having stirred up the Tlaxcalans against the Spaniards. It is to be believed that Cortés did not risk the execution of such a punishment without having first obtained, as Herrera expressly affirms, the consent of the senate of Tlaxcala; which was not difficult, considering their severity in punishing crimes even in the most conspicuous persons, and the particular hatred they bore that prince, whose pride they could no longer endure. So resounding a punishment, which should naturally have stirred up the minds of the Tlaxcalans against the Spaniards, so terrified both them and the other allies that from then on they observed the laws of military service more punctually and kept themselves more subordinate to those foreign leaders, the Spaniards drawing profit even from their own faults; but the Tlaxcalans did not fear to make some demonstrations of esteem and veneration toward their prince, weeping for his death, distributing among themselves his garments as precious relics, and celebrating, as is to be believed, his obsequies with due magnificence. The family and goods of Xicotencatl were adjudged to the King of Spain and carried to Texcoco. In the family there were thirty wives, and among the goods a great quantity of gold.

Alvarado and Olid continued their march toward Tlacopan, whence they went on to break the aqueduct of Chapultepec

Bb2                            to

that Xicotencatl was put to death in Texcoco: “because it would have been too great a risk for Cortés to resolve upon so violent an execution under the eyes of so great a number of Tlaxcalans, to whom the ignominious punishment inflicted on one of the foremost men of their nation must necessarily have been distressing;” but Cortés risked far more in imprisoning King Moctezuma in his own court, and under the eyes of an incomparably greater number of Mexicans, to whom that great affront done to the first man of their nation must without fail have been distressing. If in the Conquest of Mexico no other deeds equally rash had intervened, the reasoning of Solís would perhaps be effective; besides which, Cortés did not, according to Herrera, give sentence against Xicotencatl without the consent of the senate of Tlaxcala, and I do not doubt that in the name of that same senate it was published. [p. 196] to deprive the Mexicans of water; but they could not carry out so im‐

Bk. X. portant a hostile act without great resistance from the enemy, who,

foreseeing this blow, had made their preparations for defense both by

land and by water. These were defeated, and the Tlaxcalans, pursuing

§ 17. First hostilities of the them, killed twenty of them and took seven or eight prisoners. This Spaniards, and begin‐

ning of the siege of first step having been happily accomplished, those commanders re‐

the capital. solved to go by the causeway of Tlacopan to storm some ditch; but so

great was the multitude of Mexicans who came against them, and so

thick the cloud of arrows, darts, and stones that they shot at them,

that they killed eight Spaniards and wounded more than fifty, and

with difficulty could the latter retire, ashamed, to Tlacopan, where

Alvarado encamped in accordance with the order of the general,

while Olid marched to Coyoacán on May 30, which was consecrated

that year to the solemnity of Corpus Christi, and on which the siege

of the capital began, according to Cortés's reckoning.

Meanwhile, while Alvarado and Olid were busy filling in some

ditches that were on the shore of the lake and repairing some passes

for the convenience of the cavalry, the commander (*) Sandoval, with

the aforesaid number of Spaniards and with more than thirty-five

thousand allies, set out from Texcoco on May 31 with the purpose of

taking by assault the city of Iztapalapa, against which Cortés was par‐

ticularly bent. Sandoval entered it, making a terrible slaughter with

fire in the houses and with arms upon the inhabitants, who in terror

sought to save their lives in the boats. Cortés, in order to attack at

the same time that part of the city which was in the water, after hav‐

ing had the whole lake sounded, embarked with his men in the brig‐

antines and sailed with sails and oars toward Iztapalapa. He cast an‐

chor near an isolated hillock, not far from that city, whose summit

was occupied by many enemies

resolved

(*) Solís says that Cristóbal de Olid and Gonzalo de Sandoval marched together

from Texcoco; but this was a mistake of that author, who should have said Pedro de

Alvarado in place of Gonzalo de Sandoval. [p. 197] resolved to defend themselves and to injure the Spaniards as much as

Bk. X. they could. (s) Cortés landed there, and overcoming with a hundred and fifty men the roughness and difficulty of the ascent and the resistance of the enemy, he took the hill by storm, with the killing of all who defended it. (t) But hardly had they taken it when they saw a great fleet of boats (u) coming against them, summoned by the signals of the smoke that at the first appearance of the brigantines had been made both on that hill and in some temples of those surroundings. The Spaniards embarked at once and stood on the defensive without moving until, aided by a strong wind that opportunely arose, and increasing the speed of the brigantines by the impulse of the oars, they dashed against the boats, breaking some and capsizing others by the shock. Some of the enemy perished wounded by the balls, and many were drowned. All the other boats fled, pursued for more than eight miles by the brigantines as far as the capital.

The commander Olid, as soon as he saw from a temple of Coyoacán the fight of the brigantines, marched with his troops in battle order along the causeway that led to Mexico, took by storm some ditches and entrenchments, and killed many of the enemy. Cortés for his part that evening gathered his brigantines,

and

(s) On the top of that hillock the historian Solís built a fortress quite spacious. I say that he built it, because there is no record in any other historian that there ever was any fortress there, large or small. Cortés himself, who boasts of his victory, makes no mention of anything but the entrenchments that were there.

(t) Solís says that Cortés spared the lives of the greater part of those who defended the hillock of the lake; but Cortés himself affirms that not one of them escaped with his life. This hill, in memory of the victory won by Cortés, has been called from then until now the Peñón del Marqués, that is, the Marquis’s rock.

(u) Bernal Díaz says that the fleet that came against Cortés was composed of all the boats to be found in Mexico and in all the places situated on the lake, but this is a wild hyperbole. Solís affirms that that fleet consisted of four thousand canoes; but Cortés, who commanded the brigantines and had a greater interest than either Bernal Díaz or Solís in exaggerating the number of the enemy boats in order to make his victory more famous, says only that they were more than five hundred. [p. 198] and went with them to attack the bulwark, which was situated, as we Bk. X.

have already said, at that angle which the causeway of Coyoacán

formed with that of Iztapalapa. He fought by water and by land, and

in spite of the bravery with which the Mexican garrison that was

there defended itself, he nevertheless took it by storm, and with two

great cannon of artillery made a horrible slaughter in the multitude

that filled the lake and the causeway. That place, called Xoloc by the

Mexicans, seemed very advantageous to Cortés (x) for establishing his

camp there, and in fact it was not easy to find another more suited to

his designs; for in it he made himself master of the principal cause‐

way, and of that part of the lake by which greater relief could enter

the city, and besides, of the causeway of Coyoacán, for communica‐

tion with Olid’s camp. The short distance of that place from the

camps of Coyoacán and of Tlacopan was of great importance to

Cortés, in order to give his orders promptly and to bring aid wherever

it was needed. Finally, the nearness to Mexico helped to facilitate the

assaults. (y)

There he gathered the brigantines, and, abandoning the expedi‐

tion against Iztapalapa, took the resolution to begin the assaults at

once. He therefore had half the troops of Coyoacán brought to his

camp, and fifty picked foot from the troops of Sandoval. That night a

great crowd of enemies was heard coming toward that camp. The

Spaniards, knowing that the Mexicans were not accustomed to fight

at night unless they were sure of victory, were terrified at first; but al‐

though they suffered some damage from the enemy,

they

(x) Father Sahagún in his manuscript History of the conquest says that Cortés, by

means of certain persons of note among his prisoners, summoned the king and the

nobility of Mexico to a place called Acachinanco, and he sets down the harangue that

he made, setting forth to them the motives of the war; but such a conference seems

to me neither true nor probable. Cortés, who relates in detail what he said to the

Mexicans and what the Mexicans said to him, would certainly not have omitted a

thing so notable.

(y) Vetancurt gives it to be understood that Cortés encamped inside the city; but

this is altogether contrary to the truth, and to the account of Cortés himself, who af‐

firms that his camp was half a league distant from the city. [p. 199] they nevertheless forced them, with the fire of the artillery and mus‐ Bk. X. kets, to retire to the city. The next day they found themselves assailed by a prodigious multitude of warriors, who with frightful shouts magnified their number in the imagination of the Spaniards. Cortés, the relief that he was awaiting from Coyoacán having opportunely arrived, made a sortie with his men drawn up in battle order. They fought on both sides with great courage and obstinacy; but the Spaniards and their allies took a ditch and an entrenchment, and with the artillery and the horses did the Mexicans so much harm that they obliged them to take refuge in the city; and because on the side of the lake that lay to the west of the causeway they were troubled by the Mexican boats, Cortés had a ditch of the causeway widened so that the brigantines could pass through it, and these hurled themselves impetuously against them, pursued them as far as the city, and set fire to some houses of the suburbs.

Meanwhile Sandoval, having happily completed, though not without great risk, the expedition of Iztapalapa, marched with his troops toward Coyoacán. On the road he was attacked by the troops of Mexicaltzinco; but he defeated them, and had fire set to their city. Cortés, aware of his march and of a great ditch that had recently been made in the causeway, sent him two brigantines to facilitate the passage of the army. The latter marched toward Coyoacán, and Sandoval went with ten horse to the camp of Cortés. When he arrived there he found those Spaniards in combat with the Mexicans. The fatigue of the journey and of the battle of Mexicaltzinco did not suffice to keep him from the fray. He fought as usual with courage, but in the fighting his leg was pierced by a dart, and along with him many other Spaniards were also wounded. These advantages gained by the Mexicans are not to be compared with the loss that they suffered that day, nor with the fear that the fire of the artillery caused them, which was so great that for many days they did not dare to approach the camp of the Spaniards. These spent six days in continual

fights [p. 200] fights: the brigantines, circling around the city, set fire to many hous‐

Bk. X.

es of the suburbs, and in their forays discovered a large and deep

canal by which they could enter the city at ease; which was of great

advantage to the Spaniards in the sequel.

Alvarado for his part pressed the Mexicans as hard as he could,

taking in frequent skirmishes some ditches and entrenchments of the

causeway of Tlacopan; but some of his men were killed and many

wounded. He observed that by the causeway of Tepeyacac, situated

toward the north, relief was continually introduced into the city, and

he perceived that by that causeway escape would be easy for the be‐

sieged, whenever they should find themselves unable to resist the be‐

siegers any longer. He promptly made this known to Cortés, and the

latter commanded Sandoval to go with a hundred and eighteen

Spanish foot and a very great number of allies to occupy that place,

and thereby prevent the relief that came to the enemy. Sandoval

obeyed, though still suffering from the wound in his leg, and took

possession of that place without opposition, every communication by

land with the other cities being from then on cut off for the

Mexicans. (*)

§ 18. First entry of the be‐     This done, Cortés determined to make on the following day an ensiegers into the city. try into the city with more than five hundred Spaniards and more

than eighty thousand allies from Texcoco, from Tlaxcala, from

Chalco, and from Huexotzinco, leaving some cavalry with ten thou‐

sand allies to guard the camp; and ordering Sandoval and Alvarado

each to enter by his own causeway at the same time with their troops,

that did not

number

(*) Dr. Robertson says: “that Cortés resolved to assail the city from three different

quarters: from Texcoco on the eastern side of the lake, from Tacuba on the west, and

from Cuyocan (that is, Coyoacán) toward the south. These towns, he adds, were situ‐

ated on the principal causeways that lead to the capital, and that are made for its de‐

fense. He gave Sandoval the command of the first, &c.” But this is an error; for on

the east there was not, nor could there be, any causeway, because of the depth of the

lake. Sandoval encamped not in Texcoco, from which it was impossible to assail

Mexico, but in Tepeyacac toward the north. [p. 201] number fewer than eighty thousand men. Cortés marched along his

Bk. X. causeway with his numerous army well ordered and flanked by the brigantines, and having scarcely gone a little way he came upon a wide and deep ditch and an entrenchment more than ten feet high. The Mexicans courageously opposed their passage, but, driven back by the artillery of the brigantines, the Spaniards passed on, pressing them as far as the city, where they found another great ditch and a strong and high entrenchment. The force of the water in this ditch, the throng of enemies who had gathered to the defense, their frightful and threatening cries, and the thick rain of arrows, darts, and stones that they shot, held up for some time the resolution of the Spaniards; but having finally, with the discharge of all the artillery and of all the firearms, driven from the entrenchment those who defended it, the army passed over it, and advanced taking other ditches and entrenchments as far as a principal square of the city, which was full of people. In spite of the slaughter that they saw being made in the multitude by a great cannon planted at the entrance of the square, the Spaniards did not dare to enter it, until the general himself, reproaching them for that ignominious fear, and hurling himself intrepidly against the enemy, put courage into his soldiers. The Mexicans, terrified by so great an intrepidity, fled into the enclosure of the great temple, and seeing themselves still assailed there, took refuge in the upper courts of the temples, where they were likewise pursued; but suddenly the Spaniards found themselves attacked from behind by other Mexican troops, and put in such a strait that, being unable to sustain the fury of the enemy either within the enclosure of the temple or outside in the neighboring square, they were forced to retire to the causeway by which they had entered the city, leaving the cannon of artillery in the power of the enemy. Shortly after, three or four horse arrived opportunely in the square, and the Mexicans, persuaded that all the cavalry was coming against them, were thrown into confusion by the fear they had of those great and fiery beasts, and abandoned ignominiously

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.        Cc                       the [p. 202] the temple and the square, which were occupied without delay by the

Bk. X.

Spaniards. Ten or twelve Mexican nobles had fortified themselves in

the upper court of the great temple; but in spite of their obstinate re‐

sistance they were overcome and killed by the assailants. The Spanish

army in its retreat set fire to the largest and finest houses of the

causeway of Iztapalapa, though not without very grave danger from

the impetuosity with which the Mexicans fell upon the rear guard,

and from the harm that they did it from the flat roofs. Alvarado and

Sandoval with their troops made a great slaughter of the Mexicans,

and the allies earned on that day great praises from the Spanish

general.

§ 19.     The forces of the Spaniards increased every day so much through Increase of the auxil‐ fresh reinforcements and through new alliances of cities and of entire

iary troops of the

Spaniards. provinces that, whereas at the beginning there were in their three

camps no more than ninety thousand men, in a few days they reached

two hundred and forty thousand. The new king of Texcoco, to show

Cortés his gratitude, sought to win over to him all the nobility of his

kingdom, and on this occasion raised an army of fifty thousand men,

which he sent to the aid of the Spaniards under the orders of a prince

his brother, who received at baptism the name of Don Carlos

Ixtlilxochitl, (z) a young man to whose bravery the ancient historians

bear clear testimony, and among others Cortés himself, who vaunts

the timeliness and the importance of such aid. That prince remained

with thirty

thousand

(z) Cortés calls him Istrisuchil; Bernal Díaz and Solís, altering the name still more,

call him Suchil. Torquemada, contradicting himself, says that this young man was

Coanacochtzin, elder brother of Don Fernando Ixtlilxochitl, and a few pages later

makes this same Coanacochtzin chief councilor of King Cuauhtémoc during the

siege of the capital; but it is certain that the young leader of the Texcocan army was

Don Carlos Ixtlilxochitl, to whom, after the death of his brother Don Fernando

Cortés Ixtlilxochitl, following the conquest, the Conqueror Cortés gave the investi‐

ture of the state of Texcoco. Coanacochtzin remained at the court of Mexico from

the beginning of this year until the conquest. He was taken prisoner together with

King Cuauhtémoc, and was likewise executed with him three years later at

Izancanac, when both were traveling with Cortés toward Comayagua. [p. 203] thousand men in Cortés’s camp, and the other twenty thousand were

Bk. X. divided among the camps of Sandoval and of Alvarado. This aid from the Texcocans was soon followed by the confederation of the Xochimilcas and of the mountain Otomis with the Spaniards, which new troops increased Cortés’s army by twenty thousand men.

Nothing more was wanting to this general for the completion of the siege except to prevent the relief that was being introduced into the city by water. Keeping therefore seven brigantines, he sent the other six toward that part of the lake that lay between Tlacopan and Tepeyacac, so that from there they could easily give aid to the camps of Sandoval and of Alvarado, whenever those commanders should require it; and while not employed by them, should go cruising two by two, and try to capture all the boats that carried provisions or troops to the city.

Cortés, now finding himself with so great a number of allied troops, determined to make within three days an entry into the city. He gave therefore the necessary orders, and on the appointed day marched with the greater part of his cavalry, with three hundred Spanish foot, with seven brigantines, and with an innumerable multitude of allies. They found the ditches opened, the entrenchments rebuilt, and the enemy well prepared to resist; nevertheless, with the aid of the brigantines they took all the ditches and entrenchments that were there as far as the principal square of Tenochtitlan. There the army halted, Cortés not permitting them to advance further without leaving leveled all the difficult passes that he had taken; but while ten thousand allies were occupied in filling the ditches, the others applied themselves to burning and ruining some temples, houses, and palaces, and among others that of King Axayacatl, where the Spaniards had formerly had their quarters, and the celebrated palace of the birds of King Moctezuma. After these hostile acts, accomplished with great difficulty and with grave danger because of the efforts the Mexicans made to prevent them, Cortés sounded the retreat, which was carried out happily,

Cc2                  though

[p. 204]

Bk. X.

though the rear guard was incessantly harassed by the enemy troops.

Alvarado and Sandoval did the same on their part. This day was with‐

out doubt one of great fatigue for the Spaniards and their allies, but

also of unspeakable grief for the Mexicans, as much for the loss of so

many fine buildings as for the taunts with which they were insulted

by their own vassals confederated with the Spaniards, and by the

Tlaxcalans, their mortal enemies, who in fighting showed them the

arms and legs of the Mexicans whom they had killed, and threatened

to eat them that night at supper, as in fact they did.

§ 20.     The next day, early, in order not to give the Mexicans time to dig New entries into the out the filled ditches and to repair the demolished entrenchments,

city.

Cortés sallied from his camp in the same manner as the day before;

but in spite of his diligence the Mexicans had already rebuilt the

greater part of the destroyed fortifications, and defended them with

such obstinacy that the army of the besiegers could not take them ex‐

cept after a furious fight of five hours. The army advanced and took

two ditches of the causeway of Tlacopan; but as the day was now

about to end, it withdrew to its camp, still fighting the enemy troops,

who were pressing upon the rear guard. The same combats that

Cortés’s army had were had by those of Sandoval and of Alvarado, the

besieged having to fight at one and the same time against three most

numerous armies, superior to them in arms, in horses, in brigantines,

and in military discipline. Alvarado on his part had already ruined all

the houses that stood on either side of the causeway of Tlacopan; (A)

for the population of the capital continued on that side up to

the

(A) These houses were not built on the causeway itself, but near it on the islets

that were on either side. We do not know that there was on the causeway any other

building than a temple, which was at that point where, the causeway widening, it

formed a little square. This temple was taken by Alvarado, who kept a garrison in it

almost all the time of the siege. [p. 205] the mainland, as Cortés and Bernal Díaz attest.

Cortés would have liked to spare his troops the great fatigue of re‐ Bk. X. peating every day the fights to take the same ditches and the same entrenchments; but neither could he leave a garrison there to preserve what had been gained without sacrificing it to the fury of the enemy, nor did he wish to encamp inside the city, as some of his captains advised him; because, besides the continual assaults that they would have to endure day and night from the enemy, they could not from there so easily prevent the relief that came to the city as from the post of Xoloc.

While this relief was failing the besieged, that of the besiegers was § 21.

Confederation of sevincreasing, who at this very time received one as advantageous to eral cities of the lake them as it was harmful to the enemy. The inhabitants of the cities sit‐ with the Spaniards. uated on the shore and on the islets of the lake of Chalco had until then been enemies of the Spaniards, and could have done much harm to Cortés’s camp if their troops had assailed it from one side of the causeway at the same time that the Mexicans assailed it from the other; but they had attempted no hostility against the Spaniards, perhaps because they reserved it for a more opportune occasion. The Chalcas and other allies, to whom the neighborhood of so many enemies was not convenient, tried to draw them to their party, now with promises and now with threats and vexations, and so much did their importunity avail, and perhaps also the fear of vengeance from the Spaniards, that there came to Cortés’s camp to confederate with him the nobles of Iztapalapa, of Mexicaltzinco, of Colhuacan, of Huitzilopochco, of Mixquic, and of Cuitláhuac, which cities were a considerable part of the Mexican valley. Cortés rejoiced infinitely at that alliance, and asked of them not only that they should help him with their troops and their boats, but also that they should bring materials for making huts along that causeway, because, it being the rainy season, his men suffered too much from lack of shelter.

All

[p. 206]

All this was so well carried out by them that they immediately placed Bk. X. under Cortés’s orders a considerable body of troops, whose number is

not stated, and three thousand boats to help the brigantines in their

course, in which they promptly transported the necessary materials,

and built so many huts that all the Spaniards could stay in them com‐

fortably, together with two thousand Indians employed in their ser‐

vice; for the bulk of the allied troops was encamped at Coyoacán,

four miles distant from Xoloc; and, not content with such great aid,

they brought to Cortés’s camp many provisions, and chiefly fish and

cherries in great quantity.

Cortés, finding himself thus with his forces so increased, entered

the city with them two or three days in succession, making a consid‐

erable slaughter of the citizens. He persuaded himself that the be‐

sieged must surrender, seeing themselves faced with so excessive a

number of troops and experiencing the pernicious effects of their ob‐

stinate resistance; but he was deceived, for the Mexicans were re‐

solved to lose their lives before their liberty. He resolved, however, to

make continual entries into the city in order to compel them by in‐

cessant hostilities to ask for the peace that they refused. He formed

of his vessels two flotillas, each composed of three brigantines and of

fifteen hundred boats, ordering them to approach the city, to set fire

to the houses, and to do the Mexicans all the harm possible. He or‐

dered Sandoval and Alvarado to do the same on their part, and he

himself, with all his Spaniards and, as it seems, with eighty thousand

allies (B), marched as usual along the causeway of Iztapalapa toward

the city, without being able to obtain, either on this or on other en‐

tries of those days, any other advantages than that of gradually dimin‐

ishing the number of the enemy, ruining some buildings,

and

(B) I conjecture that the allied troops who accompanied Cortés on this entry were

eighty thousand men, because Cortés affirms that “that day there were more than a

hundred thousand in his camp,” of whom twenty or twenty-two thousand must have

been employed in the two fleets of boats. [p. 207] and advancing every day somewhat farther, with the aim of opening communication with Alvarado’s camp, though at that time it was not Bk. X. possible for him to obtain it.

Alvarado with his troops, seconded by the brigantines, had already § 22.

Operations of taken a temple that was in a little square of the causeway of Tlacopan, Alvarado, and the

prowess of in which from then on he maintained a garrison in spite of the violent Tzilacatzin. assaults of the Mexicans. He had likewise taken some ditches and entrenchments, and knowing that the greatest strength of the enemy was in Tlatelolco, where King Cuauhtémoc resided, and where an infinite number of people from Tenochtitlan had taken refuge, he directed his operations toward that quarter; but although he fought with all his forces by land and by water, he could not advance as far as he wished, because of the stubborn opposition of the besieged, in which fights many perished on both sides.

In one of the first skirmishes a sturdy and courageous Tlatelolca showed himself, disguised as an Otomi, with an Ichcahuepilli, or cotton cuirass, and without other arms than a shield and three stones, and running very swiftly toward the besiegers he hurled the three stones in succession with such dexterity and such force that with each one he struck down a Spaniard, causing no less indignation to the Spaniards than fear and wonder to their confederates. They used every diligence to get him into their hands; but they could never catch him, because in all the fights he appeared differently dressed, and in all did great harm to the besiegers, having moreover as much speed in his feet to save himself as strength in his arms to attack. The name of this celebrated Tlatelolca was Tzilacatzin.

Alvarado, puffed up by some advantages gained over the Mexicans, wished one day to advance as far as the market square: he had already taken some entrenchments and some ditches, and among others one that was more than fifty feet wide and more than seven deep, and, forgetting in his good fortune to have it filled up, as his general had ordered him, he advanced with forty or fifty Spaniards and some allies.

The

[p. 208]

The Mexicans, perceiving his carelessness, fell upon them at once, de‐

Bk. X. feated and put them to flight, and in recrossing the ditch killed some

allies and took four Spaniards prisoner, who were immediately sacri‐

ficed, in sight of Alvarado and his men, in the great temple of

Tlatelolco. This misfortune greatly vexed Cortés, as one that was

bound to increase the spirit and the pride of the enemy, and he be‐

took himself at once to Tlacopan to reprove Alvarado severely for his

disobedience and his rashness; but being informed of the courage

with which he had conducted himself on that day and of the fact that

he had made himself master of the most difficult posts, he contented

himself with a gentle admonition, impressing his orders on him con‐

cerning the manner of making the entries.

The troops of Xochimilco, of Cuitláhuac, and of other cities of the

§ 23.

Treachery of the lake  that were in Cortés’s camp, wishing to profit by the occasion Xochimilcas and of that presented itself to them in the entries of the Spaniards to sack

other peoples.

the houses of the Mexicans, adopted the most abominable perfidy.

They sent a secret embassy to King Cuauhtémoc, protesting their in‐

violable fidelity to the crown, and complaining of the Spaniards, who

forced them to take up arms against their natural lord, and adding

that they wished on their first entry to unite with the Mexicans

against those enemies of the country to kill them all, and thus put an

end to so many calamities. The king praised their purpose, assigned

them the posts they were to occupy, and even sent them gifts in re‐

turn, to reward their pretended fidelity. These traitors entered the

city, as they were wont to do, and feigning at first to turn their arms

against the Spaniards, they then began to sack the houses of the

Mexicans, killing those who resisted them and taking the women and

children prisoner; but the Mexicans, perceiving the treachery at once,

hurled themselves against them with such fury that almost all the

guilty paid for their perfidy with their lives. Very many were killed in

the fray, and the others, taken prisoners, were immediately sacrificed

by order of the king. This treachery appears neither to have been

counseled nor carried out,

except [p. 209] except by a part of the rabble of those cities, ill-bred people, ever

Bk. X. ready for such crimes.

It was now twenty days that the Spaniards had been making con‐

§ 24. tinual entries into the city; whence some captains and soldiers, weary Victory of the

Mexicans. of so many fights, the fruit of which they still saw far off, complained to the general and entreated him to venture all the great forces he had on one decisive stroke, which would finally draw them out of so many perils and hardships. Their design was to advance as far as the center of Tlatelolco, where the Mexicans had gathered their forces, in order to ruin them utterly in a single day, or at least to induce them to surrender. Cortés, who knew very well how dangerous such an enterprise was, tried to dissuade them from it with the most effective arguments; but, nothing availing, and being unable besides to resist a counsel that had become almost general, he yielded at last to their importunate entreaties. He ordered the commander Sandoval to go with a hundred and fifteen foot and ten horse to join Alvarado, to place the cavalry in ambush and have the baggage removed, feigning to depart and to abandon the siege of the city, so that the Mexicans, lured into pursuing them, should be attacked from behind by the cavalry; that with the six brigantines he should try to make himself master of that great ditch where Alvarado had been defeated, having it filled and leveled; that he should not take a step forward without leaving the way well prepared for the retreat, and that he should strive to enter the market square by force of arms.

On the appointed day for the general assault Cortés marched with twenty-five horse, with all his infantry, and with more than a hundred thousand allies. His brigantines, with more than three thousand auxiliary boats, formed the wings of his army on either side of the causeway. He entered the city without any opposition, and at once divided his army into three parts, so that by as many streets they might arrive at the same hour at the market square. The command of the first division was given to Julián de Alderete, the king’s treasurer, who

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.       Dd                      had [p. 210] had been the one who with greatest zeal had urged Cortés to underBk. X. take this expedition, and he was ordered to proceed by the main and

widest street with seventy Spanish foot, seven horse, and twenty

thousand allies. Of the other two streets that led from the main

causeway of Tlacopan to the market square, the less narrow was as‐

signed to the captains Andrés de Tapia and Jorge de Alvarado (broth‐

er of Pedro de Alvarado) with eighty Spanish foot and more than ten

thousand allies, and Cortés himself took charge of the other, narrow‐

er and more difficult, with a hundred Spanish foot and the bulk of the

auxiliary troops, leaving at the entrance of each street the cavalry and

the artillery. All entered at one stroke, fighting courageously. The

Mexicans made at first some resistance, but then, feigning cowardice,

they retired, abandoning the ditches to the Spaniards, in order that

the latter, lured by the hope of victory, might advance to greater per‐

ils. Some Spaniards pressed on to the streets nearest the market

square, incautiously leaving behind a wide ditch badly filled, and

when, being more encouraged, they were vying to be the first to enter

that square, they heard the formidable sound of the horn of the god

Painalton, which was sounded by the priests in cases of public and

pressing need to rouse the people to take up arms. There ran up at

once so great a multitude of Mexicans, and fell with such fury upon

the Spaniards and the allies, that it threw them into confusion and

forced them to turn precipitately back as far as the ditch (C), appar‐

ently filled with fascines and other light materials, and, wishing to

pass over it, they sank through the weight and the violence of the

crowd. Here was the greatest conflict and peril

of

(C) Solís places this ditch outside the city, and says that in coming out of it the

Spaniards were assailed by the Mexicans; but this is a manifest error; for it is estab‐

lished by the accounts of Cortés and other historians that the said ditch was between

the main causeway of Tlacopan and the market square, and that to go to their camp

the Spaniards had to cross the greater part of the city. [p. 211] of the fugitives; for, being unable to swim across and defend themselves at the same time, they were wounded and taken by the Bk. X. Mexicans. Cortés, who with the diligence proper to a good general had come to the ditch when the routed troops arrived there, tried to stop them with his shouts, so that they should not by their disorder facilitate the slaughter begun to be made by the enemy; but what voices are capable of restraining the flight of a confused multitude, to which fear lends speed? Pierced by the keenest grief for the loss of his men, and heedless of his own danger, he approached the ditch to rescue those he could. Some came out unarmed, others wounded, and others half drowned. He tried to put them in order and to start them toward the camp, remaining himself behind with twelve or twenty men to guard their rear; but he had scarcely begun to march when he found himself in a narrow pass surrounded by the enemy. That day would have been his last, in spite of the extraordinary courage with which he defended himself, and together with his life all hope of the conquest of Mexico would have been lost, if the Mexicans, instead of giving him death, as they easily could, had not been intent on taking him alive, to honor with so illustrious a victim their gods. They had already taken him, and were leading him without delay to the sacrifice, if his men, warned of his captivity, had not promptly come to free him. Cortés owed his life and his liberty chiefly to a soldier of his guard called Cristóbal de Olea, a man of great courage and singular skill in arms, (*) who on another occasion had delivered him from a like danger, and in this one saved him at the cost of his own life, cutting off with a stroke of his sword the arm of that Mexican who had seized him. Cortés was also indebted for his liberty to the prince Don Carlos Ixtlilxochitl, and to a brave Tlaxcalan called Temacatzin.

Dd2                           At

(*) Bernal Díaz in many places in his history greatly praises the courage of Olea, whose death was exceedingly distressing both to his general and to his companions.

[p. 212]

At length the Spaniards got out, though with great difficulty and Bk. X.

with not a few wounds, onto the wide causeway of Tlacopan, where

Cortés was able to put them in order, taking the rear guard himself

with the cavalry; but the boldness and fury with which the Mexicans

pursued them were such that it seemed to them impossible to escape

with their lives. Those who had entered by the other two streets had

had terrible fights; but because they had been more diligent in stop‐

ping up the ditches, their retreat was less difficult, when Cortés gave

them the order to march to the great square of Tenochtitlan, where

they assembled. From there they saw, to their very great distress, ris‐

ing from the braziers of the great temple the smoke of the copal that

the Mexicans burned in thanksgiving for the victory won; but their

grief increased further when they saw some heads of Spaniards

thrown toward them by the Mexicans to dishearten them, and heard

it said that the commanders Alvarado and Sandoval had been killed.

From the square they took the causeway of Iztapalapa to their camp,

pressed continually by a great multitude of enemies.

Alvarado and Sandoval had striven to enter the market square by a

street that led from that of Tlacopan to Tlatelolco, and had happily

advanced their operations as far as a post not far from that square;

but having seen the sacrifices of some Spaniards, and heard from the

Mexicans that Cortés and his captains had been killed, they retired

with the utmost difficulty; for to those enemies who had been fight‐

ing them before were added those who had defeated Cortés’s troops.

The loss that the besiegers suffered that day was seven horse,

many arms and boats, and a cannon of artillery, more than a thousand

allies, and more than sixty Spaniards, partly killed in the battle and

partly taken prisoner and immediately sacrificed in the great temple

of Tlatelolco in sight of Alvarado’s men. The captain of a brigantine

was also killed. Cortés received a wound

in [p. 213] in a leg, and scarcely was there one among the besiegers who was not wounded or ill-treated. (D)                                             Bk. X.

The Mexicans celebrated the victory for eight continuous days with illuminations and music in the temples; they made the fame fly through the whole kingdom, and had the heads of the slain Spaniards carried through the provinces to frighten those that had rebelled against the crown and to recall them to their obedience, as in fact they obtained it from some. They dug out the ditches anew, repaired the entrenchments, and restored the city, except the temples and the houses ruined by the enemy, to the state in which it had been before the siege began.

Meanwhile the Spaniards stood on the defensive in their camps, §Fights25.

of the brigantending their wounds and refreshing themselves for the fights to tines, and stratagems come; but lest the Mexicans should take advantage of their inactivity of the Mexicans. to introduce provisions into the city, Cortés ordered the brigantines to go cruising two by two about the lake. The Mexicans, recognizing the superiority of the Spanish vessels and arms, and not being able to employ equal arms, tried at least in some way to match the brigantines. They had therefore built thirty large boats, called by the Spaniards piraguas, well equipped and covered with thick planking, so as to be able to fight from them without so much risk of being damaged. They determined to lay with them an ambush for the brigantines among the thickets of reeds that were in the floating clumps in the lake, and they planted in several places thick stakes hidden under the water, so that the brigantines, striking against them, might break, or at least be hampered in their defense. The ambush being arranged, they made three or four ordinary boats come out from the reed beds of the lake, so that, by provoking two brigantines that were cruising there,

they

(D) Cortés counts no more than thirty-five or forty Spaniards killed, nor more than twenty wounded, but, following the example of other generals, he diminishes his losses, as he did in giving the account of the defeat of July 1 of the preceding year; whence it seemed to us better to follow Bernal Díaz on this point, who shows that he took particular care to count the Spaniards who were being lost. [p. 214] they might lead them, in flight, to the place of the ambush. The Spaniards, as

Bk. X.

soon as they saw the boats, ran after them, but just when they were most en‐

gaged in pursuing them, the brigantines struck against the stakes, and at the

same moment the thirty large boats came out and assailed them on every

side. The Spaniards ran great danger of losing their lives together with their

vessels; but while with the fire of their muskets they held the enemy back,

some skillful swimmers found time to pull up the stakes, and so, freed from

that encumbrance, they were able to use their artillery to put the boats to

flight. The brigantines were left badly damaged, and the Spaniards wounded;

and of the two captains who commanded them, one was killed in the fray, and

the other died within three days of his wounds. The Mexicans repaired their

boats to repeat the stratagem; but Cortés, secretly informed of the place

where they lay in ambush, prepared an ambush of his own of six brigantines,

and, profiting by the enemy's example, ordered that one brigantine should ap‐

proach the place where the boats were lying in ambush and, when it discov‐

ered them, should take to flight toward the spot where the Spaniards were

hidden. It all happened as Cortés had planned; for the Mexicans, seeing the

brigantine, promptly came out of ambush, and just when they thought them‐

selves surest of their prey, the other five brigantines came out against them

and at once began to play their artillery, with whose first discharge they over‐

turned some boats and threw the rest into disorder. The greater part of the

Mexicans perished, and some were taken prisoner, among them some nobles,

whom Cortés wished to use in order to press for some accommodation with

the court of Mexico.

§ 26.   He therefore sent word to King Cuauhtémoc, by means of these noble A fruitless embassy prisoners, that he should consider how much the forces of Mexico were di‐

to the king of minishing at the very time in which those of the Spaniards were increasing;

Mexico. that in the end they would have to yield to the greater power; that even if the

Spaniards did not enter that court to commit hostilities, it would suffice

merely to cut off their relief to make them die

of [p. 215] of hunger; that they could still avoid the disasters that threatened them; that if they consented to the proposals of peace, he would at Bk. X. once cause all hostilities to cease, the king would remain in peaceful possession of the crown, with all the greatness, all the power, and all the authority which he had hitherto enjoyed, and his subjects would remain free and masters of all their goods, without his requiring anything more from His Majesty or from his subjects than the homage due to the King of Spain, as supreme lord of that empire, whose rights had already been recognized by the Mexicans themselves, as resting on the ancient tradition of their ancestors; that if on the contrary he persisted in the war, he would be deprived of the crown, the greater part of his vassals would lose their lives, and that fine and great city would be utterly ruined. The king consulted his councilors, the generals of the army, and the heads of religion; he laid before them the subject of the embassy, the state of that court, the scarcity of provisions, the affliction of the people, and the still greater evils that threatened them, and commanded them to say freely what they thought. Some, foreseeing the outcome of the war, inclined toward peace; others, spurred by hatred of the Spaniards or by the incentive of honor, advised war. The priests, whose authority in this matter as in others was held in the highest respect, strongly opposed peace, alleging several pretended oracles of their gods, whose anger was to be feared if they yielded to the demands of those cruel enemies of their worship, and whose protection had to be sought with prayers and sacrifices. This opinion prevailed in the end, through the superstitious fear that had taken possession of their minds, and in accordance with it the Spanish general was answered that he should continue the war, since they had resolved to defend themselves to their last breath. Had they been brought to such a resolution not by superstition but by honor, by love of their country and of their own liberty, they would not have been so blameworthy, because although they foresaw their ruin as inevitable by continuing the war,

## they

[p. 216]

Bk. X.

they had no hope of improving their fortune by peace. The experi‐

ence of past events did not allow them to trust the promises that

were made to them, so that it had to seem to them more consonant

with their ideas of honor to die with arms in hand in defense of their

country and of liberty than to abandon that same country to the am‐

bition of those strangers, and to reduce themselves by their surrender

to a sad and wretched servitude.

§ 27.

Two days after the defeat of the Spaniards there arrived at Cortés's Expeditions against camp some messengers sent from the city of Cuauhnahuac to com‐

the people of Malinalco and against plain of the great harm done them by their neighbors the people of

the Matlatzincas.

Malinalco, who, as these messengers asserted, wished to ally them‐

selves with the Cohuixcas, a rather numerous nation, in order to de‐

stroy Cuauhnahuac, because it had allied itself with the Spaniards,

and then to cross the mountains and come with a great army to at‐

tack Cortés's camp. This general, although he was in a position to ask

for help rather than to give it, nevertheless, for the reputation of the

Spanish arms, and to forestall the blow with which he was threatened,

sent Captain Andrés de Tapia with the messengers, with two hundred

Spanish foot, ten horse, and a good number of allies, giving him or‐

ders to unite with the troops of Cuauhnahuac and to do whatever he

should judge conducive to the service of his king and to the safety of

the Spaniards. Tapia carried out what was enjoined upon him by his

general, and in a small place situated between Cuauhnahuac and

Malinalco he fought a great battle with the enemy, routed them, and

pursued them to the foot of the high mountain on which stood the

city of Malinalco. He could not assault it, as he would have liked, be‐

cause the mountain was inaccessible to cavalry; but he laid waste the

countryside, and since the term of ten days prescribed to him was

nearly complete, he returned to Cortés's camp.

Two days later there arrived at the same camp the messengers of

the Otomis of the Valley of Toluca, asking help against the

Matlatzincas, a powerful and warlike nation of the

same [p. 217] same valley, who were making continual war upon them, had burned one of their towns and had taken many of them prisoner, and who, Bk. X. besides this, had agreed with the Mexicans to attack Cortés's camp with all their forces from the mainland side at the same time as the Mexicans should attack it from the side of the city. On entering Mexico, the Spaniards had sometimes heard the Mexicans threaten them with the power of the Matlatzincas, and Cortés now perceived, from the report of the Otomis, the great danger he would run if he gave the enemy time to carry out their design. He would entrust this important expedition to no one but the brave and invincible Sandoval. This tireless man, although he had been wounded on the day of Cortés's defeat, had in the days that followed acted as general, going ceaselessly around all three camps and giving the most opportune orders for their security. Scarcely fourteen days had passed since the defeat when he marched toward the Valley of Toluca with eighteen horse, a hundred Spanish foot, and sixty thousand allies. On the way they saw some signs of the slaughter made there by the Matlatzincas, and when they entered the valley they found a place freshly ruined, and saw the enemy troops going laden with spoils, which they abandoned as soon as the Spaniards appeared, wishing to be less encumbered for the battle. They crossed a river that traverses the valley, and remained on its bank to await the Spaniards. Sandoval crossed it intrepidly with his army, attacked the enemy, put them to flight, and pursued them for a good nine miles to a city where they took refuge, leaving more than a thousand Matlatzincas dead on the field. Sandoval besieged the city and forced the enemy to abandon it and to take shelter in a fortress built on the top of a steep mountain. The victorious army entered the city, and after sacking it they set fire to the buildings; and because it was late and the troops were too weary, the assault on the fortress was put off until the next day, when, expecting to have

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.   Ee                      a [p. 218] a great struggle, they found the fortress empty. Sandoval resolved to Bk. X.

pass, on his return, through some places that had likewise declared

themselves hostile; but he had no need to use arms against them, for,

frightened at seeing so great an army come against them, swelled with

numerous Otomi troops, they surrendered at once. Sandoval received

them with the utmost kindness, and asked of them that they induce

the Matlatzinca nation to make friendship with the Spaniards, point‐

ing out to them the advantages they could hope to have from them

and, on the contrary, the evils that enmity would bring upon them.

These expeditions were of the greatest importance; for four days af‐

ter Sandoval had returned there arrived at Cortés's camp several lords

of the Matlatzincas, of Malinalco, and of the Cohuixcas (E) to ask

pardon for their hostilities and to establish a confederation, which

was as useful to the Spaniards as it was harmful to the Mexicans.

The Spaniards no longer had enemies to fear on the side of the

mainland, and Cortés had at his disposal so excessive a number of

troops that he could have employed in the siege of Mexico many

more people than Xerxes sent against Greece, had the situation of

that capital not made so great a multitude of besiegers rather an en‐

cumbrance. The Mexicans, on the contrary, found themselves aban‐

doned by their friends and by their subjects, surrounded by enemies,

and afflicted by famine. That unfortunate court had against it, besides

the Spaniards, the kingdom of Acolhuacan, the republics of Tlaxcala,

of Huexotzinco, and of Cholula, almost all the cities of the Valley of

Mexico, and the very numerous nations of the Totonacs, the Mixtecs,

the Otomis, the Tlahuicas, the Cohuixcas, the Matlatzincas, and oth‐

ers; so that

besides

(E) Cortés writes Cuisco instead of Cohuixco. The author of the notes on the let‐

ters of that conqueror believed that he was speaking of Huisuco, because he did not

know that there was a great province called Cohuixco. Huisuco (among the Mexicans

Huitzoco) was, and even today is, an obscure place, and by no means a great prov‐

ince, as that of Cuisco was, according to what Cortés himself affirms. [p. 219] besides the external enemies, more than half of the empire had conspired in its ruin, and the other half at least did not favor it.      Bk. X.

While the commander Sandoval was employing his courage

§ 28. against the Matlatzincas, the Tlaxcalan Chichimecatl also gave proof A memorable deed of of his own against the Mexicans. This famous general, when he saw General

Chichimecatl. that the Spaniards after the defeat were keeping on the defensive, resolved to make an entry into Mexico with his Tlaxcalans alone. He left, then, the camp of Alvarado, where he had always been since the beginning of the siege, accompanying the Spaniards in all the fights and ever making his bravery conspicuous. This time he carried all the ditches that were on the causeway of Tlacopan, and, leaving four hundred archers on guard at the most dangerous one, so that they might secure the passage on the retreat, he entered the city with the main body of his troops, where he had a terrible fight with the Mexicans, in which many were killed and wounded on both sides. The Mexicans flattered themselves that they could defeat him at the passage of the ditch, and pressed him hard in his retreat; but thanks to the archers posted on the other bank of the ditch, he crossed back happily with his Tlaxcalans, and returned full of glory to their camp. (F)

Ee2                           The

(F) Bernal Díaz says that after the defeat the Spaniards were left abandoned by their allies, who, out of the fear that the Mexicans put into them with certain threats which they made them in the name of their gods, all went away to their homes; that in Cortés's camp there remained no one but the prince Don Carlos with forty Texcocans; in Sandoval's, a lord of Huexotzinco with fifty men; and in Alvarado's, General Chichimecatl with eighty Tlaxcalans. But this cannot be, because two days after that defeat Captain Tapia set out from Cortés's camp to make war on the people of Malinalco, and took many allies with him, as the same Bernal Díaz affirms. Twelve days after Tapia, Commander Sandoval set out from the same camp with sixty thousand allies, as Cortés says. At the time when Sandoval was making war on the Matlatzincas, that is, sixteen or eighteen days after the defeat, General Chichimecatl made his famous entry, and he could not have made it without many thousands of Tlaxcalans. It is certain that not all the allies went away, and that if some did go away they returned at once, because within a few days there was in the three camps, and especially in Cortés's, a number of them far greater than there had been before the defeat. Cortés says nothing of such a desertion, and it is not very likely that he would have omitted it in recounting his misfortunes to the king.

[p. 220]

The Mexicans, to avenge the boldness of the Tlaxcalans, assailed Bk. X.

Alvarado's camp one night; but having been heard in time by the sen‐

tinels, the Spaniards and the allies ran to arms. The fight lasted three

hours, during which time Cortés, having heard a cannon shot from

his camp, and suspecting what it in fact was, thought this a fitting oc‐

casion to make an entry into the city with his men, who were by now

healed of their wounds. The Mexicans who had gone to Tlacopan, be‐

ing unable to overcome the resistance of the Spaniards, returned to

the city, where they found Cortés's army; they fought with ardor, but

without any considerable advantage on either side.

At this same time, when the need of arms and munitions was

greatest, a ship put in at Veracruz that brought the Spaniards a fresh

relief, with which they were placed in a position to continue their op‐

erations. Prince Don Carlos Ixtlilxochitl had advised the Spanish gen‐

eral not to engage in new assaults, in which his army would have to

suffer too much; that without exposing himself to so many ills, and

without ruining the beautiful buildings of that capital, he could make

himself master of it merely by preventing the entry of provisions; for

the greater the number of the besieged, the sooner they would con‐

sume the few provisions they had. This wise counsel, all the more

valuable as it was the less to be expected from a prince who by his age

and his intrepidity longed for occasions to make his courage shine,

pleased Cortés so much that, unable to restrain himself, he ran to em‐

brace him, signifying his gratitude to him in singular expressions. He

held to such counsel for some days; but then, weary of the length of

the siege, he returned to the former hostilities; but before continuing

them he offered peace anew to the Mexicans, making them a compar‐

ison of the forces of the one side and of the other, and repeating the

reasons which he had already on another occasion proposed to them.

The Mexicans replied that they would never lay down their arms until

the Spaniards should go away to their own country.

Now

[p. 221]

Now Cortés, seeing the resolution of the Mexicans, after forty-five

Bk. X. days of siege, and that the more he invited them to peace the more obstinately they rejected it, determined not to advance a step farther § 29. into the city without destroying all the buildings that stood on one Slaughter in Mexico,

and the bravery of side and the other of the street, both to avoid the harm which the flat some women. roofs did to his troops, and to constrain the besieged by such hostilities to accept his proposals. He therefore solicited, and obtained from the allied lords, some thousands of peasant laborers provided with the necessary tools for demolishing the buildings and filling the ditches. In the following days he made several entries into the city with his Spaniards and brigantines, and with more than a hundred and fifty thousand allies, destroying buildings, filling ditches, and diminishing by slaughter the number of his enemies, though not without grave danger to his own person and to his men; for he very nearly was taken prisoner, and would have been had his soldiers not come to his aid, and his troops were at times obliged to withdraw by flight from the fury of the Mexicans; some Spaniards and allies perished there; and two brigantines were already almost overwhelmed by a fleet of canoes, but another brigantine that came up drew them out of the danger.

The bravery of some Spanish women (*) became famous in these entries; they had voluntarily accompanied their husbands to the war, and by the continual hardships they suffered, and by the examples of valor which they had ever before their eyes, they had become soldiers. They stood guard, they marched with their husbands armed with cotton corselets, with buckler and sword, and they threw themselves intrepidly into the midst of the enemy, increasing, in spite of their sex, the number of the besiegers.

On July 24 a new entry was made into the city

with

(*) These women were called María de Estrada, of whose courage we have made mention elsewhere, Beatriz Bermúdez de Velasco, Juana Martín, Isabel Rodríguez, and Beatriz de Palacios. [p. 222] with a larger number of troops than on the other occasions, (G) and Bk. X.

fighting vigorously, the Spaniards made themselves masters of that

street by which the broad causeway of Iztapalapa communicated with

that of Tlacopan, which Cortés had so much desired for free commu‐

nication between his camp and Alvarado's. They carried and filled

several ditches, and burned and ruined many buildings, among others

a palace of King Cuauhtémoc, which was a vast and strong edifice

surrounded by ditches. Of the four parts of the city three remained

that day in the power of the Spaniards, the besieged having been re‐

duced to the part of Tlatelolco, which, because there was more water

there, was the stronger and the safer.

From a Mexican lady taken in the last assault the Spanish general

learned the miserable state of the city, owing to the want of provi‐

sions and the discord that existed among the besieged; for the king,

his relatives, and many nobles had determined to die rather than sur‐

render, but the people were disheartened and weary of the siege. This

was confirmed by two deserters of middling rank, who, driven by

hunger, came to Cortés's camp.

On these tidings Cortés resolved to let no day pass without enter‐

ing the city until he should carry it or ruin it; so he returned there on

the 25th with his army, and carried a broad street in which there was

a ditch so great that in the whole day it could not be filled or stopped

up. They demolished or burned all the houses that were in that part,

in spite of the resistance of the enemy. The Mexicans, seeing the al‐

lies employed in tearing down the buildings, said to them: “Ruin

them, you traitors, ruin those houses, for afterwards you will have the

labor of rebuilding them.” “We,” the allies answered, “shall surely re‐

build them, if you are the victors;

but

(G) Cortés says that the allies, when they saw the success of the Spanish arms,

came in such great numbers to serve in the siege that they could not be counted. [p. 223] but if you are vanquished, you yourselves will rebuild them, so that

Bk. X. your enemies may live in them.” Now, unable to repair the buildings, the Mexicans made some small wooden fortifications in the streets, in order to harm the besiegers from them, as they had harmed them before from the flat roofs, and to hinder the cavalry they filled the square with stones; but the besiegers made use of them to fill up the ditches.

In the entry made there on the 26th, two great ditches which the Mexicans had newly made were carried. Alvarado for his part was advancing every day farther into the city; and on the 27th he went so far, carrying several ditches and entrenchments, that he came to occupy two towers near the palace where King Cuauhtémoc resided; but he could not thence pass farther, as he wished, because of the extreme difficulty he found in the ditches and the vigorous resistance of the enemy, who forced him to retire, pressing furiously upon his rear guard. Cortés, having observed an extraordinary smoke that rose from those towers, and suspecting what had in fact happened, entered the city, as he was accustomed to do, and spent the whole day in repairing all the bad passages. He now lacked no more than one canal and one entrenchment to enter the market square. He determined to advance as far as that, as in fact he accomplished, and that was the first time since the siege began that his troops joined with those of Alvarado, with unspeakable joy on both sides. Cortés entered that square with some cavalry, and found there an innumerable people lodged in the porticoes, because they could not be contained in the houses of that ward. He climbed the temple, from which he observed the city, and saw that of its eight parts only one remained to be carried. He had fire set to the high and beautiful towers of that temple, in which, as in the greater one of Tenochtitlan, the idol of the god of war was worshiped. The Mexican commoners, on seeing that great flame, which seemed to rise to heaven, raised a great lament. Cortés, moved to pity at seeing so numerous a people

reduced [p. 224] reduced to such straits, made all hostilities cease that day, and spoke

Bk. X.

again to the besieged, that they might surrender; but they replied

that they would never do so, that in case a single Mexican remained

alive, he would continue the defense till death.

After four days had then passed without fighting, Cortés entered

§ 30. the  city anew, and there he came upon a great throng of wretched The lamentable state

of the Mexicans. men, of women, and of children, weak, emaciated, and almost dying

of hunger, which was so great that many lived only on herbs and

marsh roots, on insects, and even on the bark of trees. Moved to pity,

that general commanded his troops that they should do them no

harm; he passed to the market square, and found the porticoes filled

with disarmed people: a manifest sign of the dejection of the people,

and of the displeasure with which they endured the obstinacy of the

king and of the nobility. The greater part of that day was spent in ne‐

gotiations for peace; but Cortés, seeing that this availed nothing, gave

order to Commander Alvarado to advance by force of arms along a

great street where there were more than a thousand houses, and he

himself with all his army went to assail them from another side. So

great was the slaughter which they made that day among the besieged

that, between dead and prisoners, there were more than twelve thou‐

sand. The allies were so cruel against those unhappy victims that they

spared them neither for age nor for sex, the severe orders of the gen‐

eral not sufficing to restrain their cruelty.

The next day Cortés returned with all his forces; but he command‐

ed that no harm should be done to the besieged, both for the com‐

passion which the sight of their miseries caused him, and for the
