hope he had of inducing them to surrender. The Mexicans, seeing so

great a number of troops coming against them, and among them their

own subjects, who had formerly served them and now threatened

them with death, finding themselves now reduced to such misery and

calamity, and having before their eyes so many lamentable objects, for

they [p. 225] they had nowhere even to set foot except upon the corpses of their fellow citizens, they vented their rage in horrible cries, and demanded Bk. X. death as the only remedy for their ills. Some commoners begged Cortés to confer with certain nobles who were defending an entrenchment, in order to treat of some accommodation. Cortés went to them, although he had little hope of success. These were precisely some of those who could no longer bear the hardships of the siege; so that when they saw the Spanish general approach them, moved by despair they said to him: “If you are the son of the Sun, as some imagine, why, when your Father is so swift that in the brief space of a day he completes his course, do you take so long to free us from so many ills by death? We wish indeed to die, in order to go to heaven, where our god Huitzilopochtli awaits us to give us rest from our labors and the reward of our services.” Cortés proposed to them various reasons to move them to surrender; but as they answered that this was neither in their power nor had they any hope of being able to persuade the king, he withdrew, in order to urge him by means of a distinguished personage whom he had made prisoner three days before, and who was an uncle of the king of Texcoco. That man, although wounded, was charged with betaking himself to Tlatelolco to treat of that business with the king; but no other fruit of his embassy was seen than the reiterated outcries with which the people demanded death. (H) Some Mexican troops hurled themselves desperately against the Spaniards, but they were so weakened by hunger that the harm they did was slight, and too great that which they received from their enemies.

Cortés returned the next day to the city, expecting at every moment that the Mexicans would surrender, and, without permitting any harm to be done them, he addressed himself to

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.                Ff                      certain

(H) It was said, according to what Cortés writes, that as soon as that lord presented himself to Cuauhtémoc to set before him the proposals of peace, he was sacrificed by order of that king; but as this fact has no other foundation than that vague rumor, it is not worthy of our belief.

[p. 226]

Bk. X.

certain principal men who were in an entrenchment, already known

to him since the first time he was at that court, and asked them why

§ 31. they wished to defend themselves so obstinately, when they could no Vain efforts toward the surrender of the longer resist and found themselves in such a state that with a single

Mexicans. blow he could take the lives of all. They replied that they saw very

well that their ruin was inevitable, and would have wished to prevent

it, but they could not, because it was not for them to deliberate.

Nevertheless they offered to beg the king to be willing to hear the

proposals of peace. In fact they went at once to the palace, and after a

little while returned, saying that, because it was late, the king could

not come, and that they did not doubt that the day following he

would be with Cortés in that same square. In the center of it there

was a great square platform of earth, where the Mexicans held their

theatrical representations, as we have said elsewhere. On this stage

Cortés had carpets and low platforms placed for holding the desired

conference, and had a good meal prepared for the king and for the

nobility who were to accompany him. When the day came, he sent

word to the king that he was awaiting him in that place; but the king

sent him five very authoritative personages, excusing himself from

coming in person on account of a certain indisposition that he had,

and because he did not trust the Spaniards. Cortés received them

with the utmost kindness, gave them a stately banquet, and sent

them back to the king, that they might beg him in his name to be

willing to come to that place without fear; since he pledged his word

to respect, as was fitting, his royal person; that his presence was abso‐

lutely necessary, and without it nothing could be concluded; and he

accompanied this embassy with a gift of provisions, which was then

the most valuable thing. The ambassadors, after having made known

at the meal the great need they suffered, went away, and two hours

later returned, bringing Cortés a gift of very fine garments which the

king sent him, and repeating the aforesaid excuses. Three days passed

in these negotiations without any fruit.

Cortés

[p. 227]

Cortés had given orders to the allies to stay outside the city, be‐

Bk. X. cause the Mexicans had begged him not to permit them to take part when the conference with the king should be held; but now, having § 32. lost all hope of an accommodation, he recalled all the troops of his A      terrible conflict,

and horrible slaughcamp, in which there were more than a hundred and fifty thousand ter of the Mexicans. men, and those also of Alvarado's camp, and with all these forces together he set himself to attack certain ditches and entrenchments which were the greatest fortifications that remained to the Mexicans, and at the same hour Sandoval with his army assailed the city from the north side. This was the most disastrous day for that city, on which Mexican blood was shed more copiously than ever, those unhappy citizens having by now neither arms to repel the multitude and the fury of their enemies, nor strength to defend themselves, nor even ground to fight upon. The soil of the city was covered with corpses, and the water of the ditches and of the canals was tinged with blood. Nothing was seen but ruin and slaughter, nor was anything heard but lamentable weeping and cries of despair. The allies raged so cruelly against that wretched people that the Spaniards labored more to restrain their cruelty than to fight with their enemies. The slaughter that was made of the Mexicans on that day was so great that between dead and prisoners, by what Cortés himself says, it exceeded forty thousand persons.

The intolerable stench of so many unburied corpses then forced § 33. the besiegers to withdraw from the city; but the next day (August 13) Last assault, and capthey returned to it to give the last assault to that corner of Tlatelolco ture of the city and

of the kings. which still remained in the power of the Mexicans. Cortés brought with him three cannon and all his troops. He assigned to each of the captains the place from which he was to make the assault, and commanded them to do all that was possible to force the besieged to throw themselves into the water toward that place where Sandoval was waiting with all the brigantines, which was a kind of harbor surrounded on all sides by houses, where there landed the

Ff2                merchant [p. 228] merchant boats of the traders who came to the market of Tlatelolco; Bk. X.

and above all that they should endeavor to take King Cuauhtémoc,

since that would suffice to make them masters of the city and to put

an end to the war; but before coming to this decisive stroke, he made

new attempts to bring the besieged to some accommodation. He was

induced to this not only by compassion for so many wretched people,

but even by the desire to make himself master of the treasures of the

king and of the nobility; for if that last part of the city were taken by

assault, the Mexicans, deprived of every hope of saving their riches,

might throw them into the lake, in order that the victors should not

enjoy them, and in case they did not do so, they would be seized by

the allies, who, being innumerable and more familiar with the houses,

would leave little or nothing to the Spaniards in the disorder and con‐

fusion of the assault. He therefore spoke again, from an eminent

place, to certain Mexicans of rank who were well known to him, rep‐

resenting to them their extreme danger, and begging them to make

new entreaties to the king, that he should yield to that conference so

much desired by him for the good of the king himself and of all his

subjects; for if he still persisted in his resolve to defend himself, he

was determined not to leave a single Mexican alive that day. Two of

those nobles undertook to persuade the king, but they had scarcely

departed when they returned, accompanying the Cihuacoatl, or

supreme magistrate of the court. He was received by Cortés with sin‐

gular demonstrations of honor and cordiality, but he, with a lordly air,

by which he seemed to wish to show his spirit superior to all calami‐

ties, spoke thus to Cortés: “Spare yourself, O General, the trouble of

soliciting a conference with my king and lord Cuauhtémoc. He is re‐

solved to die rather than appear before you. I could not tell you how

much such a resolution grieves me; but there is no remedy. Take,

then, whatever counsel pleases you best, and act according to your

designs.” Cortés told him to go and prepare

the [p. 229] the minds of his citizens for the death that they would soon have to

Bk. X. suffer.

In the meantime numerous troops of women, of children, and of wretched people had come to give themselves up to the Spaniards, hastening in rivalry to escape from that extreme peril, and some of those who strove to cross the ditches by swimming were drowned, through their great weakness. Cortés commanded that no harm should be done to those who surrendered; indeed he stationed at several posts some Spaniards, in order that by their authority they should restrain the barbarous cruelty of the allies; but in spite of his orders more than fifteen thousand men, women, and children perished at the hands of those inhuman and furious troops.

The nobles and the military men who had held obstinately to the resolution of defending themselves to the last breath occupied the flat roofs of the houses and some paved streets. Cortés, seeing that it was late and that they would not surrender, had the artillery play upon them, and this not sufficing, he gave the signal for the assault with an arquebus shot. The besiegers all assailed at one hour, and pressed the weak and afflicted citizens so hard that, there being left to them in the city no place where they could defend themselves from the fury of so great a number of enemies, many threw themselves into the water, and others came to give themselves up to the victors. The Mexicans had made ready some boats in order to escape by flight from the power of the Spaniards; but Cortés, having perceived such a subterfuge, had given orders to Sandoval to take possession with the brigantines of the harbor of Tlatelolco and to seize the boats. In spite of Sandoval's extreme diligence many boats escaped, and among others precisely the one that carried the royal persons. That active commander, having noticed it, ordered García Holguín, captain of the swiftest brigantine, to give chase to it; he made such haste that in a short time he overtook them, and as the Spaniards made ready to fire upon the fugitives, these stopped their oars and threw down their arms as a sign of surrender. There were on that great boat, or piragua, the king of

Mexico

[p. 230]

Mexico, Cuauhtémoc, the queen Tecuichpotzin his wife, the king of Bk. X.

Acolhuacan Coanacochtzin, and the king of Tlacopan

Tetlepanquetzaltzin, with other personages. The brigantine came

alongside, and the king of Mexico, advancing toward the Spaniards,

said to that captain: “I am, O Captain, your prisoner, nor do I ask any

other favor of you than that you show to the queen my wife and to

her maidens that regard which is due to their sex and to their rank”;

and taking the queen by the hand, he passed with her into the brigan‐

tine. Then, observing that the Spanish captain was anxious about the

other boats, he told him not to doubt that all, as soon as they should

know that their sovereign was a prisoner, would come to die with

him.

Captain Holguín conducted those illustrious prisoners to Cortés,

who was then on the flat roof of a house in Tlatelolco. He received

them with every demonstration of honor and humanity, and had

them sit down. Cuauhtémoc, with great spirit, said to him: “I have

done, O valiant General, both for my own defense and for that of my

subjects all that the honor of my crown and zeal for my people re‐

quired of me; but because the gods were against me, I now see myself

deprived of the crown and of liberty. I am your prisoner; dispose of

my person at your pleasure”; and placing his right hand upon a dagger

which Cortés wore at his belt, he added: “Take from me with this

dagger a life that I did not lose in the defense of my kingdom.”

Cortés strove to console him with kind reasons, protesting to him

that he did not consider him his prisoner, but the prisoner of the

greatest monarch of Europe, in whose clemency he ought to trust

that not only the liberty which he had unhappily lost, but also the

throne of his most illustrious ancestors, which he had so worthily oc‐

cupied and defended, would be restored to him. But what comfort

could he have from such protestations, or what faith could he give to

the words of Cortés, he who had always been his enemy, having seen

that to King Moctezuma it was not enough to be his

friend [p. 231] friend and protector to avoid losing his crown, his liberty, and his life? He demanded of Cortés that no harm be done to his subjects, and Cortés in turn de‐ Bk. X. manded of him that he command all his subjects to surrender. Each gave his orders, and both were promptly obeyed. It was likewise ordered that all the Mexicans should leave the city without arms and without baggage, and, according to what a most truthful eyewitness affirms, (*) for three days and three nights all three causeways were seen full of men, women, and children, weak, emaciated, and filthy, who were leaving by them to take refuge in other places. The stench that so many thousands of putrefied and unburied bodies gave off was so intolerable that it caused some indisposition to the general of the Conquerors. The houses, the streets, and the canals were full of disfigured corpses; (I) the ground of the city was found dug up in some parts by the citizens, who sought roots under the earth to feed upon, and many trees were seen stripped of their bark to meet the extremity of hunger. The general had the corpses buried, and an immense quantity of wood burned throughout the city, both to purify that infected air and to celebrate his victory.

The news of the capture of the capital having spread at once throughout the land, the provinces of the empire gave obedience to Cortés, although there were some which even two years afterward troubled the Spaniards with war. The

## allies

(*) Bernal Díaz del Castillo.

(I) “It is true, and I swear it, amen, that the whole lagoon, and the houses, and the barbacoas were full of bodies and heads of dead men, so that I do not know in what manner to write it; for in the streets, and in the very courts of Tlatelolco, there was nothing else, and we could not walk except among bodies and heads of dead Indians. I have read of the destruction of Jerusalem; but whether in that there was such mortality as in this, I do not know,” etc. Bernal Díaz in ch. 156 of his history. Such expressions from an eyewitness so truthful, and one who did not know how to exaggerate, give some idea of that horrible slaughter. I suspect that the corpses were left unburied by the Mexicans on purpose, to drive off the besiegers with the stench; nor can I persuade myself otherwise, considering the extreme solicitude of those nations in the obsequies of their dead. [p. 232] allies returned to their countries exceedingly joyful over the booty, Bk. X.

and content to have overthrown that court whose domination they

could not endure, or whose arms kept them in perpetual disquiet,

without perceiving that with their own arms they were forging the

chains that were to strangle their liberty, and that, that empire once

ruined, all the other nations were bound to be abased.

The booty was much less than the victors had hoped. The clothing

that was found there was divided for the most part among the allies.

Those works of gold, silver, and feathers that were kept entire on ac‐

count of their singular artistry were sent as a gift to the Emperor

Charles V. All the rest of the gold, which was melted down, scarcely

amounted to 19,200 ounces, (K) both because the Mexicans threw

the greater part of it into the lake, (L) and because individuals,

Spaniards as well as their allies, in sacking the city sought to recom‐

pense themselves secretly for their labors.

The capture of that great court took place on August 13, 1521, a

hundred and ninety-six years after it was founded by the Aztecs, and

a hundred and sixty-nine years after it was raised into a monarchy,

which eleven kings ruled. The siege of Mexico, comparable in its mis‐

fortunes and in its slaughter with that of Jerusalem, lasted seventy-

five days, in which time several thousand of the more than two hun‐

dred thousand allies perished, and more than a hundred of the nine

hundred Spaniards. The number of the Mexicans who died is not

known; but in view of the account of Cortés and of Bernal

Díaz,

(K) Cortés says that the gold which was melted down weighed 130,000

Castellanos, which make 19,200 ounces. Bernal Díaz says that that gold amounted to

380,000 pesos, which seems to be more. Among the spoils that were sent to Charles

V there were pearls of enormous size, most precious gems, and marvelous works of

gold. The ship on which they were sent was captured by Jean Florin, a famous French

corsair, and the treasure was sent to the court of France, which authorized such rob‐

beries under the pretext, no less celebrated than frivolous, that the Most Christian

King is a son of Adam just as the Catholic King is.

(L) Bernal Díaz says that he saw some things of gold taken out of the lake, and

among others a sun like that which King Moctezuma sent to Cortés when the latter

was on the shore of Chalchiuhcuecan.

[p. 233]

Díaz, and what other historians say of it, it seems that those killed

Bk. X. were more than a hundred thousand. As for those who died of hunger, or of sickness caused by the brackish water which they drank, or by the infection of the air, Cortés himself affirms that they were more than fifty thousand. The city remained almost wholly ruined. The king of Mexico, notwithstanding the magnificent promises of the Spanish general, was after a few days ignominiously put to the torture (which he endured with unconquered constancy) in order that he should declare where the immense riches of the court and of the temples were; (M) and three years later he was hanged, on certain suspicions, together with the kings of Texcoco and of Tlacopan. (N) The Mexicans, with all

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III.                 Gg                           the

(M) The torture that was given to King Cuauhtémoc was that of burning his feet little by little after anointing them with oil. A close private friend of his accompanied him, and died in the torments. Bernal Díaz adds that the king of Tlacopan was also tormented along with him. Cortés, against his will, took up so unworthy and barbarous a resolution in order to yield to the urgings of some greedy Spaniards, who suspected that he did not wish to put the king to the torture in order to profit secretly by the whole royal treasure.

(N) Cuauhtémoc, king of Mexico, Coanacochtzin, king of Acolhuacan, and Tetlepanquetzaltzin, king of Tlacopan, were hanged from a tree by sentence of Cortés in Izancanac, capital city of the province of Acallan, on one of the three days preceding Lent of the year 1525. The cause of their death was a certain conversation which they had among themselves about their misfortunes, in which they suggested how easy it would be for them, if they wished, to kill Cortés and all the Spaniards, and to recover their liberty and their crowns. A Mexican traitor, to win the favor of the Spanish general, gave him an account of everything, altering the sense of the words, and representing as an organized conspiracy what had been merely talk in the air. Cortés, who was then journeying toward the province of Comayagua with a few Spaniards weakened by fatigue, and with more than three thousand Mexicans whom he was leading with him, became convinced that there was no other remedy for avoiding the danger by which he believed himself threatened, except that of putting the three kings to death. This execution, says Bernal Díaz, was most unjust, and blamed by all of us who traveled with him that day. It caused Cortés great melancholy and some sleepless nights. The same author adds that Father Juan de Varillas, a friar of the Order of Our Lady of Mercy, confessed and comforted them at the execution; that they were good Christians, and that they died well disposed; whence it is manifest that they had been baptized; but among so many historians of Mexico there is not one who makes mention of an event so notable and so glorious as that of the baptism of these three kings, while otherwise filling so many pages and so many [p. 234] the nations that contributed to their ruin remained, in spite of the Bk. X. Christian and most humane dispositions of the Catholic Kings, aban‐

doned to misery, to oppression, and to the contempt not only of the

Spaniards, but even of the lowest African slaves and of their infamous

descendants, God punishing in the miserable posterity of those na‐

tions the injustice, the cruelty, and the superstition of their ancestors:

a dreadful example of divine justice, and of the instability of the king‐

doms of the earth.

End of the Third Volume.

## DESCENDANTS

leaves of trifles; and what is worse, Torquemada, who labored twenty years on the

history of Mexico, and swelled his three stupendous volumes with accounts of the

discovery of the Solomon Islands, of the revolutions of the Philippines, of the perse‐

cution in Japan, and of a thousand other irrelevant things, makes no mention at all of

the conversion of those kings.

[p. 235]

## VII

Don Diego Pignatelli d'Aragon, etc., 8th Duke of Monteleone, 8th Duke of Terranova, 10th Marquis del Valle, Grand Admiral and High Constable of the kingdom of Sicily, Grandee of Spain, Prince of the Holy Roman Empire, Knight of the Golden Fleece, etc., married to Donna Margherita Pignatelli, of the Dukes of Bellosguardo. Their son was ═══

## VIII

Don Fabrizio Pignatelli d'Aragon, etc., 9th Duke of Monteleone, 9th Duke of Terranova, 11th Marquis del Valle, Grandee of Spain, Prince of the Holy Roman Empire, etc., married to Donna Costanza Medici, of the Princes of Ottaviano. Their son was ═══

IX.

Don Ettore Pignatelli d'Aragon, etc., 10th Duke of Monteleone, 10th Duke of Terranova, 12th Marquis del Valle de Oaxaca, Grandee of Spain, Prince of the Holy Roman Empire. He lives at present in Naples, married to Donna N. Piccolomini, of the Dukes of Amalfi.

From that most noble couple, whom we have placed under number VI, were born four sons, Diego, Ferdinando, Antonio, and Fabrizio, and as many daughters, Rosa, Maria Teresa, Stefania, and Caterina. 1. Don Diego was the heir of the marquisate of the Valle, or of the Vaglio, as they say in Naples, and of the duchies of Monteleone and of Terranova. 2. Don Ferdinando married Donna Lucrezia Pignatelli, Princess of Strongoli, whose son Don Salvatore took Donna Giulia Mastrilli, of the Dukes of Marigliano. 3. Don Antonio married in Spain the only daughter of the Count of Fuentes. From that marriage was born Don Gioacchino Pignatelli d'Aragon, Moncayo, etc., Count of Fuentes, Marquis of Coscojuela, Grandee of Spain, Prince of the Holy Roman Empire, Knight of the Golden Fleece and of Santiago, etc., Ambassador of the King of Spain at the courts of England and of France, and President of the Royal Council of the Military Orders, whose son Don Luigi, now living, has married the only daughter and heiress of Casimiro Pignatelli, Count of Egmont, Duke of Bisaccia, etc., Knight of the Fleece, and Lieutenant General of the Armies of the Most Christian King. 4. Don Fabrizio took to wife Virginia Pignatelli, sister of the Princess of Strongoli, whose son Don Michele is Marquis of Salice and Guagnano. 5. Rosa was given in marriage to the Prince of Scalea. 6. Maria Teresa to the Marquis of Westerloo, a Bohemian lord. 7. Stefania to the Prince of Bisignano. 8. Caterina to the Count of Acerra.

M I S TA K E S OV E R L O O K E D I N T H E F I R S T T WO

VO L U M E S .

In vol. I, p. 126, where it is said, speaking of the migration of the Toltecs, that it began in the year I Tecpatl, that is, in 596 of the common era, it should read thus: in the year I Tecpatl, that is, in 544 of the common era, as is found in my Spanish original and in my dissertations; the other was a mistake in the translation. On the same page, where it is said that the monarchy of the Toltecs began in the year VIII Acatl, that is, in 667 of the common era, it should say in the year VII Acatl, etc., since the year 667 was not VIII, but VII Acatl. In vol. 2, p. 59, where it is said that every four years the Mexican year was advanced by one day on account of the intercalary day of our leap year; so that in the last years of the Mexican century it began on February 14, etc., it should say, it began on February 13.

On p. 16 of the same vol. 2, where it is said that three festivals were held for the goddess Centeotl in the third, eighth, and eleventh months, it should say, in the fourth, eighth, and eleventh months, as is seen in the account of the festivals, p. 68, and in the Calendar, p. 236.

In the aforesaid Calendar, p. 239, where one reads Festival of Maculitochtli, it should say, Festival of Macuiltochtli, and at the end of the Calendar, where it says, The following year II Acatl begins with II Mizquili, it should say, with II Miquiztli.

T H E P R I N T E R’ S N O T I C E .

The volume of the dissertations will probably not be published until the month of August of this year (1781), but every possible effort will be made so that it may come to light sooner.
